Ruleborn: game design document v3.4

Ruleborn

Game design document, version 3.4

Collect the laws of nature, build a universe that runs on them, and pull off heists in universes that run on someone else's.

This document defines the whole game from moment zero: its scope, every system, and the logic that connects them. Names and numbers are starting values, written to be tuned.

Draft for review, September 2026. Native iPhone game built in Godot 4.5, rated 13+, for players aged 16 to 34 at its core; iPhone and Android together at global launch.

[SVG]

32What the research settled

What changed in version 3.4

The register re-read version 3.3 against itself and against the simulation built from it and left seventeen items open. Version 3.4 closes them (31.9). Two were design variables, settled by briefs 37 and 38 under rules fixed before they ran (32.8).

Cracking calls only the guards near the Vault. Every five seconds of cracking calls the guards within the crack’s reach, drawn as a ring around the Vault, rather than every guard in the Heart; the harness sizes the reach (12.6). A ward ignores the crew member disarming it (10.3).

A scout shows no patrols. The patrol shift is fixed when the target is chosen, so what the crew sees of a patrol is exactly what it does (12.12).

Pacing is priced at four heists a day, the engaged player’s: an upgrade costing 25 clean raids takes 46 to 64 days (9.6). The Aether Spring stops at level 5, because by 9.5’s own figures the Aether criterion fails at a one-half share (10.2). The rule sheet is version 2: twenty-two parameters, each naming its subject (7.3).

One number still waits on a model run: the full economy re-run that regenerates 14.2, 14.3 and 10.2’s day columns (32.9). Every sentence that quoted the earlier run now quotes the measured points.

What changed in version 3.3

The audit register left sixty-two items open against version 3.2. Version 3.3 closes all sixty-two, and says item by item which were answered and which were applied, because those are different words (31.9). Nine more research briefs, 28 to 36, each with its decision rule fixed before it ran, answered what the first twenty-seven had left (32.8). The document now keeps no open list: every question either ships a value or states the measurement that would change it and what that measurement then does (31.10).

The four decided mechanisms are applied, not recorded (32.3). Rewind’s slot now holds Tidemark, a checkpoint return replayed from the seed and input log the server already keeps, so the law count stays 48 (7.4). Guards and response are one defence layer, so a defence has four layers rather than five (13). The ambush is a guaranteed knockout keyed to the target’s own health and reached by a quiet approach, which makes moss a defensive decision (12.8). And each ability is two uses a heist with a ten-second lockout: brief 32 replaced the 24–60 second cooldown band that version 3.2 recorded, because only uses give the same once-or-twice count on a short heist and a long one (11.11).

The launch cut is taken. Twenty-four laws chosen by engine cost ship at launch (26.1), and 54 creature forms — eighteen kinds, each in the same three notes, Hush, Spark and Echo (11.10). Forms are added through seasons as content. The launch languages are English and Brazilian Portuguese (20.6).

Rarity is a strength ladder. In each school the deep law with the lower stability cost became rare: 18, 12, 12 and 6 laws by rank, and 1,200 pages for the full Codex (7.9, 14.4).

A guard is specified end to end (12.6, 12.17). Peripheral vision is graded by distance at half the zone’s rate; cracking the Vault is heard across the Heart every five seconds; crouching halves a role’s speed and running doubles it; and each of the six alarm levels adds one consequence of its own.

The economy has a criterion and sinks (9.6, 5.6). An average raid must pay at least what its crew would have produced, and the raid base rose from 100 to 2,400 because 100 failed that test at every Vault level. An attempt is worth 12% to 17% of a clean haul, so an upgrade costing 25 clean raids takes 46 to 64 days at four raids a day, the engaged player’s (9.6, 14.2). Aether and Essence lose a proportional share of what flows in, never of what is held; storage holds one day of production; and births are tuned to fill the roster on day 378.

A new player’s defence is fair by measurement (13.2). The ward cap is 8, a realm the cap cannot bring into its band gets a band of its own by a stated rule, and a generated fallback target is served when fewer than three live ones sit in band (12.22).

Numbers only a run can set ship now anyway (32.9). Hold rates, the payout band, sink shares, the birth rate, the Carrier’s share, the assist bonus and the regenerated tables each carry a value today, with the rule and the command that replace it.

Where the evidence is thin, the design still ships (32.5). The twenty-nine gaps version 3.2 wrote down each now state what ships and what would change it, and the open lists in 30.8 and 30.11 are triggers with actions (30.8).

What version 3.2 changed — kept so the document carries its own history rather than only its latest state

An updated audit of version 3.1 left fifty-two open items. Version 3.2 answers all fifty-two and applies most of them; four mechanism changes are decided but not yet propagated into the sections that own them, and 32.3 says which and why. The answers come from twenty-seven research briefs, each carrying a decision rule fixed before the research ran — so no finding came back as a choice to be made, only as which pre-committed branch fired (32). From the second brief onward only peer-reviewed and first-party sources could carry a conclusion.

Four load-bearing numbers were wrong, and two of them were wrong in the same way. The payout curve was built on 46% of a clean haul, which is the expected grade alone — a fill term was added beside grade in the same formula and the number was never re-derived. Measured, it is 13% to 19%, so an upgrade takes 32 to 46 days rather than nine (9.6). The loading break-even was stated at 70% full; the curve peaks at 90%, and 70% is close to the threshold the one peer-reviewed study of this exact decision names as the rule first-time players intuitively choose (12.19).

Three mechanisms are decided against — and only one is applied. The clock is not the heist's tension; the realm alarm is, and that is applied (12.6). The other two are not. Rewind should go, because no shipped engine restores 300 ticks and GGPO caps rollback at eight frames, but it is still a live law and cutting it moves the law count off 48. Guards and response should be one defence layer rather than two, because the Shapley value's symmetry axiom forces an equal split for components that do nothing apart, but 13 still lists five. Both are recorded as outstanding rather than described as done (32.3), and a check now fails if that slips.

Ability cooldowns must roughly double, to 24–60 seconds — decided, not yet applied. At the present 10 to 20 seconds a crew gets 25 to 83 ability windows against a whole-run decision budget of 15 to 25, so abilities alone swamp everything else the heist is about. It cannot be applied as a number: the eighteen abilities have to be designed once against the new band, and all eighteen still describe different effects or cooldowns in the roster table and the character bible (32.3).

One cognitive bound caps three systems at once (32.4). Working memory holds about four things. That single limit sets the ward count a realm can present, the variables any one heist decision may require, and the abilities a player can track — so those three compete for one budget rather than three.

A check reported green on a document containing what it banned. The retired three-rank page costs were banned by a phrase; a third section went on stating the same model in different words, in normative text, invisible to the check. Bans are now derived from the data: any page price stated beside a rank is recomputed against the canonical table, so every phrasing is caught, including ones nobody anticipated (31.5).

Twenty-nine questions nobody has answered are now written down (32.5). The share of players who build rather than raid does not exist in any published source. Neither does a raiding-versus-production income split, a measured iteration-to-build multiple, or any measurement of AI-agent productivity on new code. A gap that is written down is a research question; a gap that is not is a silent assumption.

The mutation suite proves less than it looked like proving (32.6). Mutation operators alter text that exists, so they cannot represent a fault of omission by construction — a missing fact, a rule never written. That is the class of fault the audits keep finding.

One citation in this document was checked and half-held. The catch-up discount was justified by evidence that disclosed assistance does not harm the experience. The study is real and peer-reviewed — and it was a single session in a competitive shooter, which does not reach a disclosed discount on economic progression over years. The citation stays, qualified (32.1).

The editing agent may now declare an edit impossible rather than manufacture a pass. On tasks where any pass implies a spec violation, frontier agents exploit the tests 76% of the time; giving them an explicit abort cut one benchmark from 54% to 9%, and hiding the test surface drops exploitation to near zero (32.7).

What version 3.1 changed — kept so the document carries its own history rather than only its latest state

An external audit of version 3.0 found 100 items. Version 3.1 closes the eight that were about the document rather than the game, and — more usefully — makes that whole class of failure impossible to repeat: every fact now has one home, and the build fails if a second copy disagrees with it (31).

The document checks itself (31.5). Fifteen deterministic checks run over the built file: canonical constants, retired names, content counts recounted from their own tables, arithmetic re-derived, superseded models banned by phrasing, derived attributes, numbering, citations both ways, cross-references, generated-block digests, and open audit items. A second tool breaks the document ten different ways and asserts every break is caught, because a suite that has never failed is not evidence (31.7).

Six contradictions found and closed. The simulation ran at two different tick rates; the trait and building counts disagreed with the tables that list them; the formulas appendix still encoded a detection model deleted two revisions ago and the page costs of a rarity scheme that no longer exists; the game was listed as its own fallback name; and three laws read attributes no table carried (31.9).

The Library is real. The one page source that needs no raiding had three of its ten levels specified. All ten are now given, with the curve they sit on, and it is in the page economy — which changes what a non-raider's progression actually looks like (10.4, 14.4).

The agent that writes is not the agent that checks (31.6). A verification pass that had not seen this revision being written found five further defects in it, including two inside the section claiming the fixes had landed. Frontier models reward-hack on a third of runs on some benchmarks, and telling them not to makes it worse, not better — the strongest anti-hacking prompt tested raised the rate from 80% to 95%. So every check is deterministic and none of them asks for a judgement.

Reconciliation is a gate, not a milestone (31.8). The blocking half of the register is cleared, the shared numbers move into data files, the checks are turned on and deliberately broken — and only then does the specification lock and the first line of simulation code get written.

Carried over from version 3.0

Validation without players (19.27, 25.4). The paper heist and the twenty-person build test are gone. A simulation harness answers what is structural — decision density, whether skill separates outcomes, hold rates, pacing — the developer is the first player, and everything only a person can answer waits for soft launch. The loss is named rather than hidden: simulation can show the game is not broken, which is a smaller claim than showing it is fun.

Nobody has to build (13.3). The riskiest assumption in the design is removed instead of measured: every player has a generated defence from the first minute, curation is the default creative act, and authoring is the deep end for those who want it.

Six systems specified twice now have one answer — detection, payout, rarity, defence budgets, the roster and production. The superseded halves are deleted, not left standing (30.18).

The inflation dial was keyed to a level that does not exist. It is the target's Vault level, not realm level, which stops at five (9.6).

The Vault decision was two seconds long. Every role now loads, at a fifth of the old rate, and what you carry is what you bank (12.19).

Movement, fights, the Library and matchmaking are specified (12.18, 12.20, 10.4, 12.22), including the rule that the crew never makes a decision the player did not make.

The trade-off the design rests on now binds: a crew on a heist does not work, so raiding genuinely costs production (5.8).

1Vision, pillars and audience

Ruleborn is a strategy game about floating islands that each follow a magic rule. On one island stones float, on another rivers crawl, on a third anyone knocked down comes back as an echo. You collect these rules, build your islands around them, and send a small crew of odd little creatures to rob other players' islands by outsmarting theirs.

1.1 The pitch

| Where it's used
| Line

| Store and trailer
| Your islands. Your rules. Their treasure.

| One sentence for a friend
| "I build floating islands with their own magic rules, and I rob other people's islands by outsmarting theirs."

| For the team
| Pikmin-style crews pulling heists through Monument Valley-style islands that players design and share, with Balatro-style combinations of rules and a Pokémon-style collection of creatures shaped by those rules (see 2).

1.2 The seven pillars

Every feature has to serve at least one of these. When two ideas compete, the one that serves more pillars wins.

| Pillar
| What it means in practice

| Every rule changes the game
| A new law changes how the game plays, not just its numbers, and you can see it in how everything moves. Adding one law creates hundreds of new situations.

| Clever beats strong
| Heists are won by reading an island's rules and finding a way through, not by bigger stats.

| Your islands are yours, and anyone can play them
| Which islands you open, how you shape them, which rules you write into them and which creatures you raise make every player's sky different, and the Island Workshop lets anyone publish an island for everyone to play (18.6).

| Better with friends
| Friends raid together, gift to each other, trade creatures and see each other's best moments. The strongest predictor of staying with a game is having people to play with (19.9).

| Nothing is wasted
| Characters are never spent, failure returns them tired rather than lost, losses are capped, and defending well pays.

| Short sessions, deep planning
| A session is 5 to 9 minutes. The depth lives in planning, which can happen away from the screen.

| Worth your time
| Players come back because they want to, not because they have to (see 19).

1.3 Audience

The core audience is players aged 16 to 34, with 25 to 34 at its heart: people who enjoy clever puzzles, strategy and collecting, and who are tired of pay-to-win and endless timers. Older players are welcome. The game is rated 13+, is not made for or marketed to children, and gives players aged 13 to 17 extra protections (see 20).

1.4 How we talk about it

The fiction is a storybook, not a science lesson. Words players see are plain and warm.

| We say
| We don't say

| Island, rule, law, page, crew, vault, heist, skiff, Keeper
| Physics, code, algorithm, simulation, dimension, hacking

| "This island's rule: stones float"
| "Gravity coefficient reduced by 50%"

| "Your crew got away with 3,200 Starlight"
| "Extraction successful; yield 3,200"

1.5 The ambition

The goal is a game that could become one of the most successful ever made. No honest analysis can promise or estimate that before launch: in 2025 only nine mobile games passed $1 billion in player spending, and strong launches still fail (2.7). So the ambition becomes three commitments:

Design toward what the record holders share (2.6), without copying their weaknesses.

Set gates at the level of the top 1% of games at every stage (25.1). Only a game that clears them has earned a mega-hit forecast.

Change course when the evidence says no, rather than scaling a game that isn't working.

2Lessons from the most successful games

Version 0.1 leaned on Clash of Clans and Clash Royale. This section widens the lens to the biggest earners, the most downloaded, and the most loved games of 2024 to 2026, and turns each into something Ruleborn takes or avoids.

2.1 What the charts say

In 2025 the top-grossing mobile games were live-service titles driven by events and collaborations: MONOPOLY GO!, Royal Match, Whiteout Survival, Last War and Honor of Kings traded the top spots month by month [26].

The most downloaded were the lowest-friction: Block Blast! held the top spot for much of 2025, alongside Roblox, Free Fire and Subway Surfers [26].

The biggest new hit was a collection game: Pokémon TCG Pocket made an estimated $1.3 billion in its first year and passed 150 million downloads, built on two free packs a day, showcases and trading [27].

Premium and indie games proved depth sells on phones too: Balatro passed 5 million copies [28], and Stardew Valley passed 41 million [29].

And a warning: Supercell ended development of Squad Busters in October 2025. It was the studio's first new global game since Brawl Stars, and after several reworks it became the first global game Supercell has ever shut down [30].

2.2 Sixteen games, and what we do with each

| Game
| Why it works
| What Ruleborn takes
| What Ruleborn avoids

| MONOPOLY GO!
| A constant rhythm of short events; sticker albums completed with friends
| Codex albums per school that friends help fill with page gifts; a steady but calm event calendar
| Dice energy, paid randomness, event pressure

| Royal Match
| Tiny levels with one clear goal; a charming meta of decorating
| Every heist states one goal; decorating islands with cosmetics
| Paid continues and boosters

| Whiteout Survival, Last War
| Alliance goals and shared events keep groups together
| Constellation anomalies and shared goals
| Pay-to-win, long timers, a few big spenders deciding everything

| Honor of Kings
| Revenue from skins and collaborations, not power
| Cosmetic-only earnings; skins for islands, skiffs and crews
| Toxic high-stakes competition

| Pokémon TCG Pocket
| A small daily ritual, the joy of collecting, showcasing and trading
| A showcase of your islands and Codex; page trading inside constellations
| Packs bought with money

| Pokémon GO
| Big shared events and co-op raids
| Anomalies as community events
| Location play; paid event tickets

| Block Blast!
| Fun within seconds of opening, no learning curve
| A first heist inside the first minute
| Heavy advertising

| Roblox, Minecraft
| Players make things other players play
| Defence challenges: the best player-built islands become everyone's ruins (18.5)
| Open user content that teens could misuse

| Balatro
| A few simple rules combine into endless builds
| Laws that combine through notes, harmonies and chords (8)
| A gambling look

| Stardew Valley
| A cosy routine and characters with personality
| Ruleborn with names, quirks and home routines; seasons
| Long grinds

| Pikmin
| Small creatures with elemental immunities follow a leader
| The crew model: ruleborn follow a route, and their school decides what they shrug off
| Constant micro-management

| Monument Valley
| Worlds that follow their own impossible rules, as puzzles
| Islands as visual puzzles; wonder first
| Linear, finite levels

| Into the Breach
| Enemies show what they're about to do, so cleverness wins
| Guard wind-ups and visible vision cones
| Turn-based slowness

| Hitman GO, Lara Croft GO
| Readable stealth on a phone
| Clear stealth rules: cones, shade, tall grass
| Rigid boards

| Genshin Impact
| Elements react to each other in memorable ways
| Harmonies and dissonances between laws
| Character gacha

| Squad Busters
| Strong launch, weak long-term pull
| The lesson: prove the core loop with real players before scaling (26.2)
| Relying on hype or crossovers

2.4 What changed because of this

A diorama camera you can rotate, instead of the fixed three-quarter view every base builder uses (21.1).

Combinations are the core of discovery through notes, harmonies and chords (8).

Pikmin-like crews with telegraphed enemies make heists about reading and timing (12.6 to 12.8).

Collection and showing off: Codex albums, island showcases and page trading.

Player-made islands become content through weekly defence challenges.

No base destruction and no wars: heists, co-op and friendly leagues.

2.7 The honest odds

In 2025 only 9 mobile games passed $1 billion in player spending and 828 passed $10 million, while the top 1% of publishers took 92.5% of in-app revenue [36].

Even a strong launch from the best studio can fail, as Squad Busters did [30], and soft-launch numbers can fade.

Scale in plain arithmetic: earning $2 billion a year means about $5.5 million a day. That is 18 million daily players spending 30 cents each, or 37 million spending 15 cents. Cosmetic-only games are likely nearer the lower figure, and soft launch’s measured spending replaces that assumption. With a third of monthly players playing on a given day — the daily-to-monthly gate in 25.1 — that means 55 to 110 million monthly players.

So the forecast is earned, not assumed: the gates in 25.1 say what would have to be true at each stage.

3Setting and tone

Long ago, an old keeper called the Archivist looked after the Book of Laws, a huge book whose pages told the world how to behave: stones fall, rivers flow, echoes fade. One stormy night the Book burst open. Its pages scattered across the sky, and wherever a page came to rest, a small island lifted into the air and began to follow what was written on it.

Now the sky is full of islands with strange little rules. Keepers are the people who look after a cluster of them. They gather torn pages of the Book, restore them, and write the laws into their own islands, which makes them richer and more wonderful. Other Keepers' islands hold pages you've never seen, and treasure worth borrowing.

| In the story
| In the game

| A page of the Book of Laws
| A law. Collecting enough torn pages of a law restores it.

| A floating island
| A realm. Each follows the laws written into it.

| A Keeper's cluster of islands
| The player's universe: the Heart and up to six outer realms.

| The little creatures born on the islands
| Ruleborn, shaped by the laws of the island where they hatch.

| The Archivist and other wise old keepers
| Sages, the heroes who can bend a rule for a moment.

| The skiff
| The small flying boat a crew uses to land on and leave another Keeper's islands.

Tone: wonder, cleverness and warmth; a heist caper in a picture-book sky. Nothing is killed; knocked-out characters wake up at home.

Look: floating island dioramas under a violet sky; each school of law has its own colour and shape; every law visible in how the world and its creatures move (see 21).

Humour: gentle and physical. Creatures slip on frictionless ground, sleepwalking guards bump into trees, and puffballs bleat at the worst moment.

The Archivist's laws are also called the rules of the world, and the creatures born where a page landed are the ruleborn. Both words mean the same thing; Keepers use whichever the moment calls for.

4Core loops

Ruleborn has four loops nested inside each other. The inner loops happen every session; the outer loops give reasons to come back for months.

Heists

Loot, pages, renown, strays

New laws, buildings, realms, discoveries

Stronger characters, cleverer defence

Then back to better heists. Each turn around the loop takes one or two sessions early on and a few days later.

| Loop
| Length
| What the player does
| What it feeds

| Heist
| 3 minutes
| Scout, plan a route, cross realms, open the vault, escape
| Loot, pages, renown

| Session
| 5 to 9 minutes
| Collect production, reassign workers, run one or two heists, weave or upgrade, queue a Lab experiment
| Steady growth and a reason to return in an hour

| Discovery
| 2 to 5 days
| Unlock a law from pages, open a realm, find a resonance, redesign the map
| New ways to play and defend

| Season
| 8 weeks
| Climb renown leagues, adapt to weekly eras, and from version 1.1 fight anomalies with the constellation and optionally begin a new epoch (26.1)
| Long-term goals and fresh starts

Logic
The two scarce things are stability (how much law your universe can hold) and characters' time (each one can work, guard or raid, never two at once). Almost every decision in the game is a choice about one of these two.

5Your universe

A Keeper's universe is a small sky of floating islands: the Heart and up to six outer realms, joined by bridges. This section sets out where each kind of rule applies, how realms and links work, how raiders arrive, and how much law a universe can hold.

5.1 Where rules apply

Rules nest like boxes. Each box sits inside a bigger one, and when rules meet, the smaller, inner box has the final say.

[SVG]
The six scopes, from the widest to the narrowest. Rules are applied from the outside in, so inner rules override outer ones.

| Scope
| Set by
| Applies to
| Lasts
| Example

| Era
| The game
| Every universe
| One week
| High Tide: Slow rivers costs no stability

| Grand Law
| A constellation's vote, from version 1.1 (17.2)
| Every member's universe, as a free extra crown law
| One week
| Bloom in every member's realms

| Crown law
| The Keeper, at the Spire
| Every realm of that universe, at full strength
| While woven
| Stillness everywhere: raiders can hide in any realm, but so can nothing move unseen

| Realm law
| The Keeper, at the Spire
| One realm only
| While woven
| Slow rivers in Tidehold

| Terrain
| The Keeper
| The tiles it covers
| Until changed
| A pond, tall grass, a cliff

| Zone
| Wards, raider casts, Sage powers
| A circle of tiles
| Seconds
| A cast of Haste for 10 seconds

| Character
| Birth, traits, level
| One character, everywhere
| Always
| A Swimmer ignores Slow rivers

Logic: crown and realm laws

A crown law works in every realm of its universe, including the Heart, and costs double stability. The Spire opens 1 crown slot at Spire level 2 and a second at Spire level 5.

A realm law works only in its own realm. The Heart has 2 realm slots; outer realms have 1, 2 or 3 by realm level.

The same law can't be both a crown law and a realm law in one universe; the stronger tier would simply apply.

A crown law adds 5% to its school's resource in every realm; a realm law adds 10% in its own realm.

5.2 Realms

Every universe has one Heart and up to six outer realms. Outer realms open at Keeper levels 3, 6, 10, 15, 21 and 28, and the player picks each one's type from those not yet owned, so a finished universe has one realm of each type.

| Type
| School
| Shape
| Boosted resource
| Signature terrain

| Heart
| Neutral
| Circle
| None
| Gardens and courtyards around the Spire

| Skyfield
| Motion
| Chevron

Wards and guard posts are a measured budget, not a formula. The ward limit used to be three plus the realm level, which gave the fewest wards to the realm that needs the most: the raiding crew does not grow with the realm it raids, so a low realm starts furthest behind it. Each realm level now gets the smaller of the wards it needs to reach its hold band (13.2) and eight, the legibility cap 13.2 sets: 8, 8, 6, 7 and 8. Realm level 1 keeps one guard post, because a second carries it far past its band: the weakest two-post setting there holds 62.4% of raids. Realm level 2 takes whichever of one or two posts puts its best setting within eight wards inside 30 to 45%, or nearest to it; if both land inside, the one nearer the band's centre, and on a tie one post, the fewer objects on screen. On the harness as it stands that is one post, holding 37.6% at eight wards against a band centred on 37.5%, and the aligned re-run applies the same rule (32). Levels 3 to 5 keep 2, 3 and 3 posts.

5.3 Links

| Link
| Crossing
| Special rule
| Limit
| Cost

| Stone bridge
| Normal speed
| Can hold one ward
| None
| Free

| Rope bridge
| 30% faster
| Can carry a Snap that drops the bridge; anyone on it falls back
| None
| 300

| Chute
| Fast, one way
| The owner sets the direction
| 2
| 1,500

| Rift
| Instant
| Closes for 30 seconds when an alarm sounds in either realm; 20 Aether an hour
| 1
| 5,000

The Heart has four link sockets and outer realms three. Every realm must connect to the Heart.

A universe needs one link per outer realm and may have two extra, for loops and shortcuts.

At least one walkable route must reach the Heart from every outer realm's edge. The game refuses layouts that seal the Vault.

5.4 Landing and leaving

Raiders arrive by skiff, a small flying boat, and the whole edge of the universe is open to them. Defenders can't choose where raiders come from; they can only make every edge a puzzle.

| Rule
| Detail

| Landing
| The crew lands on any edge tile of any outer realm the raider chooses. The Heart is ringed by strong winds, so skiffs can't land there.

| Being seen landing
| A landing skiff can be heard: guards within 6 tiles of the landing spot start at 250 of 1,000 suspicion (12.17), unless the spot is in fog, darkness or tall grass.

| Leaving
| At any time the crew can call the skiff to any edge tile of any realm. It takes 5 seconds to reach an outer realm and 20 seconds to fight the winds around the Heart. The crew must hold the spot until it arrives.

| Faults
| When a universe is overloaded (5.5), faults open between realms: cracks a crew can jump through once per heist, ignoring the links.

| Heist clock
| 150 seconds plus 15 seconds for each link on the shortest route from the landing realm to the Heart, up to 240 seconds. Haste makes the clock run 25% faster while the crew is in its realm. This row is the clock’s one home; 12.5 cites it.

Why open landing
Choosing where to land is one of the most fun decisions a raider makes, and it shouldn't belong to the defender. Open edges also make defence about the whole island: cliffs, tall grass, lantern light and laws along every edge, not one guarded gate.

5.5 Stability

Stability is how much law a universe can hold. It now comes from buildings, so growing it is part of the economy, and its effects come in seven bands instead of three.

Capacity = Keeper's Spire (30 + 8 × Spire level) + every Anchor Stone (8 + 5 × its level), raised 8% for each epoch after the first

Cost of a law = base cost × tier (1.0, 1.3 or 1.6) × 2 if crowned × 0.8 in a realm of its school × 0.85 for each harmony × 0.8 for each signature resonance × 0.9 in a chord × 1.25 for a dissonance

Strain = (total cost of woven laws + the draw of every sounding chord, 8.5) ÷ capacity

A resonance discount stacks like a harmony's: a law in two signature resonances pays 0.8 twice, as 8.4's “lowers both laws' cost by 20%” says of each. A chord sounds only while woven laws and every sounding chord's draw together stay within capacity (8.6).

Harmonies, resonances, chords and dissonances come from the notes laws carry (see 8). At the maximum, a Spire at level 10 and seven Anchor Stones at level 10 give 516 stability, while a full set of 22 woven laws at tier III costs around 500 to 560, so a finished universe always sits near its limit.

| Band
| Strain
| Effect
| What the islands look like

| Quiet
| Under 40%
| None
| Still air; laws barely visible

| Steady
| 40 to 70%
| +5% production everywhere
| Pylons glow softly

| Humming
| 70 to 90%
| +10% production
| A low hum; motes drift up from the pylons

| Brimming
| 90 to 100%
| +15% production; pylons have 10% more health
| Islands shimmer; the Spire's rings spin faster

| Strained
| 100 to 110%
| +15% production; one fault opens between two realms
| A glowing crack runs across one island

| Cracking
| 110 to 125%
| Two or three faults; during heists each law has a 5% chance every 10 seconds of blinking off for 2 seconds
| Cracks spread; laws flicker

| Breaking
| 125% and over
| No new weaving; four faults; laws flicker twice as often
| Islands tremble; stones drift loose

Logic
Production bonuses reward using the capacity you've built; faults and flickering are the price of pushing past it. Overloading is a legitimate choice for a player who wants more laws and accepts a weaker defence. Faults close as soon as strain drops back under 100%.

[SVG]How full an engaged player's universe runs over time, assuming typical realm matches and harmonies (simulated). It rises into Humming by month 2 and sits at the edge of Strained late in the game, where optimising becomes the puzzle.

5.6 Weaving laws

Laws are woven into slots at the Keeper's Spire: 10 minutes at tier I, 40 minutes at tier II and 2 hours at tier III. Weaving costs 40 Aether per point of the law's stability cost.

While a slot is being woven it is empty. Removing a law is instant, but the slot then rests for an hour before it can be woven again.

Upkeep is a share of the realm's own hourly Aether production, not a flat rate: 25% when the realm's stability budget is fully spent, scaling in proportion to the share actually used. The share is set by 9.5's sink rule, and 25% is what ships until the model's re-run sets it (32.9). So a realm running laws worth half its stability pays 12.5% of what it earns, and one running none pays nothing. Pricing it per point of stability would not work — law costs run 10 to 26 points, so a single common law would owe most of a realm's hourly output and a deep one more than all of it. The charge scales with the share of the budget spent, not with the points themselves. A flat charge fails at both ends of a compounding economy: measured against the production curve, 0.5 an hour was 60% of a day's Aether on day 2 and 3.2% by day 301, and raising the flat rate only postpones the late-game problem by about forty days while making the early game unplayable. Pricing it off the faucet that pays it is what EVE Online does with structure fuel — a consumable drawn every hour a service runs, scaling with what the player operates rather than with a fixed number. It pauses once the player has been away for 24 hours, and if Aether runs out, laws run at half strength rather than switching off.

5.7 The realm bible

Each realm type has a palette, a soundtrack, wildlife, a level-design pattern that shapes how heists play there, and one signature moment it exists to produce. The pattern is the important part: it is what makes a Tidehold heist feel different from an Emberreach heist even before any law is woven.

5.8 One producer of each kind per realm

Stated here once, because three tables assumed different answers and the arithmetic only works for one of them. A realm holds one Star Well, one Aether Spring and one Garden, however high its level rises.

Why not two. Permitting a second was a pure gain, so everyone would build it, and it doubled income against every pacing date in 14. Re-running the economy model, only one producer per realm reproduced the curve published before version 3.3 — within 10% of the day-301 figure and three days on the Keeper track. Re-run under all three of this section’s rules, production falls 15%, 50%, 59% and 52% below that curve at days 31, 91, 181 and 301, which 9.3 now carries. Every other reading was out by two to four times.

Staffing is partial and worthwhile. A producer runs at the share of its worker slots that are filled, so a half-staffed Well earns half. The roster can never fill everything — 38 ruleborn against far more positions — and that is the scarcity the design is built on (4), now connected to something real.

Raiding costs production. A crew on a heist is assigned to it, like a guard at a post, and does not work while assigned. Five of thirty-eight is a real choice; the older version withheld them for about 2% of a month and the trade-off the design claims did not exist.

6Terrain

Without walls, the ground shapes every route. Terrain is the Keeper's main building material for defence: 39 features in seven families, each changing how creatures move, what guards can see, and how laws behave.

6.1 Rules

Budget: an outer realm has 30 terrain points plus 15 per realm level (45 to 105). The Heart has 40 plus 10 per Spire level. Reshaping is free and instant; it's the budget that limits it.

Snapshots: a heist is played against the terrain as it was when the heist started.

The route rule: terrain can lengthen routes but never close the last route from the edges to the Heart.

Height: four heights (0 to 3). Dropping down is free and one-way, so height creates one-way paths without chutes.

Noise: stairs, gravel, scree, bells and bounce mushrooms make noise; noise raises the awareness of guards nearby.

Quiet ground: moss is the one quiet surface. It is ground cover rather than one of the 39 features: every realm is raised with moss on each tile that carries no feature, and a Keeper clears it for 1 terrain point a tile, from the same budget. Every step on moss carries three quarters as far, so a crouched step carries 0.75 m, under a tile (12.17). An ambush needs an unheard approach, and on bare ground the last crouched step beside a guard is heard, so moss is a defensive decision: the moss behind a post is the ambush's way in, and a Keeper who clears it denies the ambush there (12.8), at the price of points not spent on features.

Why so much terrain
Terrain is cheap to build in code, readable at a glance, and multiplies with laws: 39 features against 48 laws give well over a thousand distinct interactions, most of them visible in how creatures move. It also makes defence creative, which is what feeds the weekly defence challenges (18.5).

7Laws

Laws are the torn pages of the Book: rules an island follows. They are what players collect, the source of each island's economy and defence, and the puzzle in every heist. There are 48 in six schools, of which 24 ship at launch and the rest follow through seasons (7.10), three paradoxes found only in the Lab, and a system of notes that decides how laws combine (see 8).

7.1 What a law is

| Property
| Rule

| School
| One of six families, each with a colour and a shape so it never depends on colour alone.

| Rank
| Common, uncommon, rare or deep — 18, 12, 12 and 6 laws, three, two, two and one a school — with paradoxes outside the scale. Rank is a strength ladder paid for in pages; stability cost is the balance dial (7.9).

| Stability cost
| From 10 to 26 points (40 to 50 for paradoxes), before the modifiers in 5.5.

| Notes
| One or two of eight notes (Flow, Weight, Glow, Hush, Bloom, Echo, Spark, Veil) that decide harmonies and dissonances (8).

| Tiers
| I, II and III. Higher tiers are stronger and cost more stability (× 1.3 and × 1.6).

| Where it applies
| As a realm law, in one realm; as a crown law, in every realm of the universe (5.1).

| Symmetry
| A law applies to everyone where it rules: the owner's guards and wards as much as a raiding crew.

| Movement signature
| Every law changes how characters move, so a player can read the rule by watching (7.4, 21.7).

| Economy
| Crown and realm laws add to their school's resource, by the amounts 5.1 sets.

7.2 Schools

| School
| Shape
| Theme
| Boosts
| Most common notes

| Motion
| Chevron
| Jumping, sliding, wind, slopes
| Starlight
| Flow

| Matter
| Cube
| Walls, weight, glass, crystal
| Starlight
| Weight, Veil

| Time
| Hourglass
| Speed, pauses, cycles, tides
| Aether
| Hush

| Energy
| Bolt
| Light, heat, sparks, storms
| Aether
| Spark, Glow

| Life
| Leaf
| Growth, healing, flocks, pollen
| Essence
| Bloom

| Spirit
| Eye
| Echoes, illusions, dreams, masks
| Essence
| Echo, Veil, Glow

7.3 How laws combine

Each realm has a rule sheet of parameters that every character, guard and ward reads. Laws change parameters; they never contain special cases for particular characters. The sheet has exactly twenty-two parameters, and every tier effect of every law is a change to one of them (12.21).

| Parameter
| Default
| Changed by

| Speed
| 1.0 ×
| Haste, Honeycomb, Slow rivers, Wild paths, Density, Spores, Frictionless

| Push
| None
| Tilt, Current, Inward pull, Lodestones, Shifting stones — a direction field on characters and loot, or on walls along a drawn loop

| Leap and climb
| 1 tile, no climbing
| Half gravity, Leapfrog, Honeycomb

| Flight
| Flyers fly (the Flight column, 12.21)
| Heavy ground

| Contact
| Friction on; walls stop what hits them
| Frictionless, Bounce

| Sight
| 6 tiles, light
| Glowing dark, Aurora, Day and night, Sleepwalk

| Hearing
| Normal hearing
| Sleepwalk, Leapfrog

| Cover
| Walls, cover and shade block sight
| Glass, Stillness, Honeycomb, Crystal growth, Sunburst

| Trails
| None
| Spark trails, Haunting, Afterimage, Wild paths

| Alert
| Noticing as in 12.17; an alarm stays in its realm
| Leapfrog, Flock, Whispers, Haunting

| Damage
| 1.0 ×, one target a hit
| Heat bloom, Chain lightning, Brittle stone

| Health
| 1.0 ×, read by size (the Size column, 12.21)
| Density

| Touch damage
| None
| Thorns

| Unlit drain
| None
| Soul lanterns

| Ally healing
| None
| Kinship

| Structure regrowth
| None; broken things stay broken
| Regrowth, Bloom, Crystal growth, Tidemark

| Stun
| None
| Static, Sunburst

| Time
| Constant; an effect lands when it is triggered
| Haste, Held breath, Delay, Overcharge

| Loot
| 1.0 ×, fixed type, loaded at 30 a second (12.19)
| Heavy ground, Transmute, Lodestones

| Who is who
| Clear
| Mirror images, Borrowed Face

| Revival
| None. Three shapes, each with its own N: return once at N% of full strength; split into two at N%; swap with the nearest ally, who takes N% of the blow. Realm-wide sources set it for everyone they cover; a creature source for that creature
| Echoes (return, 60%), Swarm birth (split, 30%), Soul swap (swap, 50%); also the Echo form (11.10), the Echoing Skies era (18.3) and Lantern festival (8.5)

| Knockout
| A crew member at zero health waits ten seconds for capture where it fell (12.20)
| Ghost tide (8.5): it drifts back to where it entered the realm instead

The list is closed and versioned. These twenty-two are the whole rule sheet, version 2. Version 1 had twenty; Drain and Healing and regrowth each named one parameter for two subjects, so a simulation could not apply either without knowing which law set it, and version 2 split each into two whose names carry their subject. Nothing had shipped, so no pinned island or replay is affected. A law that needs a twenty-third parameter is a design decision that makes version 3, never a content addition, because a vocabulary that grows to fit each new law is not a vocabulary.

One named exception: the view-layer reveal. A reveal changes only what one player is shown — hidden wards, guard vision cones — and nothing the simulation decides, so it lives in the view layer and the server never re-simulates it. It is one-way: a reveal that ever feeds back into simulated behaviour becomes a parameter and needs that decision. Glass's tiers II and III and the Lighthouse resonance are its only users (7.7, 8.4).

Logic: order of application

Start from the defaults.

Apply the Era, then the Grand Law, then crown laws, then the realm's laws, in the fixed school order Motion, Matter, Time, Energy, Life, Spirit.

Apply terrain, then zones (wards and raider casts).

Apply Sage powers, which can suspend or flip any law.

Apply each character's own traits and immunities last.

Multipliers multiply; on/off rules take the last value applied; when several Revival sources cover one knockout, the highest N applies, and only once — the 20% Echo form inside the 40% Echoing Skies era returns once, at 40%. Every law, the sixteen conditional or periodic ones included (7.10), runs under the ordered-execution contract in 24.7. The simulation runs at a fixed 30 steps a second so heists replay identically on the server (the rate is defined once, in 24.10, and every other mention is checked against it).

7.4 The 48 laws

Each school has three common, two uncommon, two rare and one deep law — eight laws a school, six schools, 48 in all. The table gives each law's rank and base stability cost, its notes, what it does at tier I and higher tiers, and how it changes the way characters move; Launch marks the 24 that ship at launch (7.10). It is generated from the sheets in 7.7 by the build and is never written by hand, and check_law_table_against_sheets still compares all 240 shared facts on every build, reading both sides from the document.

7.5 Paradoxes

Paradoxes are wild laws found only in the Lab, by combining two tier III laws with opposite notes. Each such experiment has a 10% chance of revealing one, and the Lab warns when a pairing is "unstable". A universe can hold one paradox at a time, and paradoxes give no economy bonus.

| Paradox
| Cost
| Effect
| How characters move

| Reversal
| 45
| The Heart swaps places with the outermost realm: the Vault moves to the frontier and every route is reversed.
| Everyone walks with a slight upside-down sway; hair points at the sky.

| Twin suns
| 50
| Every other law in the realm applies twice.
| Every character casts two shadows and moves with doubled flourishes.

| Unmaking
| 40
| Every 20 seconds one law in the realm, raider casts included, switches off at random for 10 seconds.
| Characters stutter as rules blink in and out.

7.6 Getting and improving laws

Pages from heists, which is the point of raiding. After each heist the player picks one law the crew crossed to study and receives 2 of its pages, 3 if the heist was clean. The first time a player meets a law it joins the Codex with 1 page. Breaking a pylon gives a page of its law; a foiled heist gives one.

Raiding is the efficient path, not the only one. A Library at the Heart produces pages passively at the rate its own table gives — 3 a day at level 1, 9 at 5, 18 at 10, across the whole universe (10 owns this table) — enough to keep restoring something slowly, nowhere near enough to raise a school. A single heist is worth roughly two to three sessions of that trickle. Making raiding the sole source would punish the many players who prefer building (13.1), and gating a resource behind one activity is where players stop feeling directed and start feeling forced [210].

Pages lean toward what you raided. Raiding a Tidehold yields mostly Time pages — around two in three — with the rest spread across the schools you met. That is enough to make "I want Time laws, so I raid Tideholds" a real plan, without the resentment that comes from an exclusive lock [211].

Nothing is gated by the clock. No day-of-the-week rotations, no timed schools, no windows. The most-complained-about version of directional farming is the one that decides when you may pursue what you want [210]; ours decides only where.

A floor for bad luck. Every eighth heist guarantees a page of a law you are actually working on, so an unlucky run of targets never costs a week.

The Codex opens in chapters: at launch it holds the launch 24 (7), 12 findable from the start and all 24 from Keeper level 6. Laws added after launch (26.1) extend it toward the full 48 in the same steps — the 25th to 36th from level 12, the 37th to 42nd from level 18 and the rest from level 24 — so each season's laws open at once for anyone past their chapter's level. Matchmaking only shows universes running laws from open chapters.

Restoring a law: 10, 20, 35 or 60 pages by rank — 10 pages for a common law, 20 for an uncommon, 35 for a rare and 60 for a deep one. This is the home of the page costs; 9.5 and 29.1 cross-reference it.

Tiers: tier II costs 20 more pages and 2,000 Aether (Spire level 4); tier III costs 40 more pages and 8,000 Aether (Spire level 7).

Other sources: Lab experiments that find nothing give 3 pages of a law from those schools; the Collector swaps pages; constellation members can gift pages, from version 1.1 (17.4); daily goals give a few.

Starting laws: Half gravity, Slow rivers and Bloom, all at tier I.

Every school pays its own milestones. No reward waits for the whole Codex. Each school's album opens partly filled (14.8) — the starting laws open Motion, Time and Life, and any other school opens with the page its first law gives on meeting it — and pays a milestone at two, four and all eight laws restored: a Codex stamp, a skiff pennant in the school's colour and shape, and the school's crest on the player's islands. All three are cosmetic, so no milestone moves the page economy (14.4).

Why pages come from crossing
Players learn new laws by experiencing them on someone else's island. Every heist is also a lesson, which gives a reason to raid islands with unfamiliar laws rather than always the easiest target.

7.7 Every law in full

Each law's full sheet: what it does at every tier, how it changes movement, how the realm looks under it, how a raider turns it to their advantage, how a defender uses it, where it is weak, and what it sounds like. A law that cannot be used by both sides is not finished, and a law without a weakness is not shipped.

TidemarkDeep · Time · cost 26

Echo

| Effect kind
| Conditional or periodic: a periodic mark, and a return by checkpoint replay. The simulation checkpoints each realm at every mark, and the return reads the broken structures' state at the mark from that checkpoint — on the server, by replaying from it with the seed and input log it already keeps (24.10). No rolling history is stored, only structures return, and nothing reaches back further than one mark: 10 seconds, 300 steps, at tier I. It takes Rewind's slot, rank, school and note; Rewind restored the whole world ten seconds back, which no shipped engine does (32.3). Follows after launch (7.10).

| Tier I
| Every 20 seconds the tide leaves a mark on the realm; 10 seconds later, everything broken since returns to how it stood at the mark.

| Tiers II and III
| Every 16 s, 12 s

| How characters move
| Characters glance at the tide line as debris flies back to it.

| How the realm looks
| A thin silver tide line sweeps the ground at each mark; broken pieces whoosh back to it when it ebbs.

| A raider uses it to
| Break what blocks you just after a return, and be through before the next one.

| A defender uses it to
| Bring back the pylon a crew broke since the last mark.

| Its weakness
| It returns the defender's losses as readily as yours, and the timing is shown to everyone.

| It sounds like
| a slow tick that stretches and snaps back

7.8 How much there is to understand

Depth comes from combining laws, which is also the biggest risk: too many unfamiliar rules at once and new players leave. Every heist has a complexity budget.

| Rule
| Keeper levels 1 to 9
| 10 to 19
| 20 and up

| Laws on the route the player has never met
| 1 at most
| 2 at most
| 3 at most

| Rules that interact at any one moment
| Never more than 4, at any level. Working memory holds about four chunks of new, interacting information [75] [76], and difficulty rises with how many pieces must be held at once [77] [78]. A realm may hold more laws than that only when the extra ones do not interact with the crew's route.

| Realms on a typical route
| 2 to 3
| 3 to 4
| 3 to 5

| Harmony shown on the route
| Harmonies only
| Plus signature resonances
| Plus chords and paradoxes

Laws are always visible: every law has a movement signature, a world signature, a card with a looping preview, and a two-second banner when the crew enters its realm.

Colour is never the only signal: every school has a shape, and every note has a small symbol.

Each law is taught alone first: the Drifting ruins introduce each new law on its own before it appears in combinations (15).

7.9 Rank is a strength ladder, paid for in pages

Laws come in four ranks, plus paradoxes. Rank is a strength ladder: a rarer law does more, on the whole costs more stability to weave, and takes more pages to restore. Stability cost is the balance dial (5.5); pages pace the collection (7.6). The two prices do different jobs, which is how shipped games with a runtime price and an acquisition price work: the runtime price balances power and the acquisition price paces collecting. Earlier versions claimed that rank set only how long a law takes to get and not how strong it is. Across the 48 sheets rank predicts stability cost at r = +0.79, with the medians climbing the ladder — common 13.5, uncommon 17, rare 19, deep 22.5 — so the claim is withdrawn rather than enforced. tools/law_ranks.py reports r on every build.

| Rank
| Count
| Character
| Pages to restore

| Common
| 18
| Always useful, small effect, no drawback. Three per school
| 10

| Uncommon
| 12
| Conditional, with a mild cost. Two per school
| 20

| Rare
| 12
| Strong inside a condition you have to build for. Two per school
| 35

| Deep
| 6
| Changes how a realm works. Heavy stability cost and a real trade-off. One per school
| 60

| Paradox
| 3
| Universe-wide, one at a time, found only in the Lab
| 120

Each rarer rank is no larger than the one below it. The game that invented collectable rarity gives the reason: the larger the effect, the higher the rarity, and larger effects tend to cost more, so a player wants fewer of them [207]. The same holds here, because a stronger law costs more stability and a universe weaves fewer of them. Version 3.3 therefore moved, in each school, the deep law with the lower stability cost to rare, ties broken alphabetically: Frictionless, Shifting stones, Held breath, Overcharge, Regrowth and Borrowed Face. Every school now runs three, two, two and one, and the full Codex is 1,200 pages at tier I (14.4). That also ends the inversion in which deep, at twelve, matched uncommon and doubled rare.

Rarity is still set by complexity, size of effect and excitement [207], and in every large shipped game rarer cards are stronger in practice [208]. Ruleborn states that coupling rather than fighting it, and balances it with stability.

Visibility is not a price rarity pays

The scout report (12.4) shows each realm's laws — every rank alike — for 20 Aether a realm, valid for fifteen minutes. A common law is seen at exactly the same price as a deep one, so visibility is not charged against a law's strength and does nothing to balance rank. What pays for a deep law's strength is its stability cost.

Conditions, not punishments. A rare law is powerful when a condition you control is met. Laws that simply hurt their owner are avoided: the evidence on collectable design is that conditional build-around power is enjoyed while flat drawbacks are resented [207].

Collecting is grouped by school, eight laws each. A set of 48 is far past the point where a collection reads as completable, while seven to twelve is where people push to finish [209]; six sets of eight gives that feeling six times over. So each school pays its own milestones and no single reward waits for the whole Codex (7.6).

7.10 What a law costs to build, and the 24 that ship first

Every law's sheet (7.7) is tagged, as data, with the kind of engine work its effect needs. The tag is what the launch cut is ordered by, so the cut drops the expensive laws rather than the unpopular ones. The ladder has five rungs, each weighted by the work it costs; a law sits on the highest rung it needs.

| Rung
| What the effect needs
| Engine points
| Laws

| Parameter
| A change to the rule sheet (7.3) and nothing else
| 0
| 16

| Placement or attribute
| A placement the owner or the generator makes, or a creature attribute (12.21)
| 1
| 6 (4 placements, 2 attributes)

| Conditional or periodic
| Its own timer, counter or trigger, run under the ordered-execution contract (24.7)
| 2
| 16

| Spawned entity
| An object the simulation must spawn and track every step — the only kind whose cost grows the work of re-simulating a heist
| 4
| 10

| Rewind
| Restoring past world state
| 8
| 0

All 48 cost 78 engine points. Rewind was the only law on the top rung; Tidemark takes its slot as a checkpoint return on the third (7.7), and a law that needs the top rung again is a design decision, like a twenty-third parameter (7.3). Once the conditional and spawning machinery exists, the share of laws needing bespoke code should fall toward a fifth.

The launch 24. Every parameter law ships, then the cheapest others by rung — lower stability cost, then name, breaking ties — until each school has four. Tidemark is left out. The 24 cost 14 engine points, against 36 for a 24 chosen by rank, for the same amount of content. They include all three starting laws, reach all twelve chords, hold 12 of the 44 signature resonances (8.4) and pair opposite notes, so paradox experiments exist from launch. The rest follow through seasons (26).

| School
| Ships at launch
| Follows, cheapest first

| Motion
| Bounce, Half gravity, Frictionless, Inward pull
| Tilt, Current, Heavy ground, Leapfrog

| Matter
| Brittle stone, Glass, Honeycomb, Density
| Crystal growth, Shifting stones, Transmute, Lodestones

| Time
| Haste, Slow rivers, Delay, Stillness
| Day and night, Held breath, Tidemark, Afterimage

| Energy
| Glowing dark, Static, Aurora, Chain lightning
| Overcharge, Sunburst, Spark trails, Heat bloom

| Life
| Kinship, Thorns, Regrowth, Bloom
| Wild paths, Flock, Spores, Swarm birth

| Spirit
| Whispers, Echoes, Sleepwalk, Soul swap
| Soul lanterns, Borrowed Face, Haunting, Mirror images

The 24 that wait, in cut order. Expensive first, by the same rule read backwards — highest rung, then higher stability cost, then name: Swarm birth, Mirror images, Haunting, Lodestones, Spores, Heat bloom, Afterimage, Flock, Spark trails and Wild paths, the ten that spawn an entity; then Tidemark, Held breath, Sunburst, Shifting stones, Transmute, Borrowed Face, Overcharge, Day and night, Crystal growth and Leapfrog, the conditional ones; then Heavy ground, Current, Soul lanterns and Tilt, the placements. The table above gives the same order school by school from the other end, which is the order seasons bring them back. The order is data, fixed by the rule, so taste cannot re-order it (26.1).

One rule places what a generator must author. Eight laws were written with a choice that is not a number — a direction, a stream, a placement or a loop: Tilt, Current, Slow rivers, Lodestones, Heat bloom, Soul lanterns, Shifting stones and Inward pull. When the generator makes such a choice for a curated island (13.3), one rule makes it for all of them: place it against the shortest route from the landing edge to the Heart — along the route for what costs a crosser to pass, across it for what costs a crosser to cross, and never so that the route is sealed (6.1). A slope or a wind points at the Heart, because the loaded escape is the tense half (12.6). Lodestones is the one placed off the route, flanking it, because a pull on the route would drag a crew along it. Inward pull's choice is no longer a choice: its sheet fixes the reference. Each of the eight sheets states its instance. The rule rests on analogy — declarative choice rules and one constraint a solver can take, from the procedural-generation literature — not on a study of this case, because none exists; its effect on crossing difficulty is measured by the generator's expressive-range check (13.3), and an owner can change any one law's choice on the curation screen (23.2).

8Harmony: how laws combine

Version 0.2 had a list of 32 special pairs. Harmony replaces that with a system players can learn and predict: every law carries one or two notes, and notes decide what happens when laws share an island. Players meet it like music: some laws sound well together, some clash, and a few special combinations sing.

8.1 The eight notes

| Note
| What laws with this note do
| Opposite
| Symbol

| Flow
| Change how things move: speed, direction, jumping, sliding
| Weight
| A wave

| Weight
| Slow, pull, hold and weigh down
| Flow
| An anchor

| Glow
| Light, reveal and show
| Hush
| A sun

| Hush
| Hide, silence and still
| Glow
| A crescent

| Bloom
| Grow, heal and multiply
| Spark
| A sprout

| Spark
| Burst, stun, break and surge
| Bloom
| A star

| Echo
| Return, repeat and restore
| Veil
| A ring

| Veil
| Disguise, deceive and transform
| Echo
| A mask

[SVG]The eight notes. Laws that share a note harmonise; laws with opposite notes (joined by dashed lines) clash. The number shows how many of the 48 laws carry each note.

8.2 Harmonies

When two laws in the same realm share a note, they harmonise. Crown laws count in every realm, so a crown law can harmonise with every realm's laws. A harmony lowers the stability cost of both laws by 15% and strengthens the shared kind of effect. Harmonies are shown automatically when weaving, so players learn them by doing.

| Shared note
| Harmony effect on both laws
| Example

| Flow
| Movement effects 25% stronger
| Current + Tilt: wind and slope together

| Weight
| Slowing and pulling 25% stronger
| Slow rivers + Heavy ground

| Glow
| Light and reveal ranges 2 tiles longer
| Soul lanterns + Spark trails

| Hush
| Hiding works 1 tile closer to guards
| Stillness + Glowing dark

| Bloom
| Regrowth and healing 25% stronger
| Bloom + Kinship

| Spark
| Damage and stuns 25% stronger
| Static + Chain lightning

| Echo
| Returns and repeats 25% sooner or stronger
| Tidemark + Echoes

| Veil
| Illusions and disguises last 25% longer
| Mirror images + Borrowed Face

8.3 Dissonances

When a realm holds laws with opposite notes, they clash. A dissonance raises both laws' stability cost by 25% and adds a visible, unpredictable effect. Some players will choose dissonance on purpose, to make their islands chaotic for raiders.

| Clash
| Effect in the realm
| What it looks like

| Flow against Weight
| Gusts and lulls: everyone's speed changes by ±20% at random every 10 seconds
| Characters lurch and stall

| Glow against Hush
| Flicker: every 15 seconds the realm goes fully bright or fully dark for 3 seconds
| Light strobes across the island

| Bloom against Spark
| Wildfire: hedges and grass hit by any spark effect burn for 5 seconds, hurting anyone inside
| Small flames race through greenery

| Echo against Veil
| Déjà vu: once per heist, an illusion of the crew appears somewhere else and draws guards
| A ghostly copy of the crew

8.4 Signature resonances

A signature resonance is a named, hand-designed bonus between two specific laws. It lowers both laws' cost by 20% and adds a unique effect. There are 44 in the full table: the 32 from version 0.2 and 12 for the newer laws. Twelve of them join two laws that both ship at launch (7.10); the rest arrive with their laws. Unlike harmonies, they are hidden until discovered in the Lab, or hinted at after crossing them in someone else's universe.

8.5 Chords

A chord is three different notes, none of them opposites, carried by three distinct laws in the same realm (crown laws count; 8.8). It lowers the cost of all three laws by 10% and adds a strong effect. While it sounds, a chord also draws its own stability, and that draw is what places chords late; the laws' own costs, which are the strength dial (7.9), do not move. The Lab names chords — from level 3 it identifies one the player has woven or crossed — and is a teaching surface, not a lock.

Chord draw = stability capacity on the day before the intended discovery − 0.9 × the cheapest chord-completing triple, priced by 5.5 at its cheapest placement that day, + 1, rounded up to a whole point

The intended discovery is Lab level 3 and Spire 5, about day 42 for an engaged player (10.2, 14.3), so the capacity is day 41's: Spire 5 gives 70, and five Anchor Stones give 145 — levels 5, 5, 4, 4 and 3, each later stone following the first stone's schedule from the day its realm opened — for 215

The cheapest chord on day 41, priced by 5.5 at tier I with the Heart's two slots and the Spire's two crown slots (5.1): Slow rivers and Thorns woven in the Heart with Bloom crowned, completing Deep sleep. Two harmonies and two signature resonances (Mangrove banks, Briar maze) cut it and the crown doubles Bloom: 6.8 + 8.67 + 11.97 = 27.44. 8.8's bare-cost triple, Bloom, Brittle stone and Tilt at 32, is not the cheapest once 5.5's discounts apply

Draw = 215 − 0.9 × 27.44 + 1 = 191.3, so 192 stability: on day 41 the cheapest chord needs 24.69 + 192 = 216.69 against 215

192 ships until the economy model's own day-41 capacity replaces it by the same rule (the measurement ledger in 32); tools/scope_cut.py asserts the earliest chord day on every build

Placing chords late is an intention, not a proven optimum. No study shows that a combination found late is enjoyed more than one taught early. Shipped combinatorial games open their deepest layer by mastery or play rather than by a date, which supports a gate the player grows into but gives no day, so day 42 — the one date the design already attached to chords — stands.

| Chord
| Notes
| Effect

| Tempest
| Flow Spark Glow
| Everyone moves 20% faster, and every 20 seconds lightning lights the whole realm for a second.

| Deep sleep
| Weight Hush Bloom
| Every 20 seconds each guard dozes for 3 seconds; flowers muffle footsteps.

| Ghost tide
| Flow Echo Hush
| Knocked-out characters drift back to where they entered the realm instead of being caught (Knockout, 7.3).

| Fair lights
| Glow Bloom Veil
| Floating lights form illusions of crew members that draw guards away.

| Iron bell
| Weight Echo Glow
| Every step echoes: guards hear 2 tiles further but see 2 tiles less.

| Mirage
| Flow Veil Glow
| Anything moving appears one tile from where it really is.

| Clockwork
| Weight Spark Echo
| Every 15 seconds all wards in the realm trigger together.

| Wild hunt
| Flow Spark Hush
| Anyone running makes no noise at all.

| Hidden garden
| Bloom Hush Veil
| Tall grass spreads one tile a minute across the realm.

| Lantern festival
| Glow Bloom Echo
| Lanterns heal anyone in their light, and each character returns once at the revive health, 30% of its role's health (Revival, 7.3; 12.20).

| Quicksand
| Weight Veil Hush
| A few tiles become hidden pits that drop anyone a level.

| Rebound
| Flow Echo Spark
| Every stun bounces on to the nearest enemy.

8.6 How players discover combinations

| Kind
| How it's found
| Shown when
| Count

| Harmony
| Automatic whenever two laws share a note
| While weaving and scouting, from the first day
| 8 kinds

| Dissonance
| Automatic whenever opposite notes meet
| While weaving, with a warning
| 4 kinds

| Signature resonance
| The Lab, or crossing it in a heist (gives a hint)
| Once discovered; the Codex shows how many remain in each school
| 44 (12 at launch)

| Chord
| Sounds whenever three distinct laws in one realm carry its notes and the universe can pay its draw (8.5); the Lab names it from level 3, and crossing one gives a hint
| Once discovered
| 12

| Paradox
| The Lab, from two tier III laws with opposite notes
| Once discovered
| 3

Taught in stages. Working memory holds about four new chunks at once (7.8), so the table is never taught whole. The first session teaches one note and one resonance; two-note resonances arrive through play; the three-note chord comes last, as the late discovery 8.5 places at about day 42.

Why notes
A list of pairs has to be memorised; notes can be reasoned about. A player who knows that Hush laws hide better together can predict hundreds of combinations without looking anything up, which is how Genshin Impact's elemental reactions and Balatro's jokers stay learnable while feeling endless. The hidden signatures and chords keep the thrill of discovery on top of that.

8.7 What happens when everything applies at once

A law, a resonance and a chord can all change the same thing. The result must be identical on the phone, on the server and a year later, so the order is fixed rather than arbitrary. It is adapted from the rules engine that has been solving this problem in public for thirty years [217].

| Step
| Rule

| 1
| Kind before number. Changes to what something is — terrain becoming shadow, a guard gaining an ability — resolve before changes to how much of something there is. Within numbers, "becomes X" comes before "and then add Y"

| 2
| Older first. A law woven while designing the island applies before one triggered mid-heist

| 3
| Dependencies jump the queue. If one effect changes what another does or whom it touches, the second waits for the first. Circular cases fall back to age order

| 4
| Ties break by name. Lower rank, then alphabetical order, then the older object. Because names are unique and fixed, a tie breaks the same way everywhere, forever

Paradoxes always win. They are written as "cannot" rather than "less", and resolve last inside their layer, so no ordinary law can overturn one.

8.8 A chord needs three laws, not three notes

Because 23 of the 48 laws carry two notes, every chord was reachable with two laws, several with two commons — Slow rivers and Whispers together gave Iron bell for 26 stability, inside a second-day player's budget. So a chord now requires three distinct laws contributing the three notes. That rule makes a chord three laws; it does not make it late. Five well-chosen laws reach all twelve chords — Afterimage, Day and night, Held breath, Sunburst and Wild paths are one such five, and no four do since the re-noting below — and the cheapest chord-completing triple — Bloom, Brittle stone and Tilt — costs 32, less than the 38 stability a level 1 Spire gives on the first day (5.5). The size of the law table is not the gate; the chord's own stability draw is (8.5).

The note counts below are generated from the sheets in 7.7 on every build and are never typed here: Echo 10, Hush 10, Flow 9, Glow 9, Weight 9, Bloom 8, Spark 8, Veil 8. The table used to run Glow 12 to Hush 6 — the revealing note most abundant and the hiding note scarcest, for a defender who has every reason to reveal. Version 3.3 re-noted nine laws, each by 8.1's own definitions. Day and night, Honeycomb, Bloom and Delay gain Hush, because each hides or silences: a dark phase, a climber unseen on a wall, hedges that close behind a crew and block sight again, a ward that stays silent for its delay. Crystal growth trades Glow for Veil, because it transforms walls and reveals nothing. Flock and Heat bloom give up Glow, because neither lights, reveals or shows. Aurora and Soul swap give up Flow, because neither changes speed, direction, jumping or sliding. The result is a band of eight to ten rather than a flat count, skewed on purpose toward demand: Hush, Echo, Flow and Glow each complete five of the twelve chords, the other four complete four, and no note that completes five is carried less often than one that completes four. No law carries two opposite notes, and all twelve chords stay reachable; tools/note_rebalance.py checks both.

A standing note budget. The note count is frozen at eight and laws are the expansion axis (24), so six new laws a season noted by taste would bring a skew back within a few seasons. Each season's laws are therefore allocated against the band before they are authored: after the drop no note may have more than two carriers fewer than the most-carried one, and each note that completes five chords stays at or above each note that completes four. The content build fails otherwise (24).

9Resources and economy

Three resources run the islands, a handful of progress currencies track growth, and one premium currency buys only looks. Every one connects buildings, laws and characters in a loop, shown below.

Stability is not a currency: it's the capacity built by the Spire and Anchor Stones (5.5).

Thirty-eight characters against the 63 producer slots of a finished universe (seven realms, three producers each, three slots at the top of every ladder) is an allocation budget: under-staffing is normal, and choosing which producer goes short is the decision

| Guarding
| Guardian Posts
| Scouts for sight, Wardens for chokepoints, Menders for support
| Guards are the defender's ruleborn; see 12.8

| Heists
| The crew of 3 to 5 plus a Sage
| A mix of roles and immunities that fits the target's laws
| Crews come home tired and rest

| Home life
| Hearths
| Anyone resting
| Rest time falls with Hearth level

9.3 Production

Output an hour = base × 1.35level − 1 × staffing (filled slots ÷ total slots, pro-rata — 5.8 owns this) × (1 + 0.10 per realm law of a matching school + 0.05 per matching crown law) × 1.1 in a realm of that school × (1 + worker bonuses) × stability band bonus × (1 − 0.10 per fault in the realm)

Base: Star Well 80 Starlight, Aether Spring 60 Aether, Garden 40 Essence. Each building holds 12 hours of output until collected.

Daily production of an engaged player, before heist loot. Days 31 to 301 are the economy model's re-run under 5.8's rules: Starlight as measured, Aether and Essence at each row's earlier ratio to it (the Aether column predates the Spring's cut at level 5, 10.2, and the full re-run replaces it, 32.9), which the shared production formula fixes. Days 2 and 8 are unchanged, because the re-run holds the first month. The table is regenerated whole, never edited by hand (14.2).

9.4 Storage, exposure and theft

One-way theft (changed in version 0.8)
By default a raider's haul is created by the game from a snapshot of the target, and the defender loses nothing at all. Defenders still earn rewards for foiling raids. Players who want real stakes opt into the Open Vault league, where the rules below apply in full and rewards are 40% higher on both sides. The reasoning is in 27.5.

The Vault holds most Starlight and Essence. Its sealed share (40% at Vault level 1, rising to 71.5% at Vault level 10) can never be stolen.

Storehouses in outer realms add capacity and are fully exposed, so where you place them decides what raiders pass on the way in.

What a heist pays is computed once, in 9.6, and the exposed amount here moves it across a deliberately wide band, so scouting changes the decision. The percentages this paragraph used to carry are gone: they described a world where the loot left the defender, and it does not.

No balance target. What a heist brings, as a share of a day's production, is generated by the economy model and published with its tables, like every other figure there; it is never set. No shipped base-builder publishes its raiding-versus-production income split — not Supercell, not any disclosed economy talk — and practitioner accounts contradict each other, so a share target would have no external anchor. What governs instead is days-to-upgrade and the raid-worth check (9.6): an average attempt pays at least what its crew would have produced staying home, which the economy model computes and the checks bind.

Entry fee: 2% of the raider's Starlight, from 100 to 25,000, returned in full after a clean heist.

Defence rewards: a foiled heist gives the defender the entry fee plus 5% of what the crew was carrying, a share that rises by 13.1's rule.

| Terrain

| Restoring a law
