| Tier II, tier III
| 20 pages and 2,000 Aether; 40 pages and 8,000 Aether

| Weaving
| 40 Aether per point of stability cost

| Upkeep
| 25% of hourly Aether production at a full stability budget, pro-rata below it, paused after 24 hours away (5.6); the share is set by the sink rule below this table

| Lab experiment
| 400 Aether × Lab level

| Scouting
| 20 Aether per realm, 10% cheaper per Observatory level above 1

| Refreshing targets
| 50 Aether, doubling with each refresh in the same hour

| Raider casts
| 30 Aether per point of the law's base cost

| A new ruleborn
| 100 × 1.3265ruleborn owned Essence, where owned is n − 1 for the nth birth (about 1,270 for the 10th, 21,500 for the 20th, 362,000 for the 30th). The growth rate is tuned so an engaged player's 38th character arrives on the day 14.3 puts every building at its top level, day 378 today

| Character levels
| 150 × 1.55level − 2 Essence per level, up to level 10

| Waking a tired character
| 2 Essence per character level per minute of rest left; a convenience, not a sink, because at a full roster it never falls due

| The roster's keep
| 25% of Essence production, taken as it is collected and never from stock, so no roster ever starves; the share is set by the sink rule below this table

| Sage levels
| 15 × 1.13level − 1 Insight, up to level 40

| Heist entry fee
| 2% of your Starlight, 100 to 25,000

| Cosmetics
| Starglass only

What soaks up the rest (changed in version 3.3)
A faucet runs every day and almost every price in this list is paid once, so totalled over an account to day 301 the older prices absorbed about a fifth of the Aether and a tenth of the Essence the islands made. The criterion is now written down: from day 2 to day 301, what Aether and Essence produce over an account's life does not exceed what every price here can absorb. Three steps meet it, in this order, each sized by the economy model to the criterion. Upkeep keeps its proportional form as Aether's recurring sink, at the smallest share of production that meets the criterion, searched upward from a quarter at a full stability budget. The roster's keep is Essence's recurring sink, and it ships whatever the model finds, because a sink that outlives the one-time prices is required once the roster is full and every character is level 10: a share of Essence production, taken from production and never from stock, so no roster ever starves and nothing is wasted (1.2). It too is the smallest share that meets the criterion, searched upward from a quarter. Neither share goes above one half, so a building always keeps at least half of what it shows. If either criterion still fails at one half, the Aether Spring and Garden ladders in 10.2 are cut from the top, starting above level 5 and level 3 respectively, one level at a time until it holds. That always terminates, and it deletes only Starlight prices, whose faucet is already outrun. Both shares ship at a quarter until the model's re-run sets them (32).

Births are tuned after the sinks. The growth rate in the birth row is the one at which an engaged player's 38th character arrives on the day every building reaches its top level (14.3). No shipped game publishes an intended time to a full roster, so that horizon is the default. At 1.15 the roster filled by day 31 and the price could not bind; 1.3265 is the rate that 9.3's production gives, shipped to four places because 1.33 would move the 38th birth to about day 393, with the keep at a quarter and the Essence that raids bring (9.6), until the re-run sets it. If the regenerated tables move that day, the rate is re-tuned to the new one.

Watched from the first soft-launch day: a volume-weighted price index, with its mean daily change and its coefficient of variation. If the mean daily change stays positive for fourteen consecutive days, both shares rise by a quarter of their value, never past one half, and again each fortnight it holds. Veteran wealth concentration is reported but is not a trigger, because no trusted threshold for it exists. A transaction tax, the sink the literature studies most, is unavailable here: there is no trade to tax.

9.6 What a raid pays

The number at the centre of the economy, and until now undefined. Because theft is one-way, a haul is minted rather than taken — which makes it a pure faucet, and makes its size the single dial that sets how fast the whole game inflates.

The decision that shapes everything else
The obvious approach is the one base builders use: pay the raider a percentage of what the defender is holding [223]. That cannot work here. In those games the loot leaves the defender, so the money supply is unchanged; here it is created, so tying the payout to how much people hoard would tie the inflation rate to behaviour we neither see clearly nor control. The haul is therefore minted rather than transferred — but it is still keyed to the target, through the target's Vault level in B(V) and its exposed wealth in richness. That combination is the shipped norm rather than a compromise: Clash of Clans, since the snapshot-base and Magic Shield changes, mints the attacker's loot while the defender loses no loot or trophies, and Boom Beach locks loot at scout time so attacker gain already differs from defender loss — yet in both the amount is a function of the defender's snapshot holdings. Minting and keying to the target are separate decisions, and conflating them is what produced the earlier claim that the payout should follow the raider's own level instead [223].

Haul = B(V) × grade × difficulty × richness × fatigue × fill
Exposed share in bag units = a full five-crew's carry × the richness ratio before clamping

The conversion is what makes one island worth more than another in the bags as well as in the ledger. A full five-crew carries 750 at level 1 (11.1), so a target exactly at its level median exposes 750 in bag units. It scales with carry, so it holds as characters level (12.19). A target poorer than the median runs out of exposed loot before a full crew's bags are full; a richer one fills the bags first. Fill is measured against whichever comes first, so poverty is priced once, by richness, and an emptier Vault is never charged twice (12.19).

| Term
| Value
| What it does

| B(V), the base
| 2,400 × 1.5(V−1)
| V is the target's Vault level, one to ten — not the realm level, which stops at five. 2,400 at Vault level 1, about 12,200 at 5, about 92,300 at 10. The master inflation lever. Changing the growth rate from 1.5 to 1.65 nearly triples the faucet by level 10, so it is locked first and moved rarely. The base, 2,400, is set by the raid-worth check below

| Grade
| Clean 1.0, loud 0.7, partial 0.4, foiled 0
| Supersedes the older outcome multipliers in 12.9

| Difficulty
| Level or above 1.0 to 1.2, one below 0.8, two below 0.5, three or more below 0.25
| Punching up pays a little more; farming the weak pays almost nothing. The bonus is capped so it cannot be gamed

| Richness
| Each currency's exposed amount against its own level median, the two ratios averaged, then clamped between 0.6 and 1.8, with a reduction above 1.5
| A richer island is worth clearly more, so scouting changes the decision and the map stays a decision space. The band is deliberately wide: a narrow one makes every target pay the same, which removes the reason to scout and makes target choice meaningless. The floor means a clean raid on a fully sealed island still pays 0.6 — nobody ever leaves with nothing [224] — and above 1.5 the excess is halved — so 1.8 pays 1.65 — which is the brake that stops the top of the band inflating the faucet, which is how Boom Beach shapes the same trade-off

| Fill
| The share loaded of the smaller of the crew's bags and the target's exposed share in bag units
| What you carry is what you bank (12.19). This is the mechanism behind the Vault phase's central decision, and it does not raise the ceiling: a full crew is fill 1.0

| Fatigue
| 0.85 on each successive raid that day
| Six raids pay about 4.15 times one, so playing more is worth it and playing endlessly is not

What is exposed: everything in storehouses, plus the unsealed part of the Vault, where the sealed share runs 40% at Vault level 1 and rises by 3.5 points a level to 71.5% at Vault level 10 (10.2 owns this).

What it works out to

| Vault level
| Base
| A clean raid
| Average per attempt
| A full day, four raids

| 2
| 3,600
| 3,600
| 440 to 611
| 1,402 to 1,948

| 6
| 18,225
| 18,225
| 2,227 to 3,095
| 7,098 to 9,862

| 10
| 92,264
| 92,264
| 11,275 to 15,666
| 35,932 to 49,928

The average is a band because its driver is: it moves with how the raid is played (12.19). Measured over 1,200 paired seeds on the same islands, an attempt is worth 12% to 17% of a clean same-level haul — 0.1222 for a crew that stops at a fixed 70% fill and 0.1698 for one that reads the guards, the two crews that name the band's edges. So an upgrade costing 25 clean raids takes 185 to 257 attempts, or 46 to 64 days at four a day, the engaged player’s (14.2) — a figure that must carry the fatigue term, because four raids in a day are worth 3.19 raids, not four. The band is re-measured, at 1,200 paired seeds or more, once the harness carries 12.19's target stopping rule and 12.17's peripheral rate, since both move it; the re-measured band replaces this one, the pacing in 14 is recomputed from it, and the golden test is pinned to whichever band is published.

Why this is a band, and why it moved
An earlier version of this section put the figure at 46%, which is the expected grade alone: 62.5% success times a 35/40/25 split of clean, loud and partial gives 0.456. The fill term was then added beside grade in the same formula and the number was never re-derived. Measured over 1,200 paired seeds, expected grade times fill is 0.1222 for a crew stopping at a fixed 70% fill and 0.1698 for one reading guard suspicion, a gap of 0.048 ± 0.011 (t = 4.4) — so the curve was built on a figure 2.7 to 3.7 times too high, and real pacing is that much slower than the sentence promised. The ratio between the two crews, 1.39 times, has no outside benchmark. An earlier measurement over 300 raids gave 0.127 and 0.187, 1.47 times apart, and 300 seeds cannot carry that precision: the per-seed spread of the paired difference is 0.375 against an effect of 0.048, so t = 3 needs about 560 seeds. Every harness figure in this section states its n, every comparison its standard error, and a claim near a boundary needs t ≥ 3. Publishing a distribution as a constant is a named balancing defect, and the convention is to state the band and let the simulation report it. If 46 to 64 days is too slow for early retention, the upgrade cost moves — never the published figure. A check re-derives this sentence from the band above it and from the full-day column of the table above that, so the two cannot drift apart again the way 46% and “nine days” did (31.5). The first version of that check omitted fatigue and therefore certified a wrong sentence — a reminder that a check derived from the same mistaken model as the text it guards will agree with it.

What a raid must be worth

No shipped base-builder publishes how much of a player's income raiding should supply, so no share is set here, and what a heist comes to against a day's production is an output of the economy model, never a target (9.4). What is checked is the choice a player actually makes. A crew on a heist does not work (5.8), and a raid that pays less than staying home is a raid players skip. So: at every Vault level, an average attempt pays at least what the same characters would have produced working over the heist and its rest — 3 minutes away and 45 resting (11.6). The attempt is priced at the band's lower edge, with fatigue averaged over an engaged player's four heists a day (14.1), so the check holds for a player who stops at a fixed fill and not only for one who reads the guards.

Computed from 10.2's producer ladders and build days and 12.5's crew sizes, the old base of 100 failed it at every level: by about 3 times at Vault level 10, and by 24 times at Vault level 2, where a crew of four leaves producers making about 109 an hour a worker. The base is therefore 2,400, the smallest hundred that passes at every level. The 1.5 growth rate does not move, and nor does the band. The economy model re-runs the check with the roster it actually staffs; if an attempt fails at any level, the base rises until it passes, and the pacing is recomputed.

The caps that hold it

Per raid: never more than 1.98 times the base. That is the ceiling the terms actually allow: richness is clamped at 1.8 but the excess above 1.5 is halved, so the most any hoard can pay is 1.65, and the difficulty bonus tops out at 1.2 — 1.65 × 1.2 = 1.98. The clamp is what holds it; no hoard can spike the faucet.

Per day: the fatigue curve, plus a hard ceiling half again above it for players punching up.

Per week: five days' worth, not seven — so missing two days a week costs nothing, and grinding every day gains little.

There is no hard-currency sink, because nothing is sold for power. The caps are the raid faucet's inflation control; the economy's recurring sinks are upkeep and the roster's keep (9.5), and the health signals are the price index there and the weekly growth in what players are holding [225].

What has to be watched
As players get better, more raids come out clean, which raises the faucet without anything changing. If the clean rate climbs from 35% to 60%, payouts rise about 14% on their own. The grade split is monitored and the caps re-tuned rather than the formula rewritten.

10Buildings

Seventeen kinds of building. There are no build timers: building and upgrading are instant, and the pace comes from resources and from the Spire's level, which caps every other building. Every level of every building is listed below with its cost, what it gives, and when an engaged player typically builds it.

10.1 Placement rules

Buildings sit on the realm grid and block movement and sight. Most are 2 by 2 tiles; the Spire and Vault are 3 by 3.

No building may be taller than the Spire's level allows: a building's level can't exceed the Spire's (for five-level buildings, half the Spire's level, rounded up).

Producing buildings need workers: 1 slot at levels 1 to 3, 2 at levels 4 to 6, 3 at levels 7 to 10.

Moving a building is free and instant, like terrain.

10.2 Every level of every building

"Engaged player builds it" is the day an engaged player's first building of that kind reaches the level, from the simulation in 14. "Income hours to afford" is the cost divided by that player's hourly Starlight production at that moment, a measure of how big the purchase feels.

Aether storage is stated here and nowhere else; 9.1 cites it. It is one day of the Aether a universe can make at that Spire level: every Aether Spring the player can hold by then — one a realm, the Heart included, in every realm open at the Keeper level that allows the next Spire level (5.2, 5.8) — at the Spire's level and fully staffed, for 24 hours, rounded up to the next thousand. It is overflow protection, not a sink, so it never binds for a player who plays daily: the Springs hold another 12 hours of their own (9.3), and every Aether price stays payable, since tier II's 2,000 arrives at Spire level 4 and tier III's 8,000 at level 7. The economy model re-checks every level against the engaged player's own day, bonuses included; a level whose day exceeds its cap rises to that day.

Guardian Post

What a post upgrade is for. A Guardian Post's five levels move a realm's hold rate by about 12 points, close to the 15-point width of a hold band (13.2), so the ladder is the dial that tunes a defence within its band. That holds only if each realm's ladder starts in the right place, and a flat start does not: measured from a +10% start, the ladder ran entirely below the band at realm level 1, reached it only at its top at level 2, stayed inside at level 3, and carried levels 4 and 5 out of the top. So the slope is the same everywhere, +10% health and sight a level, and the start is set per realm level: the post's level-1 health and sight at that level, chosen so the midpoint of its five levels sits nearest the centre of that level's band. Every post still starts at level 1, and an upgrade moves a defence within its band rather than out of it.

A Guardian Post's guard by realm level, as a change to the guard's own health and sight. The table above is the ladder from a +10% start. Levels 3 to 5 are computed from the ladders measured at their unchanged ward counts; levels 1 and 2 now hold eight wards, where no full ladder has been measured, so their starts are computed from their measured level-1 holds at eight wards (13.2) and the slope measured at realm level 2 at five wards, 0.315 points of hold per 1%, until the aligned re-run sets all five by the rule above (32).

| Realm level
| Post level 1
| Post level 5
| Hold band (13.2)

| 1
| +6%
| +46%
| 25 to 40%

| 2
| -10%
| +30%
| 30 to 45%

| 3
| +3%
| +43%
| 30 to 45%

| 4
| -18%
| +22%
| 30 to 45%

| 5
| -17%
| +23%
| 30 to 45%

At five wards realm level 2's start had to rise, to about +26%; at eight its level-1 post already holds 37.6% from a +10% start, the centre of its band, so a +10% ladder would end near 44% at post level 3 and 50% at level 5, past the band. Its start falls to -10%, putting its ladder at about 31% to 44%. Realm level 1 holds 27.6% from +10% at eight wards; +6% puts its ladder at about 26% to 39%, inside 25 to 40%. Levels 4 and 5 start below the guard's own health and sight because the other layers there already reach the band; the post's first job at those levels is to station a guard, and its upgrades tune within the band.

10.3 Wards

Every ward as a sensor and an effect. Every ward has health by level, derived with smashing and disarming below, except Crystal Wall, whose health is its effect.

| Ward
| School
| Sensor: kind and radius
| Effect at level 1: what, where, how much, how long
| An upgrade scales
| Resets after
| Seen

| Gravity Well
| Motion
| Triggered: a crew member comes within 3 tiles
| Pulls everyone within 3 tiles to its centre and holds them 2 seconds. No damage.
| The hold
| 10 s
| Hidden until revealed or fired

| Crystal Wall
| Matter
| Passive: no sensor
| A breakable wall (800 health) that blocks a path. It can never close the last route to the Heart.
| The wall's health
| Stays broken
| Always

| Time Snare
| Time
| Triggered: a crew member steps on its tile
| Freezes that character for 2.5 seconds. No damage.
| The freeze
| 12 s
| Hidden until revealed or fired

| Static Coil
| Energy
| Aura: anyone within 2 tiles
| Shocks everyone within 2 tiles every 3 seconds, for 8% of their maximum health a shock.
| The shock's damage
| Always on
| Hidden until revealed or it first shocks

| Thorn Hedge
| Life
| Passive: no sensor
| Blocks a path and hurts anyone pushing through, 5% of their health a second while they are in it.
| The damage
| Regrows only with Bloom
| Always

| Lantern Glare
| Spirit
| Triggered: a crew member comes within 2 tiles
| Shows the crew to every guard in the realm for 5 seconds and blinds them for 2. No damage.
| The reveal
| 20 s
| Hidden until revealed or fired

What an upgrade scales, and what it never does. 10.2's “effect +15%” a level scales the number in the column above and nothing else. It never scales a distance: a ward whose radius grew on upgrade would cover tiles it did not cover when it was placed, and the editor could no longer draw its footprint, so a ward's level-1 footprint is its level-5 footprint. Each level also takes a tenth of the level-1 reset off the reset; 10.2 shows the ten-second case. Two ward levels ship at launch, and levels 3 to 5 follow as content (26.1).

Smashing or disarming. Every ward has 550 health a level, and Breakers deal it double damage (11.1); Crystal Wall's health is its effect instead. A hidden ward whose position is known — from a Lantern Wisp, the Archivist's Reveal or the Cartographer's maps (12.4), or because it has fired — can instead be disarmed. The disarmer stands on the ward's tile for 15 seconds at every ward level, 30% less with the Tinker trait (11.3), and cannot move, hide or use an ability until it finishes; stepping off starts it again. While it disarms, the ward’s sensor ignores that crew member, so every known ward can be disarmed; it still fires on anyone else in reach (brief 37). Disarming makes no noise, but the disarmer is in the open on a known tile for the whole time, in view of any guard whose cone crosses it. Smashing is loud: while it lasts, guards within 4 tiles notice the crew 25% faster, as on loose stones (6.2). A level-1 Breaker deals a ward 80 a hit, one hit every 0.8 seconds (12.20), so it smashes a level-1 ward in about 6 seconds, a level-2 ward in about 11 and a level-3 ward in about 17: smashing is quicker up to ward level 2 and disarming from level 3, or from level 2 with a Tinker's 10.5 seconds. At 500 a level the level-2 ward fell in 10.4 seconds and beat the Tinker, so the coefficient moved to 550, the smallest multiple of 50 at which the crossover lands there. The harness checks it against Breaker damage in play, and if it does not hold, the 550 moves until it does (32). Passive wards have no sensor to disarm: they are broken, or pushed through.

Shape before colour. Each ward reads by its shape and its icon; its school colour is a third cue and never the only one, so a colour-blind raider can tell every ward apart. Six ward types is a deliberate count: within the six or seven kinds of defensive marker the legibility cap allows and pre-production's art-style test checks (13.2, 26.2), not an oversight against larger trap rosters elsewhere in the genre.

A rope bridge can carry a Snap instead of a ward: when triggered, the bridge drops and anyone on it falls back to the realm they came from. Wards of a school are 25% stronger in a realm running a law of the same school.

10.4 The Library

A load-bearing faucet that four sections referenced and no table contained: the seventeenth building type, at the Heart, one per universe. It is the only page source that does not require raiding, which makes it the whole of a non-raider's progression and the reason 13.3 can claim nobody has to build. Three of its ten levels were specified; a curve cannot be priced from three points, so all ten are given, in 10.2's table with every other building, where the build screen and the economy model read them.

Cost: 900 Starlight at level 1, × 1.99 a level to level 5, × 1.61 a level thereafter, rounded to three figures; the curve is a fit within 2% of 10.2’s table, and the table governs

Pages a day: 3 at level 1, + 1.5 a level to level 5, + 1.8 a level thereafter

Both curves are fitted to the three levels the document already stated (1, 5 and 10), which are unchanged

Pages from the Library are drawn from schools the player already has laws in, so it deepens what someone is building rather than handing them new directions. Those come from raiding.

What this settles. It settles the count — 17 building types, not 16 (26.1) — and gives the economy a curve to price. Adding the Library to the page table in 14.4 roughly doubles what an engaged player earns, and the rarity decision that pacing rests on is taken: 18 common, 12 uncommon, 12 rare and 6 deep laws, 1,200 pages to restore all 48 (7.9). 14.4 re-derives the page economy with the Library in it; this section owns the building, and 14.4 owns the pages.

11Characters

Characters are the second scarce thing after stability. Every character is a ruleborn with a school, a role, an ability and two traits, and at any moment is either working, guarding, resting, on a heist or idle.

11.1 Roles

| Role
| Health
| Speed (tiles/s)
| Power
| Carry
| Job in a heist

| 4.5
| 12

| Breaker
| 180
| 3.2
| 40

| Carrier
| 160
| 3.6

| 6

Numbers are level 1 values. When guarding at home, every role fights with its power and health; Wardens and Scouts make the best guards. Speed is walking speed: crouched, a character moves at half of it, and running at double (12.17).

Bag space. A full five-crew carries 750 at level 1, and the Carrier's 300 is 40% of it: the other four together hold half as much again as the Carrier, so no one bag decides the haul (12.19). The Carrier keeps 300 because its bag, the largest, is what sets the ten-second load at 30 a second. If whether the Carrier filled still explains more than half the variance in fill for the competent harness crew, the Carrier's share steps down by 5 points at a time, the other four rising while the Carrier keeps 300, until it no longer does (the measurement ledger, 32).

Breaking together. The Breaker's double damage makes it the fastest at any ward, pylon or Vault, so help has to be worth something for “who cracks” to be a decision (12.6). While two or more characters work the same object, every hit on it deals 25% more: the assist bonus. It does not stack, so a third character adds nothing to it; a helper never makes a break slower; and it is bounded so that two non-Breakers of equal level never break faster than a lone Breaker — at level 1 the strongest pair, a Warden and a Scout, deal 40 a hit with it against the lone Breaker's 80. The 25% ships until the harness sets the size: the smallest, inside that bound, at which the Breaker alone and the Breaker with help are each the best crew's choice in at least a quarter of breaks, found by forbidding each in turn and measuring what the crew loses (restricted play, Jaffe and others, AIIDE 2012). If no size inside the bound gets there, the bonus is removed, 12.6 stops calling who cracks a decision, and the Breaker's speed is simply its role (the measurement ledger, 32).

11.3 Traits

Every ruleborn is born with two traits. They are common (60% of rolls), uncommon (30%) or rare (10%). Traits tied to a law are three times as likely when a character is born in a realm running that law.

| Kind
| Traits

| Body
| Swift: +10% speed. Sturdy: +15% health. Brave: +10% power when outnumbered. Hoarder: +30% carry. Greedy: +50% carry but drops 20% of it when hit. Clumsy: +15% speed, 10% chance to trigger any ward it passes.

| Heist
| Quiet: guards notice it 25% later. Tinker: disarms wards 30% faster. Lucky: 10% chance of a bonus page. Loyal: rests 25% less after a success. Shadow: invisible after standing still 3 seconds.

| Law-linked
| Floaty: an extra leap under Half gravity. Swimmer: ignores Slow rivers. Grounded: unmoved by Current and Inward pull. Sure-footed: doesn't slide on Frictionless. Night-eyed: full sight in the dark. Insulated: immune to Static and Chain lightning. Sun-hardened: immune to Sunburst. Thornproof: immune to Thorns. Clear-headed: immune to Spores and Delay. Echo-touched: returns once at 50% after being knocked out, even without Echoes (the Revival row, 7.3; 12.20). Lantern-born: safe outside lantern light.

| Home
| Homebody: +20% work, -10% power in heists. Sleepy: +30% work, rests 50% longer. Green thumb: +25% in Gardens. Well-digger: +25% in Star Wells. Spring-finder: +25% in Aether Springs. Watchful: +3 sight as a guard. Stubborn: +25% health as a guard. Teacher: births in its realm roll one extra trait to choose from. Steady: its building keeps producing for one hour after it leaves.

11.4 Levels

Essence to reach level n = 150 × 1.55n−2 (150 for level 2, about 5,000 for level 10)

Each level: +8% health and power, +5% carry. Abilities improve at levels 4, 7 and 10 (20% stronger effect each time; the two uses a heist and the 10-second lockout never change, 11.11)

Highest level = 2 × the level of the Hearth the character lives in

11.5 Where characters come from

Births at a Nursery. The kind is drawn from the schools around the Nursery, weighted as 11.9 states: 3 for each law woven in its realm, 2 for the realm's own school and 1 for each crown law. Within a school, roles the player has fewest of are twice as likely. A birth is paid in Essence at a price that rises with every character already owned; 9.5 owns the price.

Strays found on heists. About one heist in eight finds a stray in the target universe; carrying it out adds it to the roster with three traits, one of them rare.

Visitors' favours, the season track and, from version 1.1, constellation events.

Every player starts with five ruleborn: three wake in the first minute, and two strays follow the crew home after the first heist, where a free Hearth waits for them. The roster holds 3 characters for the Spire plus the places in every Hearth, 38 at the most at launch: an allocation budget against the 63 producer slots of a finished universe, never enough to staff everything (5.8, 9.2). The roster has a keep: 25% of Essence production, taken as it is collected and never from stock, so no roster ever starves (9.5). There is no training and no army camp: the crew for a heist is chosen from characters who are idle or can be pulled off work.

11.6 States and tiredness

| State
| Rules

| Working
| Produces in one building. Production starts 5 minutes after assignment, so constant swapping doesn't pay.

| Guarding
| Stationed at a Guardian Post. Guards defend only while on duty at the moment a heist starts.

| On a heist
| Away for the heist's 3 minutes.

| Resting
| After a heist: 45 minutes after a clean escape, 60 after a loud one, 180 if caught. Hearths shorten rest 5% per level. Waking early costs 2 Essence per level per minute left.

| Idle
| Available for anything.

Why this is the core trade-off
A character on a heist isn't working or guarding. Raiding more means earning less at home and defending worse, so a larger roster is the real measure of strength, and every session starts with a small decision about who does what.

11.7 Sages, the heroes

Sages are rare, named characters who bend rules instead of fighting. A player brings one Sage on each heist, and can station another at the Heart as its guardian. Sages are earned through play and never sold. (They were called Keepers in version 0.1; the name now belongs to the player alone.)

| Sage
| Unlocks
| Heist powers
| At home

| The Archivist
| Start
| Suspend: switch off one law in the current realm for 10 s (1 use). Reveal: show every ward in the realm (2 uses). Recall: bring a knocked-out character back at 40% (1 use; the Revival row, 7.3; 12.20).
| Lab experiments 20% faster. On guard: when an alarm sounds, the crew is shown to every guard for 5 s.

| The Tinkerer
| Keeper level 7
| Invert: turn a law into its opposite for 10 s, such as Half gravity into heavy gravity (1 use). Jam: disable a ward for 12 s (2 uses). Plank: lay a 4-tile shortcut over a gap for 15 s (2 uses).
| Buildings cost 10% less. On guard: the first ward the crew disables rearms after 5 s.

| The Gardener
| Keeper level 13
| Vine bridge: join two points up to 6 tiles apart for 20 s (1 use). Renew: heal the crew 30% over 5 s (1 use). Decoy: plant a sapling guards chase for 8 s (2 uses).
| Gardens make 15% more. On guard: hedges and wards in the Heart heal 50% faster.

| The Wanderer
| Keeper level 20
| Copy: take one enemy law and carry it around the crew for the rest of the heist (1 use). Rift: open a two-way rift between two realms already visited for 20 s (1 use). Tailwind: crew moves 40% faster for 6 s (2 uses).
| The Observatory offers one more target. On guard: raiders can't call their skiff to one chosen outer realm for the first 60 s.

After using any power the Sage cools down for 20 seconds before it can use another, shown as the dial on its portrait (21.8). The crew's abilities have no cooldown (12.6), so the Sage's is the one cooldown in a heist and the one timer Sparkwright cuts (11.11); Held breath slows it and the crew's ability lockouts alike (7.4). Sages level from 1 to 40 with Insight. Every fifth level improves one power (an extra use, a longer effect or a shorter cooldown). The Chronicler (Time) and the Lamplighter (Energy) follow after launch.

11.8 Special characters

Visitors

Visitors arrive at the Visitor's Dock and stay 12 hours.

| Visitor
| What they offer

| The Cartographer
| Sells a full scouting map of one target, with ward positions, for Starlight instead of Aether.

| The Collector
| Swaps pages: three of one law for one of another (five for two at Dock level 3).

| The Stray
| Asks a favour, such as crossing a Slow rivers realm twice or discovering a resonance, and joins the roster with three traits when it's done.

Paradox-born

Legendary ruleborn. While a paradox is woven, the Nursery in its realm can birth its paradox-born once. A player can own one of each.

| Paradox-born
| From
| Gift

| The Reverser
| Reversal
| Swaps places with any character or guard in sight (two uses a heist, with the 10-second lockout, 11.11).

| The Twinned
| Twin suns
| Acts twice: every ability fires two times.

| The Unmade
| Unmaking
| Ignores every law, helpful or harmful.

Anomalies

Cosmic bosses fought by a whole constellation from version 1.1 (see 17.3): the Devourer, a drifting black hole; the Hourglass Storm, which scrambles time; and the Wildfire Bloom, where fire and growth run wild.

11.9 How laws shape ruleborn

What hatches: each Nursery weighs the schools around it: 3 for each law woven in its realm, 2 for the realm's own school and 1 for each crown law. A Tidehold Nursery with Slow rivers and Bloom woven hatches Time ruleborn five times in eight and Life ruleborn three times in eight.

Law-linked traits: 30% of births roll a trait tied to one of the realm's laws, such as Swimmer from Slow rivers or Floaty from Half gravity; a tier III law raises that to 40%.

Resistances: many ruleborn resist a law of their own school (a Tidewalker ignores Slow rivers, a Stormhound is immune to Static), which is why crew choice matters against a target's laws.

Movement: laws change how every character moves (7.4), and each body exaggerates it differently: a Featherkin floats furthest under Half gravity, while a Mossbear barely leaves the ground (21.6).

Strays: the surprise strays from heists (14.6) are born under one of the laws the crew crossed.

11.10 Law-shaped forms

Every ruleborn hatches in a form shaped by the laws around its Nursery, so the islands a player designs decide the creatures they collect. Eighteen kinds and three notes make 54 forms. The three notes are the same for every kind: Hush, Spark and Echo, the three most carried by the 24 laws that ship at launch (7.10) — Hush by six of them; Echo, Spark and Weight by five each — so every form can be hatched from laws that ship. Ties go to the note that completes more chords, which puts Echo (five chords) ahead, and then to alphabetical order, which puts Spark ahead of Weight (four chords each).

| Note of the form
| How it looks
| Its small quirk

| Hush
| Midnight colours and soft, silent edges
| Noticed 10% later

| Spark
| Crackling tips and ember flecks
| +5% power

| Echo
| Faint ghost outlines that trail a step behind
| Returns once after a knockout, at N = 20% (Revival, 7.3)

How forms are decided: whichever of the three notes is carried by the most laws in the Nursery's realm, crown laws included, sets the form. When they tie, including when the realm carries none of the three, the odds are shown before hatching.

Collecting by design: to hatch Hush forms, weave Hush laws next to a Nursery. Forms are never sold, and there are no paid hatches.

The Forms album: completing all three forms of one kind unlocks an outfit; completing one note across every kind unlocks a skiff sail.

More forms come as content. Further notes are added for every kind at once through seasons, like laws; a form that has shipped is never withdrawn (32.5).

Trading: from version 1.1, constellation members can swap one ruleborn a week when both agree. No currency is involved.

Balance: quirks are small sidegrades, so a player's favourite form is never wrong.

11.11 The character bible

Players form real attachments to game characters, and those attachments go with enjoyment and time played; a survey of 213 players found seven distinct kinds, from admiring a character's competence to worrying about its wellbeing [124] [125]. Attachment is built from small, specific things — a look, a habit, a way of moving — so every ruleborn is specified rather than left to the artist or the balance sheet. Proportions follow the cuteness rules in 19.19; abilities follow the roles in 11.1. These cards are the one home for every ability: the table in 11.2 is generated from them. Each card gives the ability and the rule-sheet change it makes (7.3), because an ability that cannot be written as a change to a named parameter is the same failure as a law full of special cases (12.21).
