Rules that hold across all eighteen

Each has one habit you can watch at home. Idle behaviour is where attachment is built, not in combat.

Each resists exactly one law family, so crew choice against a scouted island is a real decision (12.4).

No ruleborn is strictly better than another. Levels raise numbers; they never turn a Scout into a Warden.

Each ability does one thing no other ruleborn's does, fits its role in 11.1, and is a change to a named parameter on the rule sheet, written on its card. The ability makes a creature feel capable; its look and personality carry the attachment (19.19), so a balance pass never flattens an ability into a copy of another in the belief that identity lives elsewhere.

Each ability has two uses a heist, with a lockout of 10 seconds between them and no cooldown. At most four abilities are ready at once, and the portrait shows the uses left as pips (12.6, 21.8).

Each has a sound so a player can tell who acted without looking (21.8).

Each takes three forms (11.10), and the form changes the look and one small quirk, never the role.

12Heists

A heist is a run of two and a half to four minutes into another player's universe: get in, open the Vault, get out before the way home closes. Nothing is destroyed for good, and the challenge is reading the laws, not out-muscling them.

12.1 Why raid

| Reward
| Why it matters

| Starlight and Essence
| Minted by 9.6's formula; what they come to as a share of growth is an output of the economy model, never a target (9.4)

| Pages of the laws you crossed
| The reason to raid. Pages restore and upgrade laws, a heist is worth two to three sessions of the Library's trickle, and what you get leans toward the school you raided (7.6)

| Insight
| Levels your Sages

| Renown
| Sets your league, your season rewards and your targets

| Strays
| Rare recruits with three traits

| Constellation progress
| Counts toward shared goals, from version 1.1

12.2 The heist in six steps

1Find

2Scout

3Plan

4Cross

5Vault

6Escape

Steps 1 to 3: no time limitSteps 4 to 6: on the clock, 2.5 to 4 minutes

12.3 Finding a target

The Observatory offers 3 universes (5 at level 5) biased so the median heist is winnable (12.15): within 150 Renown of yours, within 3 Keeper levels, and within 20% of your universe's strength (stability capacity plus the levels of your five best characters).

Refreshing the list costs Aether: 50, doubling with each refresh in the same hour.

Echo universes: when fewer than three real universes fit, echoes count as live candidates: snapshots of real universes from the last 14 days. If the band is still short of three, 12.22's generated fallback targets make up the difference. Robbing an echo takes nothing from its owner; its loot is created by the game. This keeps matchmaking full while the player base is small.

Complexity budget: suggested targets respect the limits on unfamiliar laws in 7.8.

New players can't be raided by other players for their first 72 hours, or until they raid a player themselves.

Trace: within 24 hours of being robbed, a player can target the raider once, with a free scout and no entry fee.

12.4 Scouting

Free information: name, Keeper level, number of realms, Renown and a rough loot estimate. A paid scout (20 Aether per realm, less with Observatory levels) shows each realm's laws, the number and roles of its guards, and how many wards of each type it has, but not where they are. Ward positions come from a Lantern Wisp during the heist, the Archivist's Reveal, or the Cartographer's maps. A scout stays valid for 15 minutes.

12.5 Planning

| Choice
| Rules

| Entry
| Any edge tile of any outer realm (see 5.4), or a fault if the target is overloaded.

| Route
| A path of realms to the Heart and out again. The game proposes the shortest; the player can drag it through other realms.

| Crew
| A crew of 3 to 5: 3 characters, 4 from Keeper level 8, 5 from level 16. Resting characters can't go.

| Sage
| One, from Keeper level 2.

| Casts
| 1 slot, 2 from Keeper level 12. A cast brings one of your own unlocked laws into their universe: it covers a circle of 4 tiles for 10 seconds and costs 30 Aether per point of the law's base cost.

The heist clock, and how Haste speeds it, are set in 5.4.

12.6 Moment to moment

A heist should feel like a string of small, tense decisions: roughly one every 8 to 12 seconds, or 15 to 25 in a run. Each is about timing, routes or greed, never about tapping faster.

What counts as a decision. An event counts only where the crew has at least two viable options, neither dominated by the other, whose outcomes differ materially given the state of the run. Tapping along a forced corridor is an input, not a decision, and the harness counts by this definition (19.27). Each decision should weigh no more than about four interacting things — this guard's cone, the crew's position, the alarm and one teammate — because people reason about at most one relation between four variables at a time (30.4) and working memory holds about four chunks [75]. That bounds how complex one decision is, not how many a run holds: the count is set by depth, and the matchmaker controls depth (12.22). No second pacing measure is kept beside the count, because no reading of one would change anything the design does.

| Phase
| Typical time
| Decisions
| Tension comes from

| Entry
| 0 to 20 s
| Read the realm's law banner; get past the first guard
| The first vision cones

| Crossing
| 20 to 120 s
| Take or skip forks; break a pylon or not; when to use abilities and casts; whether to split
| Guard awareness meters and the realm's alarm

| Vault
| About 30 s
| Who cracks and who helps (the assist bonus, 11.1), who loads, when to stop loading
| Cracking is loud: every hit is heard within 5 m, and every 5 seconds of cracking every guard within the crack’s reach of the Vault, 5 m by the walking path, hears it and starts Investigating it. The reach is drawn as a ring around the Vault while anyone cracks (12.17)

| Escape
| Last 30 to 60 s
| Where to call the skiff; whether to drop loot to run faster
| The realm alarm, which is the pressure device here. It climbs 0 to 6 — once every thirty seconds, once per knocked-out guard discovered and once per bell post rung — and it works through the perception system the player is already reading rather than through a number on its own. Every level adds one new effect through a perception parameter the player already reads (12.17): from level 1 suspicion drains at half its rate, from 2 every noise carries 1 m further except a crouched step on moss, from 3 an investigation lasts twenty seconds instead of ten, from 4 the alarm spreads twice as far, at 5 another Warden arrives at an entrance, and at 6 every guard in the realm starts Searching. The clock turns red for its last 30 seconds, but it is a soft session bound rather than the tension: it exists to bound session length and to force an eventual exit so a stalled raider cannot camp a defender's realm. Klei built the same escalating alarm in Invisible, Inc. expressly to “create movie-like tension and drama”, and rejected a timer because it would not tie together the systems already present — then cut the alarm from 20 to 30 levels down to six, because smaller numbers were “more readable and the levels more memorable”, which is the granularity this design already uses

The crew loads to a target, not to a trigger
The player sets how full they want the crew before it leaves; the crew loads to whatever that target and the remaining clock afford at 30 a second, and leaves with that. It is expressed as a target rather than as a threshold the crew trips over for two reasons. It makes the response to the clock smooth — shortening the clock then cuts the haul rather than killing the crew outright — so the peak above is something a parameter sweep locates instead of a number this document asserts. And it keeps the player responsible for a decision that semi-autonomous creatures execute, which is the pattern that works in every game built this way: Miyamoto on Pikmin holds that “if something is irreversible, you need to figure out a way to prevent undesired things from happening”, Majesty gives the player bounty flags rather than orders, and autobattler players “control the plan, not every swing of the sword”. What this document does not claim is that a target feels more tense than a trigger — no trusted source compares the two, and the case for it is the control model and the smooth response, not a finding about tension.

Abilities are counted in that budget. Using an ability is one of the decisions above, so each ruleborn's ability has two uses a heist, with a lockout of one decision slot (10 seconds) between them, and no cooldown (11.11). A crew of three to five therefore has 6 to 10 ability choices whatever the clock, which leaves at least 5 of the 15 to 25 decisions for routes, pylons, casts and greed, and the count no longer grows with the clock. At most four abilities are ready at once, the working-memory bound (32.4). In a crew of five the rightmost portrait's ability waits, and becomes ready the moment any other ability's use is taken — that ability entering its lockout counts — and an ability whose lockout ends while four are ready waits its turn; an ability with no uses left no longer counts. Held breath applies to the lockout and to the Sage's cooldown (7.4, 11.7). The harness counts every decision in a run, casts included: if the median run leaves the 15 to 25 band, each ability falls to one use, and if the median is still out at one use the excess is not the abilities', so the matchmaker's depth centre (12.22) moves down one realm at a time until the median run is back in band. tools/ability_text.py --self-test pins the rule.

Camera: a fixed top-down view follows the crew. When the crew splits, it follows the group last touched while the other group's portraits show its health and danger. Crossing a link plays a one-second swoop to the next realm. Pinching zooms out to the whole realm, never the whole universe.

Always on screen: the clock, the realm's alarm meter, the vision cones and awareness of guards the crew can see, carried loot with both what the bags can still take and what is left to load (12.19), each crew member's ability uses left, and the Vault's progress.

Pylons: breaking one switches its law off for the rest of the heist and gives a page of it. A pylon shows its law's school colour and that law's own glyph, at the 3:1 contrast accessibility standards set for meaningful graphics, so colour never carries the school alone, and a tag names its law the first time the crew sees it (15.3). The raider knows what a pylon carries before committing to the detour, and the detour's price follows from the pylon's health (10.2): about 1% of a heist at pylon level 1 and 9% at level 10.

The Vault: 1,500 health per Vault level; Breakers deal double damage to it, and two or more characters working it together earn the assist bonus (11.1). Once open, every crew member loads at 30 a second, or 60 while Skyskipper's ability runs (12.19 owns this rate) until the target fill is reached, the bags are full, or the exposed share is gone. A full five-crew load therefore takes about ten seconds, which is one decision at the cadence this section sets. Storehouses on the route open the same way.

Escape: call the skiff to any edge tile. It takes 5 seconds to reach an outer realm and 20 seconds to reach the Heart (5.4). Anyone still inside when the clock ends is caught and drops what it carries. Loot already carried out is always kept, and every law met joins the Codex whatever the outcome.

12.7 Controls

Never more than eight things to tap at once, each at least 9 mm across, which is roughly 48 points, and none in a screen corner (21.8).

| Control
| Tap
| Hold or drag

| Crew portraits (3 to 5)
| Fire the character's ability at the best target, while it has a use left and is ready (12.6)
| Drag onto the world to aim; drag onto a path at a fork to split the crew

| Sage (1)
| Open a small wheel with its three powers
| Drag a power onto the world to aim it

| Cast (1)
| Open a wheel with the player's one or two casts
| Drag to place the circle

| Get out (1)
| Call the skiff to the nearest edge and send everyone to it
| Hold to choose where the skiff comes

While the crew is split, a regroup button takes the place of "Get out". Controls can be mirrored for left-handed play.

12.8 Guards, stealth and fights

Fights must reward cleverness, not only bigger numbers. Guards are the defender's own ruleborn on Guardian Posts, and their role decides how they behave.

| Guard's role
| Behaviour
| Beaten by

| Scout
| Sees 8 tiles and raises the alarm 50% faster, but is weak in a fight
| Tall grass, Stillness, or a quick ambush

| Warden
| Tough and slow; holds chokepoints
| Going around, leaping, or freezing it

| Breaker
| Hits hard but sees only a short way
| Staying out of reach while a Warden holds it

| Carrier
| Fast; chases and follows loot trails
| Splitting, decoys, or dropping a rope bridge behind you

| Mender
| Heals other guards
| Knocking it out first

Logic: rules that let clever beat strong

Sight and awareness: specified once, in 12.17. Three vision zones inside a cone, a suspicion meter that fills by zone, six states. The older single-cone and percent-per-second rules are gone; where any other section still describes perception, 12.17 wins.

Ambush: the first hit on a guard that has not noticed the crew knocks it out, whatever its role or level. The hit is keyed to the guard's own health — it deals exactly what the guard has left — so no change to Power, levels or traits can break it, and a build test asserts it for all twenty-five pairings of attacker and guard role. Triple Power and the half-health floor are gone. The takedown is not free, and its costs are the design. It needs an unseen start: the guard must still be Unaware when the hit lands. It needs an unheard approach: a crouched step carries 1 m (12.17), so on bare ground the last step to a guard's side is heard, and only moss, which carries it 0.75 m at every alarm level (12.17), lets a crew member arrive unheard — unless the crew member is already waiting unseen beside the guard's path and lets the guard come to it. That makes moss a defensive decision: a Keeper who clears the moss behind a post denies the ambush there (6.1). The takedown takes 2 seconds, in which the attacker does nothing else. And it leaves a body: a guard that discovers a knocked-out guard raises the realm alarm by one (12.17). A crew member can drag a body at crouching speed and hide it, and a body is seen exactly as a still crew member lying on its tile would be, so tall grass, a bush, fog or a tunnel hides it. A body raises the alarm once at most: after its first discovery it is handled, and no guard finding it again raises the alarm.

Wind-ups: every guard attack shows a 0.8-second wind-up. A freeze during it (Stillwatch, 11.11) cancels the attack and dazes the guard for a second; the ambush no longer stuns, it knocks out.

Level cap in fights: a level advantage counts for at most 3 levels, so a well-played crew can rob a stronger universe and a careless strong crew can still be caught.

Knockouts: a crew member at zero health is caught unless revived (12.20). A knocked-out guard stays down unless a law brings it back.

12.9 Outcomes

What each outcome multiplies. The haul itself is computed in 9.6; this table no longer carries a second, competing account of it.

| Outcome
| Condition
| Haul
| Renown
| Rest
| Entry fee

| Clean
| No alarm; everyone out with loot
| ×1.0
| ×1.0
| 45 min
| Returned

| Loud
| Alarm raised; everyone out
| ×0.7
| ×0.8
| 60 min
| Returned

| Partial
| Some caught; some loot out
| ×0.4
| ×0.5
| 60 min, 180 for caught
| Half returned

| Foiled
| No loot out
| ×0
| −8, flat
| 180 min
| Paid to defender

Renown for a clean heist = 20 × (1 + (defender's Renown − raider's Renown) ÷ 1,000), kept between 8 and 40, then multiplied by the outcome above. The defender gains or loses the mirror amount. A foiled heist costs the raider a flat 8 rather than a share of a gain that was never made.

12.10 Heists against the game

Drifting ruins: three universes built by the game each day, for pages and Essence but no Renown. They carry the tutorial and give new players safe practice with unfamiliar laws.

Echo trials: weekly puzzle heists with a fixed crew and set laws, scored on style (clean, fast, untouched). They reward Insight and cosmetics.

12.11 Heists with friends

Friendly heists (at launch): rob a friend's island for fun. The friend loses nothing, both see the replay, and the friend earns a page for every raider their guards catch.

Co-op heists (version 1.1), asynchronous: they are turn-based, reusing the deterministic simulation at almost no cost. No real-time version is planned, for the netcode cost 18.10 and 30.8 record. Two or three friends raid one universe together, sharing one clock and splitting the loot evenly. Crews are interdependent: one player's ruleborn can break pylons but can't carry loot, another can carry but not fight, a third can cast but not climb. Cooperation and interdependence each raise enjoyment and trust on their own, and together they add up [67]. Players aged 13 to 15 use preset pings only.

Why: players in tight-knit groups stay the longest [34], and social ties matter most for long-term retention [20].

12.12 Choice, novelty and suspense

Version 0.4 planned ruins that quietly adjusted their difficulty. Three experiments across more than 20,000 play sessions found that difficulty itself did little for motivation, while choice of difficulty, novelty and suspense each raised it [43]. Difficulty is therefore no longer something the game does to the player behind their back.

| Lever
| How every heist uses it

| Choice
| Every ruin offers three openly labelled difficulties with their rewards shown, and a recommendation the player can ignore. Targets are chosen by the player from a list that spans easier and harder universes.

| Novelty
| Every run contains at least one thing the player hasn't seen: an unfamiliar law, a new terrain feature, a harmony, a visiting Sage, a weather mood. The game tracks what each player has met and draws from what they haven't.

| Suspense
| Outcomes stay uncertain until late: the Vault's contents are only revealed when it opens, guards' patrols shift slightly each run, and the last 30 seconds put the clock and the escape in doubt. The patrol shift is drawn once, from the heist's seed, which is fixed when the target is chosen, and applied as the heist starts, so what the crew sees of a patrol during the run is exactly what it will do. A scout shows no positions (12.4); patrols are learned in the heist, as the stealth games this borrows from teach them (brief 38). Nothing moves at random during a run, so a scouted island stays predictable (12.17), and this is the only form of the shift the server can re-simulate (24.10).

What adaptation is still allowed: a safety net, never a dial. After two failures in a row the game offers an easier version, visibly and by name. It never silently makes a chosen difficulty easier or harder.

Heists against players are never adjusted for engagement at all (19.12).

12.13 Why it went that way

How players explain an outcome shapes what they feel and whether they try again [68], so every heist result names the moment that decided it and what could have been done instead.

The decisive moment: "Your Carrier was spotted crossing the open ledge at 0:48" with a three-second replay of it.

One thing to try: a single suggestion tied to that moment, never a list.

Never blame luck, and never take credit for it: if a fault or a flicker decided the run, the result says so plainly.

Never blame the player's spending: no result ever suggests a purchase would have changed the outcome.

In co-op, results are shown for the crew as a whole. No player is ever singled out as the cause of a failure.

12.14 Temporary failure, never perpetual

Failure is part of the pleasure of succeeding, but only while it stays temporary; repeated failure with no way through is what makes players leave [81]. Disengagement follows when a game repeatedly frustrates a player's sense of control or competence and they revise their expectations of it [82].

The three-strike rule: nobody fails the same content three times without the game visibly changing something — an easier version by name, a new route revealed, or a different target suggested.

Say it out loud: any assistance is disclosed. Telling players they are being helped did not harm their experience in a controlled study; equalised performance mattered more than knowing about the help [83].

Never take control away: stun-like effects that remove control frustrate autonomy [84], so no law freezes a crew for more than 2 seconds, and the player can always still call the skiff.

Struggle is designed, defeat is not: a foiled heist still returns the crew, the pages and the Codex entries. The loss is the haul, never the progress.

12.15 Why these targets are offered

Version 1.4 aimed for even matches. Six million matches from 262,000 players say that is the wrong target: churn rose when players met stronger opponents, and being matched with weaker ones reduced churn more than fair matches did [146]. A short-term win rate near 50% is what satisfies [147] — players want to win slightly more than they lose.

The median offered target is winnable at the player's chosen difficulty. Harder targets are always in the list, clearly marked, never forced.

Nothing is hidden. Targets are drawn by Renown band and the player's own choice, and the reason each was offered is printed on the card (19.12).

Ratings are internal. Skill and island difficulty use a Bayesian rating with an explicit uncertainty term; the raw numbers are never shown, because visible ratings invite target-shopping.

A new island starts wide. Its difficulty rating begins uncertain and narrows as it is attacked, so early raiders are not punished for being first.

12.16 Guards say what they are thinking

In two controlled studies of top-down stealth, contextual guard dialogue let a simple AI match a complex one for player enjoyment [148]. Telegraphing buys as much perceived intelligence as expensive behaviour, at a fraction of the cost.

Every guard state change is spoken: "was that the wind?", "someone's been here", "back to the post".

Every attack keeps its 0.8-second wind-up (12.8), so the player always has a window to act.

Near misses are never tuned. They raise arousal and the urge to continue but are experienced negatively, through a wanting-without-liking mechanism linked to problem gambling [149]. They will happen naturally; we do not engineer their frequency (19.12).

12.17 How guards perceive

The most important system in the game, and until now the least specified. Every number is an integer, because the simulation contains no decimals (24.7), and nothing here is random: a player who scouts an island can predict exactly what will happen. That is required anyway, since the server re-simulates the result.

What each guard can see

Vision is not one cone but three zones inside a cone, plus weak peripheral awareness — the model shipped stealth games converge on, because a single cone gives absurd long-range vision or blind close range [215].

| Guard
| Cone
| Near
| Mid
| Far
| Corner of the eye
| Turn

| Scout
| 45°
| 0–2 m
| 2–4 m
| 4–6 m
| 110°
| 6°/tick

| Warden
| 40°
| 0–1.5 m
| 1.5–3 m
| 3–4.5 m
| 90°
| 3°/tick

| Breaker
| 35°
| 0–1.5 m
| 1.5–2.5 m
| 2.5–3.5 m
| 70°
| 8°/tick

| Carrier
| 30°
| 0–1 m
| 1–2 m
| 2–3 m
| 60°
| 4°/tick

| Mender
| 40°
| 0–1.5 m
| 1.5–2.5 m
| none
| 100°
| 4°/tick

A guard standing a level higher sees a metre further; one standing below loses its far zone entirely. The corner of the eye is graded by distance like the cone: outside the cone but inside the angle in the last column, suspicion fills at the rate of the distance zone the crew member is in, halved — 33 a tick at near distance — which is how shipped stealth games grade peripheral vision (Splinter Cell: Blacklist, Shadow Tactics). Being crouched halves how fast you are noticed, running raises it by three quarters, an unlit tile halves it again, and standing still in tall grass makes you invisible until a guard walks into the tile. Each gait has a speed as well as a sound: crouched, a crew member moves at half its role's speed (11.1), and running, at double, so the quiet gait costs time and the fast one costs noise. The simulation harness uses these readings, and the findings measured under its older ones are re-measured on it before any of their numbers enter this document (the measurement ledger, 32).

Being noticed takes time

Each guard carries a suspicion value from 0 to 1000. Inside the near zone it fills in about half a second, the middle zone in about a second, the far zone in 2.8 seconds (12 a tick at 30 ticks a second). It empties more slowly than it fills — about 1.7 seconds — which is what creates the tail after a close call. A state cannot fall back for half a second once reached, so nothing flickers at the edge of a zone.

| State
| At
| What the guard does

| Unaware
| under 200
| Patrols or holds its post

| Suspicious
| 200 to 599
| Stops, turns toward it, stays on route

| Investigating
| 600, or on hearing something
| Walks to the spot and searches nearby for ten seconds, twenty from alarm level 3

| Alerted
| 1000
| Calls out, converges, raises the alarm. Never lethal — the crew is knocked out and sent home

| Searching
| After losing sight
| Twenty seconds of wider, faster searching

| Returning
| After giving up
| Walks home, still easily re-alerted

Alarm spreads by distance, not everywhere at once: guards within four metres start investigating half a second later. The realm alarm itself climbs from 0 to 6 — once every 30 seconds, once per knocked-out guard discovered, each body counting once (12.8), and once each time a bell post rings (6.2). Each level adds one new effect through a parameter this section already has, and keeps the ones below it: the pattern of Invisible, Inc.'s six-step alarm (12.6), rather than one number made stronger. Rising pressure with no dice in it.

| Alarm
| What changes, from this level on

| 1
| Suspicion empties at half its rate, about 3.4 seconds instead of 1.7, so the tail after a close call lengthens

| 2
| Every noise in the table below carries 1 m further, except a crouched step on moss (12.8)

| 3
| An Investigating guard searches for 20 seconds instead of 10

| 4
| The alarm spreads twice as far: guards within 8 m, not 4, start investigating

| 5
| Another Warden arrives at an entrance

| 6
| Every guard in the realm enters Searching

The ladder is fixed. The harness measures what it does to the hold band (13.2) and the decision band (12.6) in the same batch, and the bands absorb it (the measurement ledger, 32).

Noise travels the way a crew walks

Sound follows the walkable path rather than passing through walls, so a wall genuinely muffles direction: a guard turns toward the way around, not toward you [216].

| Action
| Heard within
| Action
| Heard within

| Crawling (no crew gait, 12.18)
| silent
| Landing from one level
| 2 m

| Crouched walk
| 1 m
| Landing from three
| 4 m

| Walking
| 2.5 m
| Breaking something
| 5 m

| Running
| 4.5 m
| Gravel and water are louder; on moss every step carries three quarters as far, a crouched step 0.75 m (6.1)

Guards far off screen hear at half strength, so nothing is lost to something the player could not have seen [215].

Cracking the Vault is heard further than anything in the table. Every hit is heard within 5 m, as breaking anything is, and every five seconds of cracking every guard within the crack’s reach — 5 m by the walking path, drawn as a ring around the Vault while anyone cracks — hears it and starts Investigating it (12.6). A call to the whole Heart was the one threat in this section with no visible extent, and it made the Heart a room to clear before the Vault could be touched (brief 37). The reach ships at 5 m, the per-hit distance, which the harness measured as passing the depth gate; the sweep in 32.9 sets its final size. It is a heard event, not a suspicion increment, because suspicion added below 600 drains before it can move an unaware guard. The Heart is the bound: shipped stealth games bound sustained breaching noise by a radius, and no guard outside the Heart hears it.

12.18 How a crew moves, and what it decides

The document specified what guards see long before it specified how a crew walks. This settles it, because everything about whether delegated control feels like playing rests here.

| Question
| Answer

| Tiles or free movement?
| Both, deliberately. Terrain, laws and building are per tile; position and facing are continuous, in millitiles. That is the only reading under which one-metre tiles and sub-metre vision zones are both true

| How is a move ordered?
| Tap a crew member to select, tap the ground to send. Tap two to move them together. A held tap on a wall, ledge or vent offers the action that fits. A held tap on open ground offers the gait for that move — crouch, walk or run — and a plain tap sends at the last gait chosen. There is no crawl and no gait button, so the eight-control cap holds (12.7)

| Pathfinding
| Breadth-first on the tile graph with a fixed neighbour order, ties broken by tile index. Deterministic by construction (24.10)

| What is recorded
| The command, never the path. A destination and a tick, so the same route is recomputed identically on the server

| Crew avoiding each other
| They pass through one another. Collision between friends is a source of non-determinism and of the exact frustration this rule exists to prevent

The crew never makes a decision the player did not make

No auto-attack, no auto-flee, no automatic re-routing around a guard the player walked them into, no picking a different door. If a crew member is spotted it keeps doing what it was told until told otherwise. A creature cannot look stupid for a decision it never made — and the failure this protects against, delegated control reading as watching rather than playing, is a named risk (30.7).

12.19 The Vault, and when to stop loading

12.6 called the loading phase the heist's central decision, and its own numbers gave that decision two seconds and one option. Both are fixed here.

Every role can load, at 30 units a second rather than only Carriers at 150, and no role loads at any other rate; the one faster rate is an ability's, Skyskipper's 60 a second for 5 s (11.11). A full five-crew's bags hold 750 at level 1 (11.1), and the largest, the Carrier's 300, sets the time: a full crew fills in about ten seconds, which is one decision at the eight-to-twelve second cadence 12.6 sets for itself. The rate scales with Carry as characters level, so ten seconds holds all game.

No one bag decides the haul. The Carrier holds 40% of a five-crew's bags, not the 54% it held in version 3.2, so whether the Carrier filled no longer nearly decides fill; 11.1 carries the rule that steps its share down if it still does. A full load pays exactly what it did; partial loads change shape.

What you carry is what you bank. The haul in 9.6 is multiplied by fill: what the crew carries out, measured against the smaller of its bags and the target's exposed share, both in bag units (9.6 converts one to the other). A poor target is priced once, by richness, and not a second time by bags it could never fill. Loading stops when the target fill is reached, the bags are full or the exposed share is gone, and the heist shows both what the bags can still take and what is left to load (21.8), so the player always sees which will bind first. So loading longer earns more and the alarm clock is the price. Dropping loot to run faster costs exactly the fraction dropped.

The curve rises to about 90% full, and no turn-over is claimed. Measured over 300 raids a point with everything else fixed and only the stopping point varying, expected grade times fill runs 0.102 at 50% fill, 0.111 at 60%, 0.127 at 70%, 0.132 at 80%, 0.165 at 90% and 0.159 at 100%, with a standard error of about 0.02 on any difference between two points (9.6's per-seed spread, 0.375, over 300), so the fall from 90% to 100% is inside it (t about 0.3). An earlier version of this sentence put the break-even at 70%, which is well below the curve's highest measured point and is close to the threshold the one peer-reviewed study of this exact decision names as the rule first-time players intuitively choose — the thing optimal play beats (Quacks of Quedlinburg, IEEE Conference on Games, 2022, where risk-managing heuristics average around 30 victory points and trained agents reach 34 to 35). No trusted source shows any designer deliberately setting a bank-or-push threshold below its optimum for readability, so this document does not claim one.

Cracking is about 8 to 15 seconds of pressure at Vault level 1 with a Breaker in the crew — 15 for a Breaker alone, 8 for a full crew with the assist bonus (11.1) — and each Vault level adds 1,500 health. The choice is who cracks and who helps (11.1); the seconds after it are the wind-up, not the choice. Crews without a Breaker can crack, because nothing in the design forbids it; they are slower — about 26 seconds for four at Vault level 1 without the assist bonus, and about 22 with it.

The clock is a price, and the harness has to show it. The harness crew loads to a target, as a player does (12.6), not to a switch. The clock experiment is run at the full clock, 70% and 55% of it, and passes only if mean fill falls as the clock shortens, with no collapse to zero at 70%; the sweep then reports the break-even as a band, which replaces the curve above. If it fails, the stopping rule becomes “load while the remaining clock exceeds the way home plus one decision slot (10 seconds)”, tested against the same condition. If that fails too, this section stops calling the clock a price: it states the clock as a hard limit, and the curve above is no longer read as a price. The test that pins the wall is re-pinned to whichever holds.

The curve is re-measured, and the new run replaces it. The figures above were measured before the ten-second load at 750 bags, the target stopping rule and the aligned perception rules (12.17), and without the Spire's reach re-sealing the Vault (13.2). The grade × fill curve and the band in 9.6 are re-measured with all four, the reach at 8 units a second, and the numbers here are replaced by that run (the measurement ledger, 32).

Why the pressure here is greed, not caution
Every psychological force this moment recruits points the same way, and the design's own rules are why. The house-money effect (Thaler & Johnson, Management Science, 1990) applies: a crew holding a part-loaded haul holds a prior gain it does not yet treat as its own, and that licenses pushing — and a repeated loop with a rare large vault payoff meets the conditions later work sets on the effect. The endowment effect, which would pull the other way, probably does not fire at all, because a caught crew loses the haul and nothing it owned, and endowment needs ownership the crew never has. And the escape hatch itself raises the stakes: people place larger bets when a post-bet cash-out option exists (Psychological Science, 2024, preregistered), and calling the skiff is exactly such an option. So the loading decision is designed as greed, and the escape as dread — the haul is shown as held from the moment it is loaded (12.6), so the run home trades on loss aversion once the player feels they own it.

12.20 What happens when a guard catches you

| Rule
| Detail

| Attack cadence
| One hit every 0.8 seconds for damage equal to Power, on both sides. This is the only cadence the document states, and it is the reading under which Vault health, role Power and the phase lengths in 12.6 agree

| May a crew fight?
| Yes, at any moment — but hitting a guard that has not been ambushed sends its suspicion straight to 1000. An open fight is an alarm with a price, not a forbidden move

| A crew member at zero health
| Drops its loot on the tile and has ten seconds before capture. An ally standing adjacent revives it in 3 seconds, a Mender in 1.5, and it returns at 30% of its full health: enough to reach the skiff, not enough to stand in a fight. That is the median of the shipped ally revives (Apex Legends 20%, Left 4 Dead 30%, The Finals 50%). Every other way back — a law, an era, a form, the Echo-touched trait, Echoplate or the Archivist's Recall — is a source on the Revival row (7.3) that states only its own share; when more than one applies, the highest applies, once

| Captured
| Sent home, rested longer, and its share of the haul lost. Never killed, never lost permanently (12.14)

12.21 Attributes laws are allowed to read

Two laws read properties no creature had — Density changes what "big" and "small" creatures do, and Heavy ground grounds "flyers". A law that reads a parameter which does not exist is the same failure as a law full of special cases, seen from the other side. Both attributes are now carried by the roster table itself rather than asserted in prose, and both are checked by the build.

Size is a band on every ruleborn — small, standard or large — and it is derived, not assigned: the head count already given on each character card in 11.11 decides it. Under 2.0 heads is small, 2.0 to 2.5 standard, above 2.5 large. That yields 4 small, 10 standard and 4 large, and every large one is a Warden, which is why Density reads as a Warden rule in play. The band is now a column in the roster table in 11.2, so a law can read it, and the build re-runs the derivation: change a character's proportions and its size changes with them, or the check fails (31.4).

Flight is a column in the same table, stated for all 18 rather than implied by one creature's passive line. Three fly: Featherkin, Lantern Wisp and Soulmender. Heavy ground grounds exactly those three, and nothing else in the document has to be read to know it.

Split guards created by Swarm birth inherit the role of the guard they came from, at 30% of its health and power, so perception has something to resolve against.

The rule: every tier effect of every law must be expressible as a change to a named parameter. Anything that cannot be is a design smell to resolve while authoring, not at implementation.

12.22 How a target is chosen

| Step
| Rule

| 1. The band
| Renown within 150, Keeper level within 3, an island difficulty rating within one band of the raider's recent success rate, and depth: the number of realms the route from the landing realm to the Heart crosses. Depth is centred on three realms, where a run carries 15 to 25 decisions (12.6) and every realm level's hold band is reachable (13.2), so one selection meets both. Its tolerance is ±1 realm for the first five seconds of search, ±2 after five and ±3 after ten. Every offered target shows its depth, so a longer run is a choice the player sees coming. Depth here is realms crossed; the count of feasible stealth paths is the generator's own difficulty measure (13.3), not a filter

| 2. The rating
| Skill and island difficulty are Bayesian, with an uncertainty term. A new island enters with its mean from the defence simulator — its hold rate against the reference crews (13.4), converted to the rating scale — and a wide deviation, 350 on Glicko-2's scale, which narrows with every observed attack under Glicko-2's update (volatility 0.06, system constant 0.5). The simulator supplies the prior and live raids the evidence, so no recruited players are needed. This step is the home of those constants. Renown and Keeper level are filters; the rating orders what survives them

| 3. The guarantee
| The list is built so the median offered target is winnable at the player's chosen difficulty: candidates are sorted by predicted success and the middle of the list is drawn from the raider-success band for the target's realm level — 60 to 75% at realm level 1, 55 to 70% from realm level 2 (13.2). A route that crosses realms of different levels takes the band of the lowest. Every island carries its origin, authored or generated, and is judged against that origin's band

| 4. When the band is thin
| If fewer than three live candidates, echoes included (12.3), sit inside the band — three being the smallest list whose middle entry is neither end — fallback targets make up the difference. They are not hand-authored: 13.3's generator makes them under its sign filter, the harness checks each against the band before it is served, and they are regenerated each season so none becomes solved and shared. There are as many as it takes to cover every realm-level-and-band cell the live pool misses. Each is labelled as a generated target at the point of attack, because it is nobody's realm and must not pass as another player's. If the band is empty rather than thin, fallback targets from the nearest band fill it, labelled with the band they come from. Only then do the filters widen, in order — rating, then Keeper level, then Renown — and ruins fill what remains (12.10). The list is never empty and never silently unfair

| 5. The lowest tier
| The 0.25 payout for targets three or more levels below is unreachable through normal matchmaking by design. It exists for challenge links, friendly heists and Trace targets

13Defence

Defence is designed, not built. There are no walls to spam and no towers to level: the defender chooses laws, a map, a few wards and who stands guard.

Four layers. No layer decides an outcome on its own, and the share beside each is the design target, not a measurement: 13.4 measures each share, and the targets are re-derived under the merged layer by the rule in the measurement ledger (32).

| Layer
| Share
| How it defends

| Geometry
| 25%
| Where the Vault sits, which routes exist, what can be seen from where, chutes that force a choice, bridges that drop on alarm. Free, unlimited, and the layer a thoughtful designer beats a richer player with. 25% is the target on an authored island; on a generated one 13.4’s ablation measures it, about 16% of the credit on the shaped generator

| Detection and response
| 35%
| Your own ruleborn on posts, and what acts on what they see: bells, the realm's alarm, the Spire's reach and the guardian Sage's power (13.2). This is what turns being seen into being caught, and it is one layer because neither half turns back a raid without the other. Anyone guarding is someone not raiding — the single decision that links the two halves of the game. Guards who have been home a while are rested and fight better, so the choice is a trade rather than a loss [212]

| Wards
| 20%
| Six to eight per realm, by 5.2's measured budget, placed where the laws make them strongest and where the geometry says a crew must pass

| Laws
| 20%
| The flavour and the puzzle: a Heart running Heavy ground and Haunting makes every loaded carrier slow and easy to follow. One layer among four, not the defence itself

On an authored island, roughly 60% of a defence's strength should come from how it was designed and 40% from what has been built; a generated island is measured rather than targeted, layer by layer (13.4, 32.9), so a clever player at a lower level can still turn back a richer raider. The published design of the base builders this borrows from makes the same point: no single element stops an attack — walls funnel, traps punish the route, and the defenders held in reserve are the hidden win condition [213].

Logic: snapshots
A heist is played against a snapshot of the defender's universe taken when it starts: laws, map, wards, and whoever is on guard. Changes the defender makes during those minutes don't affect it. The defender is notified afterwards and can watch the replay.

Defending pays

A foiled heist gives the defender the raider's entry fee and the mirror of the Renown the raider loses.

If the raider cast a law during a foiled heist, the defender gets one page of it.

Knocked-out raiders leave behind whatever they were carrying.

Rehearsals

Defence is designed, so defenders need a way to test their design. A rehearsal sends one of three simulated crews (cautious, greedy or fast) against the universe as it stands and shows the route it took, where it was seen and what it carried out. Rehearsals are free and unlimited, give no rewards, and run on the same simulation as real heists.

Watching live (after launch)

In version 1.1, a player who is in the game when raided can watch the heist live and fire their guardian Sage's power once.

Why fairness rules exist
A defence that can't be beaten makes raiding pointless, and a heist that can't be stopped makes building pointless. The route rule (there's always a way in), the scouting rules (laws are always visible before you commit) and the loss caps keep both sides worth playing.

13.1 Making sure islands get built at all

The riskiest assumption in the whole design
Ruleborn assumes players will design islands as a defence. Every comparable game says they will not. In asymmetric games the population piles into one role — one game had to offer a 400% reward bonus to fill the less popular side [150] — and in base builders most players copy a layout rather than design one, which is what the enormous copy-a-base ecosystem exists to serve. Creative communities run at roughly 1% creating and 9% editing [73]. If this holds here, the Workshop starves, the human presence that makes heists matter disappears, and the content ecosystem collapses.

| Mitigation
| Detail

| Everyone builds once, briefly
| Before the first heist against a player, a 60-second guided build: place three terrain features, one guard and one law. It is short, it is not optional, and it is the moment most players discover they enjoy it.

| Seeded islands
| The target pool and the ruins are stocked with designed islands from the start, so raiders never run dry while the player base is thin.

| Defenders are paid best
| The largest non-cosmetic rewards in the game go to islands other people choose to play: defence rewards, creator marks, and the fund (18.9).

| Design is a two-minute act, not an afternoon
| Terrain and buildings are instant and free to move (6.1, 10.1), so redesigning is cheap. One-tap layouts exist for players who do not want to design; they are deliberately weaker than a designed island.

| It is measured weekly
| Share of active players who changed their defence in the last 7 days, and who published a defence of their own in their first week (not a Workshop island, whose gate is 25.1's 1.5%). Below 15%, the defence reward's share of what a foiled crew was carrying (9.4) rises by 5 points a season, at most three times, from 5% to 20%; nothing further is tried, because every player already has a generated defence (13.3). This measures how many players author a defence, not whether the game has islands.

13.2 What a fair defence looks like

Because theft is one-way and a defender loses nothing, this game can afford a far higher rate of successful defence than a base builder where losing hurts — and it should, because that is what makes designing an island worth doing.

| Target
| Value
| Why

| Raids that succeed
| 55 to 70%; 60 to 75% at realm level 1
| Most raids should pay, or raiding dies. Three in ten to four and a half in ten defences holding is what gives defenders something to be proud of. It mirrors the hold band, so it ramps with it: 55 to 70% starts at realm level 2, the first level whose generated islands reach 30 to 45% (below)

| Defences that hold
| 30 to 45%; 25 to 40% for generated islands at realm level 1
| Below a quarter, defenders feel helpless and stop designing. Above half, raiding feels futile. Authored islands are held to 30 to 45% at every realm level; a generated island is held to the band its realm level can reach (below)

| From design rather than level
| About 60%
| A well-built island beats a richer one often enough to matter

| Any single layer
| Decides no outcome alone
| If one layer decides outcomes, the others are decoration. No trusted source gives a numeric ceiling on one system's share, so this is a power budget rather than a percentage: 13.4's slot limits enforce it and its measured shares show it

Hold bands by realm level. An authored island is held to 30 to 45% at every realm level, and the Workshop checks it against that band before it is published. A generated island is held to the band its realm level can reach within 5.2's budget. A realm level keeps 30 to 45% if its best setting within eight wards reaches it. Otherwise the band keeps its 15-point width and its lower edge moves down 5 points at a time until the measured hold sits inside with at least one standard error to spare. Realm level 2's guard posts follow 5.2's rule, and if neither count reaches 30 to 45% the same rule moves its band. On current numbers only realm level 1 moves, to 25 to 40%. Every island is tagged with its origin, so the matchmaker holds it to the right band (12.22).

Generated islands by realm level, at one geometry band and 5.2's wards and posts. Holds are measured against the competent reference crew across three realms, before the peripheral-vision alignment (12.17), and realm level 4 was measured at two guard posts rather than 5.2's three. The aligned re-run, at 800 raids or more a level, re-sets every band by the rule above (32).

| Realm level
| Wards
| Guard posts
| Measured hold
| Generated hold band
| Raids that succeed

| 1
| 8
| 1
| 27.6% ± 1.6
| 25 to 40%
| 60 to 75%

| 2
| 8
| 1
| 37.6% ± 2.2
| 30 to 45%
| 55 to 70%

| 3
| 6
| 2
| 34.4% ± 2.1
| 30 to 45%
| 55 to 70%

| 4
| 7
| 3
| 40.4% ± 2.2
| 30 to 45%
| 55 to 70%

| 5
| 8
| 3
| 39.8% ± 2.2
| 30 to 45%
| 55 to 70%

What a low band protects. Shipped base builders make their lowest tiers deliberately weak and protect new players with shields and matchmaking rather than with a defensible base, because a raided player there loses something. Here the defender loses nothing, so what is left to protect is a feeling: a new player watching their realm fall and concluding their defence is pointless. The answer is not a hold rate the realm level cannot deliver but showing the defender what the defence did. After every raid the defender's report shows how much of the haul the defence denied, how long it held the crew up, where the crew was seen and which wards fired, not only whether it held.

Two bands that must meet. The hold band and 12.6's decision band of 15 to 25 are set in different places, and every ward adds decisions as well as hold, so both are spent from one budget. A build-blocking check (31.5) asserts at every realm level that one setting inside the ward budget meets both. If it fails at a realm level, that level's hold band moves by the rule above, taking the best setting inside the decision band.

Eight wards is a legibility cap. A raider has to count and tell apart what stands in a realm at a glance, on a phone, against the clock. Reliable at-a-glance counting holds below four objects, visual working memory holds about four, and colour-blind-safe palettes stop near eight hues, so eight is the most the design allows. Pre-production's art-style test (26.2) checks it on target devices, by the developer and under simulation of the three common forms of colour blindness, together with six or seven distinct kinds of defensive marker. If eight wards do not read, the cap drops by one at a time until they do, and the bands move by the rule above.

Detection and response is one layer. Guards see, and the response acts on what they see: bells and the realm's alarm (12.6); the guardian Sage's on-guard power (11.7); and the Spire's reach, which re-seals the Vault while it is being robbed, 8 units a second from the moment it opens, taking back what no bag holds yet. A raid it seals completely still pays 0.6, the floor 9.6 sets, because nobody leaves with nothing. Neither half turns back a raid without the other: in every one of the 32 coalitions the five-layer measurement ran before the two merged, guards without response and response without guards each turned back none. Giving response effects that work with no guard present — the reach and the Sage's power, each of which costs a raider part of the haul on its own — left both shares where they were, because a re-seal needs a crew already slowed and a slow needs a crew already seen. A layer that contributes nothing without another is one layer, not a coalition of two, so the two are one row with one share. Dividing the credit inside a declared pair first, the Owen value, is rejected for that reason, and Banzhaf's index because its shares do not sum to the whole. An inseparable pair is not a fault: tower-defence designers state it as a goal.

The health signal is the shape of the outcomes, not the average. A game where raids either sweep or fail completely, with nothing in between, is a polarised one even if the mean looks right; the developers of the largest base builder treat exactly that compression as the warning sign [213]. We watch the spread of clean runs, partial hauls and foiled raids across the population, and treat a populated middle as the goal there.

The populated middle is a population signal, not a test one crew must pass
Supercell's Clash of Clans team watches exactly this and states the reason: when three-star and one-star rates grew “unusually close”, that compression was “a clear sign of a polarized meta, where attacks tend to be either near perfect or near total failures with little middle ground”. It took them two balance updates to widen it, and the first did not work. But their unit of analysis is the whole population and their concern is strategic diversity.

Applied to a single crew the signal inverts. Measured over 300 raids on identical seeds, a crew following a fixed stopping threshold puts 70% of its runs in the middle bands; a crew reading guard suspicion and vision cones puts 16% there, with 58% under a fifth full and 26% above four fifths. The better player has the distribution this document would have called unhealthy, and sweeping the stopping threshold only moves the polarisation with it. That is rational risk-selection using information the interface already shows — abandoning bad runs, pressing good ones — and it is what mastery looks like. Supercell's own position is that “balance is not about making every strategy equal” but about multiple viable paths and skill continuing to matter, and Riot's published framework balances four separate skill tiers rather than one. So the middle is watched across players, and the spread between players is published as a band (9.6) rather than averaged away. Capping the top is rejected: it would punish the skilled crew for the shape of the distribution instead of changing it.

Comparable games are far more attacker-sided than this — one runs at around 98% successful attacks [214] — but they are punishing games, and this one is not.

13.3 Nobody has to build

The riskiest assumption in this design was that players would author islands. It was to be tested on twenty people. Since that test cannot be run, the assumption is removed instead of measured.

| Rule
| What it means

| Every player has a defence from the first minute
| Generated from their realm and their ruleborn, weaving only those of their laws that help the defender, and built to hold within its realm level's generated band (13.2) — legal, coherent, never empty, and effective. Nobody is ever a blank island in someone else's target list

| Curation is the default creative act
| Swap a law, move a post, pick a different generated layout, name it. Editing is a far commoner behaviour than authoring, and it is where most players will live [73]. Asking people whether they would build was never going to settle it: across thousands of participants the gap between intending and doing runs near 46% [218], and shifting an intention moves behaviour only half as far [219] [73]

| Authoring is the deep end, not the entry
| The full builder is there for anyone who wants it, and the creator ladder in 18.7 rewards what other people choose to play. It is a wing of the game, not its foundation — and the ladder rests on recognition, the lever with a measured effect on whether anyone contributes [220]

| The Workshop is stocked either way
| Generated islands, seeded designs and published ones share one pool. A raider never runs dry, whatever share of players build

What the generator optimises for

“Generated from their laws” could mean three things, and two of them are worse than nothing. Laws apply to everyone in a realm (7.3), and most of those a rule sheet can express help the raider or cut both ways. A designer can build around a symmetric rule so that it favours the defence; a generator cannot, so every such law it weaves hands the advantage to the raider. The generator therefore reads the sign of each law and weaves only the ones that help the defender, and it optimises for one thing: holding inside its realm level's generated band (13.2). That band is a stated requirement on the generator, measured on every build at 800 raids or more a realm level (13.4), as the Workshop checks an authored island against the authored band before it is published.

Cover stays off the route. Concealment — tall grass, fog, anything that hides a raider (12.17) — is never placed on the shortest route from a landing edge to the Heart, where it would be cover handed to the attacker. It may sit beside the route as bait only where it funnels a crew into a ward.

The goal is far from every edge. The Vault, and in an outer realm the way on toward the Heart, is placed as far from every landing edge as the grid allows.

Guards and wards cover the shortest routes. They are placed by visibility decomposition over the shortest routes, the placement method with a known generative solution, and in the numbers 5.2 allows.

Never seal the route. This is an integrity constraint on every generated island, not a preference: no choice of placement, stream, loop or wall may leave the landing edge without a walkable route to the Heart (6.1). The eight authored laws whose sheets call for a choice (7.10) are choice rules under it.

Difficulty is counted, not guessed. A layout is validated by counting its feasible stealth paths: fewer is harder. That count is the generator's difficulty measure; the matchmaker's depth is realms crossed (12.22).

Expressive range is checked. Every build plots its batch of generated islands by that count and by hold rate, so a rule that shifts crossing difficulty — the orientation rule for the eight choice laws in 7.10 among them — shows as a shift across the batch rather than on one island.

One geometry band. With none, wards barely move the defence, because a ward is worth almost nothing without geometry to hide it behind; with two, the wards the hold band needs push a run past 12.6's 25 decisions.

Generated geometry is the weaker population, and that is why the creator ladder exists. Placing guards and wards on given geometry is a solved problem; composing geometry with intent is not. Against the same crew, geometry a designer lays out is worth several times what the generator's is worth, so the generator is not claimed to equal a designer. Authored islands are the stronger population, held to the authored band, and the creator ladder (18.7) rewards the players who make them.

Why this is not a retreat. Every other system survives untouched: laws still define an island, defences still hold or fall, the ladder still exists, the ruins still fill. What changes is where the first draft comes from — and a game that works when 1% author is a better game than one that needs 30% to.

If soft launch shows people authoring more than expected, nothing has to change: the builder is already there, and the ladder already rewards it. The design is now robust to the answer rather than dependent on it.

13.4 Budgets, and how a layer's share is measured

13.2 sets targets for how much each layer should contribute. Targets without a mechanism are decoration, so here is both: what a defender may spend, and how the shares are actually measured.

What an island may hold

A universe has one stability pool (5.5), and each layer has a slot limit by realm level as well as a price. The limits matter more than the pool: they are what makes it impossible to pour everything into one layer, which is how 13.2's power budget — no layer decides outcomes alone — is enforced rather than merely hoped for [226].

One set of limits, drawn against the universe's stability pool (5.5), shown at three of the five realm levels. The older per-realm pool is gone. 5.2 is the home of every column, and a build check compares the two tables column by column (31.5), so an edit to either is checked against the other.

| Realm level
| Guardian Posts
| Wards
| Law slots
| Prices, in stability

| 1
| 1
| 8
| 1
| Laws 10 to 26 by rank (7.4); wards and posts are built with Starlight (10.2) and hold stability only while staffed

| 3
| 2
| 6
| 2

| 5
| 3
| 8
| 3

One ruleborn stands at one Guardian Post. That was never stated anywhere, and it is the missing conversion between counting posts and counting guards — without it the two tables could not be reconciled and defence could have consumed more characters than production. Wards, posts and law slots follow 5.2: 8, 8, 6, 7 and 8 wards, 1, 1, 2, 3 and 3 posts and 1, 1, 2, 2 and 3 law slots across realm levels 1 to 5, the wards and posts set against the raiding crew rather than by formula (13.2).

Geometry costs nothing and is unlimited, which is deliberate: it is the layer a thoughtful designer beats a richer one with. The response half of detection and response is earned rather than bought: the Spire's reach comes with the Spire, and a guardian Sage with play (11.7).

What "a layer's share" actually means

It means this, exactly: run the same thousand raids against the island with that layer switched off, and see how much less of the haul the defence denies. The measure is expected haul denied — what the raids would have paid against no defence, less what they paid, through 9.6's grade and fill — because three of the four layers work by delay, and a delay that costs the raider loot without ending the raid is invisible to the hold rate. The hold rate is its special case, where grade × fill is zero. Do it for every combination of the four layers — there are only sixteen — and divide the credit the way cooperative game theory does, which is the standard method for attributing an outcome to the parts that produced it and is already used to attribute win rates in live games [227].

| Question
| How it is answered

| How much does each layer contribute?
| All 16 combinations, at least 1,000 simulated raids each — 16,000 runs or more, which a deterministic simulation does in seconds. That puts each share within about a point and a half, and the standard error is printed beside every share [228]

| Is design beating progression?
| Split each layer's levers into those any player can use — placement, choice, pattern — and those unlocked by level, then attribute across the two groups. The target is 60% from design (13.2)

| Has a change helped?
| About 1,600 raids per version to detect a five-point shift in hold rate with confidence. Anything smaller is noise

| What is held constant?
| A frozen set of attacking crews, seeds and target levels, versioned like code, so results stay comparable between builds

The targets in 13.2 therefore stop being aspirations and become a test that either passes or fails on every build.

14Progression and pacing

How long everything takes, for three kinds of player. Every timing in this section comes from the economy model (24), which simulates each kind of player day by day using the costs and production rates in 9 and 10.

14.1 Three kinds of player

| Player
| Sessions a day
| Heists a day
| Minutes a day
| Lab experiments a day

| Engaged
| 5
| 4
| About 40
| 3

| Regular
| 3
| 2
| About 20
| 2

| Casual
| 1
| 1
| About 8
| 1

Logic: what the model assumes

Producing buildings hold 12 hours of output, so one session a day collects half of what they make.

Heist success follows the arc in 14.5 (about 80% at first, 60% after a month), and a heist pays what 9.6's formula gives at its published band, with fatigue; what that comes to as a share of a day's production is an output, never an input.

Players save for the next Spire level first, then buy the cheapest available upgrade; producers come before everything else.

Pages and Insight arrive as described in 7.6 and 11.7; births and levels are bought whenever Essence allows, after upkeep and the roster's keep (9.5).

14.2 Keeper levels

Keeper experience comes from successful heists (30 + 6 × Keeper level), foiled heists (10 + 2 × level), spending (1 for every 40 Starlight) and restoring laws (60 + 10 × level). The experience table is set so an engaged player follows a target curve: day ≈ 0.13 × level1.96, which means level 8 in a week, 16 in a month, 28 in three months and 40 in six.

Where these tables come from (changed in version 3.3)
The tables and chart in 14.2 and 14.3, 9.3's production table and the day columns in 10.2 are generated by the economy model under 5.8's rules — one producer of each kind per realm, pro-rata staffing, a crew assigned rather than timed — and replaced whole, never patched cell by cell, because hand-editing is what produced tables describing a universe the rules do not permit. A check asserts that each published table equals the model's output. The run also carries the raid band and its fatigue term (9.6), the base the raid-worth check sets, and the sinks and birth rate in 9.5. 9.3 already carries the re-run's production. The Keeper and milestone tables here ship at their earlier run until the full re-run replaces them (32): the partial re-run put Keeper 5 to 20 within two days of this table, Keeper 28 at day 108 and Keeper 40 at day 275 for an engaged player, Keeper 40 at day 368 for a regular one, and a casual player's finish at about day 1,343. The rows between those points are not filled by hand. Pacing is a band, not a curve: what an attempt pays varies 1.39× with how the raid is played (9.6), so the full re-run publishes every milestone twice, at the bottom and the top of that band, and the sentences quote the band. When the regenerated curve breaks a pacing sentence anywhere in this document, the sentence moves, never the table.
