| Naming and dressing your ruleborn moves from a shop feature to a first-week moment (19.20)

| What looks good is judged easier to use — but the link is largely explained by how easily the thing is understood, not by beauty itself. Controlling for that ease cut the correlation from .79 to .34.
| Foundational studies [116]; experiments on direction [117]; the fluency explanation [118]
| Polish serves legibility first. A screen is finished when it reads instantly, not when it is decorated (21.8)

| Things that are easy to say and read are judged more favourable and less risky.
| Experiments on name pronounceability [119]
| Every name a player must say out loud — the game, the schools, the creatures — is chosen to be easy to pronounce in both launch languages, English and Brazilian Portuguese (19.20)

19.20 Five more rules

| Rule
| What it changes

| Design the peak and the ending, not the average
| Every session gets one designed peak — the Vault opening, a law discovered, a friend's island cleared — and a deliberate ending: the haul counting up, the creature coming home, one line of what comes next. No session ends on a menu or a defeat screen with nothing after it [111]

| Make them yours in the first week
| Naming a ruleborn is offered the first time one hatches, and a free outfit comes with it. Customisation is introduced before it is ever sold [113]

| Build the faces to the cuteness rules
| Head-to-body proportion, eye size and eye height are specified, not left to the artist's taste, and every new creature is checked against them [108]

| Legibility is the polish budget
| When a screen can be made prettier or clearer, it is made clearer. Beauty pays off mainly through ease of understanding [118]

| Say every name out loud
| Names that are hard to pronounce make a thing feel riskier and less likeable, so the final naming pass tests each candidate aloud, read by a native speaker, in both launch languages, English and Brazilian Portuguese [119]

19.21 Decisions re-checked against the grade of their evidence

Every decision in this document was traced back to the sources behind it and re-read against the grading in 28.1. Decisions resting only on B or C sources are listed here, with what would change them.

| Decision
| What it rests on
| Verdict

| Portrait by default
| One A source on how phones are held [103]; the rest is market pattern and platform documentation, all grade C [104] [105] [106]
| Held, but marked. No experiment says portrait sells better. This is the weakest major decision in the document, which is why landscape ships alongside it. It is not tested, since no result would change the default (19.17)

| The Island Workshop
| An A source on valuing what you build [49]; a C source on games with player-made content retaining better, which is a correlation [39]; participation rates from a heuristic and one observational study [73] [74]
| Held. The targets are set at the low end (1.5% publishing), so the feature does not depend on the optimistic reading

| Cosmetics and a season pass only
| An A source against paid randomness [16]; the revenue comparison itself is grade C [89] [90]
| Held on ethics, not on economics. The business case stays an assumption, and soft launch tests it: global launch goes ahead only if 22.2's launch gates hold after two rounds of tuning (22.2)

| One-way theft
| A-grade work on need frustration and attribution [82] [68]; a disputed A source on loss aversion [15]. No experiment has tested one-way theft in a live game
| Held. The Open Vault league gives the edge to players who opt in, so one-way theft stays the default whatever the two leagues show; soft launch reports both and nothing waits on the comparison (27.5)

| Two languages at launch
| Market data only, grade C. No launch in the right reference class shipped twelve, and neither did Supercell's: Clash Royale launched in English only
| Reduced to English and Brazilian Portuguese, and a test. Store listings are localised more widely first, and the game follows the demand they measure, one locale a season (26.4)

| The mega-hit gates
| Benchmark data from thousands of games, grade C [35]
| Held as targets, not as science. They are thresholds for a decision, not claims about players

| Cuteness proportions
| A-grade experiments, but measured on infant faces and on judgements, not on players of a game [108] [109] [110]
| Held, with the gap stated. Directness is the weak link, so creature designs are A/B tested in soft launch rather than assumed

| No login rewards
| A-grade work on expected rewards [3] and need frustration [82], against near-universal industry practice [88]
| Held. This is where the evidence and the industry disagree, and we are following the evidence knowingly

19.22 Rules for citing anything in future versions

No claim about players from a grade-C source. Industry data sets market shape, prices and benchmarks. It never establishes an effect.

Every effect gets discounted before it is used for planning: published effects replicate at roughly half their original size, and meta-analytic estimates can run about three times high [120] [121] [122].

Directness is checked out loud: who was measured, doing what, on which outcome, and how far that is from our players.

A citation must be able to lose. If no finding could change the decision, the citation is decoration and is removed.

Contradictions are printed, not hidden (19.8, 27.5).

Our own data outranks all of it once the slice and soft launch produce it.

19.23 Evidence behind the expanded content

| Finding
| Evidence
| What it changed

| Attachment to a game character correlates with enjoyment and time played, and can be measured on a 17-item scale.
| Scale development and validation [124]
| Character attachment becomes a measured outcome at soft launch, not a hope

| Attachment is not one thing: players describe seven kinds, including admiring competence, enjoying a character's company, and worrying about its wellbeing.
| Qualitative survey of 213 players [125]
| Every ruleborn gets a habit you can watch, a personality line and a vulnerability, so more than one kind of attachment is available (11.11)

| Identification with a character rises with customisation and predicts autonomy, immersion, effort and enjoyment.
| Experiment [113]; controlled study [114]
| Naming and dressing in week one (19.20), and forms tied to the islands you build (11.10)

| Curiosity is the strongest predictor of both enjoyment and time played in moment-to-moment feedback.
| Experiment [62]
| Every law sheet now includes a weakness and a defender use, so laws stay worth investigating after they are unlocked (7.7)

| Depth is limited by what interacts at once, not by how much exists.
| Working-memory and cognitive-load work [75] [77] [78]
| The content can be this large precisely because the four-rule cap governs any one heist (7.8)

19.24 Evidence behind the endgame

| Finding
| Evidence
| What it decided

| People seek entertainment both for pleasure and for meaning, and the meaningful kind is a distinct experience involving reflection, being moved, and a sense that something mattered.
| Motivation research [126]; scoping review in games [127]
| The endgame is built around a place, a reputation and a body of work rather than a final unlock (14.9)

| Specific, difficult goals beat vague or easy ones across roughly a thousand studies, provided the person is committed and gets feedback.
| 35-year synthesis [128]
| Six named endgame goals with visible tracks, instead of an open grind (14.9)

| Achievement predicts retention early; social ties predict it at the top.
| 51,104 players [20]
| The economy stops being the point at month five; reputation and creation take over

| Perceived value of time predicts wellbeing; hours do not.
| [5] [6]
| "Was this worth your time?" remains the measure at every stage, including the endgame

19.25 Evidence review of the endgame, and what it changed

| Finding
| Evidence
| What changed

| Appreciation is measurably distinct from fun, but no study shows it predicts continued play, and its link to wellbeing is negligible in longitudinal data.
| Scale development [129]; scoping review [127]; two-wave panel of 2,561 people [130]
| The endgame thesis is labelled a hypothesis with a test attached (14.9, 14.10)

| Poorly met needs predict more hours with less enjoyment and more tension afterwards; harmonious and obsessive players differ in harm at the same play time, and obsessive players keep going while exhausted.
| [2]; passion research [132] [133]; 597 long-term players [134]
| Passion is measured, and playtime-up-enjoyment-down becomes a kill criterion (14.10)

| Practice explains a minority of skill: about 26% of variance in games, and roughly 5% when measured from logs rather than recall.
| Meta-analysis [141]
| Mastery goals are about skill expression and self-referenced progress, never accumulated hours (14.9)

| Mastery-approach goals predict intrinsic motivation and lower anxiety; appearance-based performance goals correlate negatively with performance and positively with anxiety.
| Meta-analyses of 243 studies and of induced goals [142]
| Reputation is built on self-referenced mastery and peer recognition; global ranking is secondary and optional (14.9, 17.7)

| Guilds average about 17 members with a median of 9 and dissolve often; commitment rather than membership predicts staying.
| Large-scale guild data [138]; investment model study [135]
| Crews of 4 to 8 inside constellations (17.7)

| People are motivated to expand the self through new activities and identities, and losing an expanded self is disorienting.
| Self-expansion research [136]; serious-leisure framework [143]
| Authored work can never be destroyed by another player: heists take resources and reputation, never creations (9.4, 14.9)

| High monetary incentives crowd out motivation specifically in the most motivated people, and creator earnings concentrate into a tiny elite.
| Experimental economics [144]; platform economic reporting [145]
| Confirms no creator revenue share, and shifts Workshop rewards toward recognition for the long tail (18.7)

| A battle pass had minimal effect on player uptake while spending rose; player themes were grind-versus-spend, elitism and pressure.
| Mixed-methods study of a live game [140]
| Seasons without pressure: nothing expires forever, catch-up built in, no countdowns (18.8)

| Veterans fade over weeks rather than stopping cleanly, and departure can be predicted well in advance.
| Operator analytics, method unpublished [137]
| The signal triggers a legacy card and a graceful off-ramp, never a win-back campaign (14.10)

19.26 The creator payment decision, against the evidence

This is the one place in the document where a decision runs against the direction of its own evidence, so it is recorded plainly rather than dressed up.

| What the evidence says
| What we decided
| How the risk is handled

| Money crowds out motivation in the most motivated people [144], and expected contingent rewards lower later interest [3]
| Pay creators anyway, from a fixed fund
| No promised rate, no live counter, paid in arrears — closer to the unexpected rewards that did not undermine interest in the same meta-analysis [3]

| Creator earnings concentrate into a tiny elite [145]
| Pay the tail, not the elite
| A per-creator cap of 2% of the fund and a minimum payment at 500 completions

| Paid creation can turn a hobby into work
| Accept the risk, measure it
| Output and meaning measured for the top 200 creators before and six months after; the fund is withdrawn if either falls (18.9)

| Payments to minors create legal exposure in a 13+ game
| Payment is 18+ only
| Younger creators keep the full ladder and can claim later; nothing is lost by waiting

Recorded in 27.5 as an assumption we are knowingly making against the grade of the evidence, with a stated failure condition. If it fails, the in-game ladder in 18.7 continues unchanged — which is why that ladder was built first.

19.27 Validating without players

Earlier versions of this section asked for thirty people in a room. That cohort does not exist for this project and waiting for it would stop the work, so the questions stay and the method changes: everything is answered by simulation, by instrumentation, and by the one person who will build it playing it.

What this costs, stated plainly
Simulation can prove a system is legible, balanced and decision-dense. It cannot prove a game is enjoyable — nothing except a person playing can, and that evidence now arrives at soft launch rather than before code. That is a real and accepted loss, and it is the reason the thresholds below are deliberately strict: they are the strongest proxies available, not a substitute for the thing itself. The panels this section used to call for were real and costed [180]; they are dropped for want of people, not for want of value.

| Question
| How it is answered without anyone
| Passes if

| Is the heist tense?
| A harness runs thousands of heists with scripted crews at three skill levels — careless, competent and optimal. Tension is inferred from structure rather than reported: how often a run comes within a second of capture, how many decisions the player faced, how close the clock ran
| A median run at the matched depth (12.22) carries 15 to 25 meaningful decisions, each with at least two viable, non-dominated and consequential options (12.6), and a near-capture in about a third of runs — the frequency at which a close call stays salient, from the inverted-U in the near-miss literature, where a 30% rate produced more persistence than either 15% or 45% (Kassinove & Schare, Psychology of Addictive Behaviors, 2001). The clock is not gated on: measured across four depths and three skill levels the last ten seconds decide nothing, because a median run takes 45 to 150 seconds against a clock of 150 to 195

| Is there depth, or only noise?
| The same harness, comparing skill levels. A game with depth separates them; a game without does not
| The optimal crew succeeds at least 35 points more often than the careless one on the same islands. If skill does not separate outcomes, the systems are decoration

| Does delegated control feel like playing?
| Not asked of strangers. Two checks instead. The rule in 12.18 — the crew never makes a decision the player did not make — is checked directly: every state change in a replay traces to a command. And felt agency is gated through its strongest known driver. The sense-of-agency literature finds predictability the dominant determinant of felt agency, and a statistical-learning model formalises how agency tracks it; Majesty and Pikmin keep agency without direct control because their inputs reliably shape outcomes. So the harness measures the share of heist-outcome variance explained by the player's commands, against what the crew's movement and the island add. That evidence is laboratory work with button presses and tones, not game research, and this proxy is the largest inferential leap in this section
| No unattributed decisions in ten thousand replays, and commands explain a majority of outcome variance across the harness runs

| Is the defence fair?
| The ablation method in 13.4: haul denied, of which the hold rate is the special case, across every combination of layers, at 1,000 or more raids a combination with the standard error beside every share
| Hold rate inside the band 13.2 sets for each realm; every layer inside its power budget in 13.2, because a layer that decides outcomes on its own makes the others decoration; design beating progression 60/40

| Does the economy hold?
| The day-by-day model re-run whenever a number changes (24)
| Pacing within 10% of the published curve, no runaway faucet at any level

| Does it run on a real phone?
| Unchanged: the spike on real devices (24.2). Needs no players, only two handsets
| Bit-identical checksums, and a build that survives store ingestion

The developer is the first player

The instruments that need a person — the eleven-item experience inventory [181], the appreciation and passion scales — move from before code to soft launch, where about one player in seven answers a survey [182] and behaviour is the better witness anyway [183]. Until then the developer plays every build, and is counted as one person rather than a panel. The honest test is whether the person who built it opens it without being paid to. That is weak evidence and is recorded as weak evidence — but it is not nothing, and it arrives every day.

Everything else that once needed a room now waits for soft launch, where the instruments in 14.10 and 25.4 do the asking, on real players, at no cost, and with behaviour rather than opinion. Tension is asked about then, with the three-item scale built for it [221], once there is someone to ask.

The harnesses are on the critical path. Phases 2 and 3 exit on them (26.2), so they are built before the vertical slice, not beside it (26.7).

What stays ungated before soft launch, and what happens if the proxy is wrong

Enjoyment and week-one retention are ungated before soft launch. Nothing before it measures either. That is an accepted consequence of recruiting nobody, not an oversight, and it is written here so it cannot be mistaken for one.

The risk is named accurately. Solo post-mortems name skipping early outside validation as their most common regret — “we are doing the playtest too late”, in the Stagdraft post-mortem's words. That is exactly the risk this plan accepts.

The proxy can be falsified, and its fallback is specified now. The fallback ships if commands explain less than half of outcome variance in the harness or in the browser prototype's sessions, or if soft launch shows the proxy satisfied while players report watching rather than playing: fewer than 70% saying they felt they were playing (30.8). The fallback: the crew never acts between orders. Each member stops where it was sent and waits for the next order, and the proxy is re-run against it. It is the most direct control the eight-control interface allows (12.7), so if the proxy still fails, it stays as the shipped scheme and nothing further is tried; a phase gate waiting on the proxy is then passed by the fallback, not by the number.

The browser prototype stays up. The published browser prototype of the core loop costs nothing to leave running. Nobody is recruited to it and nobody is sent it to play: only play that arrives of its own accord is instrumented, and only with the player's consent, asked before the first heist. Its sessions feed the predictability proxy above.

20Wellbeing, safety and age

20.1 Audience and rating

Core audience: ages 16 to 34, with 25 to 34 at its heart. Players 35 and older are welcome. People aged 25 to 44 make up about half of all players worldwide, and interest in strategy stays stable with age while the appetite for competition falls, so planning and cooperation keep older players [25].

Why complexity is budgeted: across 1.57 million players surveyed since 2015, interest in long-term planning has declined [25]. The complexity budget in 7.8 answers this.

Rating: 13+ on the App Store, chosen on purpose even though the content alone would likely rate 9+; Apple lets developers set a higher rating to match their minimum age [25]. The matching rating is used on Google Play.

Age check: at first launch the game asks the platform for the player's age band (under 13, 13 to 15, 16 to 17, or 18 and over) instead of a birthdate. Players under 13 are told the game is for 13 and up, and no account is created.

Marketing: aimed at ages 18 to 34, with short clips of heists and law combinations. No marketing aimed at people under 18.

20.2 Protections by age band

| Setting
| 13 to 15
| 16 and 17
| 18 and over

| Chat
| Preset phrases and emotes only
| Preset by default; filtered free chat only after an explicit confirmation
| Filtered free chat

| Profile
| Private
| Private by default
| Player's choice

| Notifications at night
| Never, 21:00 to 08:00
| Never, 22:00 to 08:00
| Off 22:00 to 08:00 by default

| Play-time reminder
| Every 60 minutes
| Every 90 minutes by default
| Optional

| Purchases
| Family approval where set up; €25 a month cap
| €50 a month cap
| Monthly spending summary

| Tracking and advertising
| None
| None
| None; Ruleborn has no ads

20.3 Guardrails for everyone

No paid randomness of any kind, and odds shown for every random reward.

No power, speed or skipped waiting for money.

Nothing decays or weakens while a player is away.

No obligations: missing co-op events, donations or anomalies costs nothing.

No grind: no reward needs weeks of repetitive play, and repeatable rewards have daily and weekly caps.

Losses are capped and results show what was kept.

Every session can end with a short summary and a one-tap "Was this worth your time?" rating.

20.4 Moderation

A word filter in every supported language, for chat, player names and constellation names.

Report and block from any name or message; reports reviewed by a person within 24 hours.

Repeat offenders are muted, then banned. Constellation leaders can mute members.

A part-time community moderator from soft launch onward, when there are players to moderate. Before it the beta is played only by the developer (26.2), who reviews any report (26.3).

20.5 Privacy

Collect only what's needed to run the game; no advertising identifiers; analytics are pseudonymous.

Age bands instead of birthdates. The age of digital consent varies across the EU from 13 to 16 (Portugal's is 13), so consent follows the player's country.

Account and data deletion from inside the game.

20.6 Accessibility and languages

School colours always paired with shapes (7.8), and palettes tested for the common forms of colour blindness.

Adjustable text size, left-handed controls, reduced motion, switchable haptics, and no information carried by sound alone.

Languages at launch: English and Brazilian Portuguese. Store listings are localised more widely — Spanish, French, German, Italian, Japanese, Korean and Chinese — as the demand test, and each season the locale whose listing brought the most installs is translated next, one at a time (26.4). The developer owns translation (26.3).

20.7 Creation and sharing by age band

| Feature

20.8 Accessibility in full

Roughly a fifth to a third of players report a disability, depending on the survey, and platform holders now count hundreds of millions of players with disabilities [53]. Accessibility is therefore both right and a serious part of the reach a game of this ambition needs.

| Area
| What Ruleborn does

| Seeing
| Every school has a shape as well as a colour, and every note a symbol; palettes checked by simulation for the three common forms of colour blindness before soft launch; text size adjustable to 200%; a high-contrast mode; no information carried by colour alone; law names always written out, never only as icons.

| Hearing
| No information carried by sound alone; every alarm and cue has a visual and a haptic form; captions for the few spoken lines; separate volume for music, effects and alerts.

| Moving
| No action needs more than one finger; nothing needs a fast tap, a double tap or a drag that can't also be done as two taps; controls can be mirrored for left-handed play and moved; hold times adjustable; the heist can be paused at any time in ruins and rehearsals.

| Understanding
| Plain language throughout (1.4); one new idea per session (15); every rule visible on screen when it matters; a slower mode for ruins that runs the simulation at 75% speed; no reading speed requirements on banners, which stay until dismissed if the player chooses.

| Comfort
| Reduced-motion mode removes swoops and most particles; no flashing above three flashes a second; screen shake can be switched off; haptics adjustable or off.

| Screen readers
| Every menu, card and result works with VoiceOver and TalkBack; the universe map can be read realm by realm.

| Testing
| Nobody is recruited before soft launch (25.4). Until then every feature above is checked against this list by the developer, and every palette by colour-blindness simulation. From soft launch, players with disabilities who opt in are asked in the game, as every soft-launch player is (25.4), and their reports decide what is fixed or added. The accessibility set ships regardless of what they report (30.11).

| Honesty
| The store page lists exactly which accessibility features exist, so nobody buys into a barrier.

20.9 Why minors default to preset chat

No study directly compares preset-phrase chat with filtered free chat for minors — a real gap. What exists is prevalence: one in three boys aged 9 to 12 reported an online sexual interaction in 2024 [151], 40% of minors report being approached by someone they believed was trying to manipulate them [152], and recorded offences of sexual communication with a child rose 89% over six years in one country's police data [153]. The recurring pattern in every safety authority's guidance is that offenders move the conversation off-platform, which free text enables and preset phrases do not.

Everyone under 18 starts on preset chat. 16 and 17 year olds can move to filtered free chat after an explicit confirmation; 13 to 15 cannot.

Known material is caught by hash-matching, which is cheap and reliable; classifiers, which run at 70 to 97% accuracy depending on the task [154], add coverage for new material, with human review behind them.

Reporting is prominent, because even among harmed minors only about half use a reporting feature [152].

Moderation protects new players first. Analysing 2.5 million players and 18.25 million matches, toxic teammates significantly predicted newcomers quitting but had no significant effect on players past level 30 [155]. Moderation effort is spent where it changes outcomes.

20.10 The cheapest safe configuration

Three design decisions remove most of the moderation cost and nearly all of the legal exposure, and they are recorded here as decisions rather than as savings.

| Decision
| What it avoids

| No player-uploaded images, ever. Workshop islands are structured data — laws, terrain, guards, layout — not files
| Hosting images creates a legal duty to detect and report child sexual abuse material. The detection services that exist are priced for platforms, not for one person [184]. Designing images out removes the duty and the bill together

| Preset-phrase chat for everyone under 18 (20.2)
| Most grooming risk, most moderation volume and most consent machinery

| No advertising SDKs and no cross-app tracking
| The tracking prompt, most consent requirements, and the hardest parts of the privacy declarations

Free automated moderation runs in front of any player-written text; a paid service is only needed if free chat is ever opened, and is cheap per message even then [185].

Support is self-service first: an in-game help section and a ticket form. Deflection of roughly 40 to 70% is normal when self-service is done well [186].

Volunteers may triage reports inside their constellation; they never see personal data and never decide bans.

What cannot be avoided: truthful privacy declarations on both stores, a real privacy policy and terms, answering deletion requests, the age gate, and tax filing.

20.11 What the stores and the law require, all of which is free to build

| Obligation
| What it means here

| Apple's user-content rules
| A filter on published content, a way to report it, action on a report within 24 hours including removing the content and the account behind it, a way to block another player, published contact details, and account deletion inside the app [194]

| Google's user-content policy
| Ongoing moderation, in-app reporting and blocking, a terms-of-use gate, and a truthful data-safety declaration

| The European Digital Services Act
| A notice-and-action route, a reason given to anyone whose content is restricted, a single point of contact and clear terms. The heavier platform duties do not apply to a micro-enterprise [195]

| Children's privacy law
| A neutral age gate, no accounts below 13, and immediate deletion of anything collected from someone who turns out to be younger. Penalties are per-violation and large, so the gate is not decorative [196]

None of this costs money. All of it costs attention, and the 24-hour rule in particular is a commitment one person must be able to keep — which is another reason the Workshop carries no images and chat carries no free text.

20.12 Islands of players who leave

| Case
| What happens to their islands

| An account is deleted
| Personal details are erased or severed. The island itself — terrain, guards, laws — is not personal data once the name is gone, so it may remain as an unnamed derelict in the ruins, or be withdrawn entirely at the player's request. Erasure completes within thirty days, backups included, with a log kept

| An account is banned
| Their islands leave matchmaking and the Workshop immediately. The minimum identifiers needed to enforce the ban are kept, which the law allows

| Limits on publishing
| Between five and ten published islands per account, with a storage quota per account. Islands are small structured data (20.10), so the quota is generous — but it is enforced at the point of upload, because it is also the cost control

21Look and feel

A guide for Claude Design and for building the world in Godot: how the islands, buildings, characters, laws and interface look, move and sound. The principle behind all of it: every rule must be visible, and everything must read at thumb size.

21.1 Direction and camera

| Topic
| Rule

| Style
| Picture-book dioramas floating in a violet sky: chunky, soft-edged, toy-like shapes; warm light on the islands and cool shadows beneath; gentle physical humour in every animation.

| Readability
| Silhouette first. Every character, building and law signature must be recognisable as a dark shape at 32 points tall.

| Home camera
| An orthographic diorama view at a 35° pitch in a tall frame, rotatable in 90° steps, with three zoom levels: the whole universe, one realm, and a close-up. The universe is arranged as a vertical column of islands rather than a wide row, so it fits a portrait screen. Islands hang over the sky with roots and waterfalls beneath.

| Heist camera
| A closer follow camera at 50° pitch showing about 12 tiles across; a one-second swoop between realms; pinch to see a whole realm (12.6).

| Scale
| One tile is one metre. Ruleborn stand 0.5 to 1.4 metres, Sages about 1.5, buildings 2 to 6 metres. A realm is 10 to 14 tiles across, with a rim of cliffs and hanging roots.

| Screen
| Portrait-first, designed for 9:19.5, one-handed, with every frequently used control inside comfortable thumb reach in the lower two-thirds. Heists can be rotated to landscape for a wider view. Primary controls are at least 9 mm across — about 48 points — because real-world tap error only flattens near 9 to 10 mm and rises above 40% below 8 mm, measured across 120 million touch events [174]. Nothing interactive sits in a screen corner, where error rates are worst. Safe areas respected; nothing important within 44 points of the notch or home bar.

21.2 Colour and shape

| Main colour
| Shadow tint

Neutrals: island stone #D8CFC2, soil #8A6A4E, wood #B07A4F, ink #221C3A, paper #FCFBFF.

Sky: day #9FB9F0 to #F6D9C8; dusk #6A4FC9 to #F29C7B; night #1A1433 to #3A2B6B. The sky follows the player's local time, and Day and night overrides it inside its realm.

Notes use the colours of the wheel in 8.1, each with its symbol (wave, anchor, sun, crescent, sprout, star, ring, mask).

Colour-blind check: every pair of schools must stay distinguishable by shape alone, and palettes are tested for the three common forms of colour blindness (20.6).

21.3 Rendering and performance

Shading: a three-step toon ramp with coloured shadows tinted toward violet, a soft rim light, and 1.5-pixel ink outlines. Hand-painted gradient textures only; no photographic textures. The toon, outline, water, waterfall and sky shaders already exist from the mock-ups.

Effects: law signatures use particles, vertex animation and screen-space tints, never full-screen post-processing on the phone.

Budgets: characters 3,000 to 6,000 triangles, buildings 2,000 to 8,000, a whole realm 25,000 to 40,000, under 150 draw calls on screen.

Frame rate: 60 frames a second on iPhone 12 and newer, never below 30 on the oldest supported phone.

21.6 Characters

Shape, to the cuteness rules: 1.7 to 2.9 heads tall — ten of the eighteen sit in the two-to-two-and-a-half core, four below it and four above, which is what gives 12.21 its small, standard and large bands; the skull wider than the jaw; eyes at or below the vertical midline of the face, each at least a fifth of face width, set wide apart; small nose and mouth; short limbs; a rounded silhouette with no sharp points. These proportions reliably raise perceived cuteness and the urge to look after something, and the effect works on animals and invented creatures as much as on infants (19.19). Each ruleborn's school colour is its main colour, and its school shape appears as a marking.

Role accessories: Scouts wear goggles, Wardens carry a shield, Breakers a hammer or horn, Carriers a big pack, Menders a satchel of leaves. As guards, ruleborn add a sash and a small lantern.

Guards on screen: a soft fan of light shows each guard's vision cone, and a pip over its head fills white, then yellow, then red with awareness.

Animation set: two idles, walk, run, carry, climb, leap, slide, wade, ability, hit, knocked out (dizzy stars, then a puff of petals as it goes home), celebrate, nap, and one work loop per building.

Every creature passes the squint test: recognisable from its silhouette alone, in black, at 32 points. A form that only differs by colour has failed.

Forms: each of the three forms (11.10) changes a ruleborn's pattern, glow and one accessory in its note's colour, using shared materials, so 54 forms need 18 models, not 54.

Laws as modifiers: law signatures are procedural changes to the shared animations (gravity, speed, lean, squash and stretch, time scale, trails), so 48 laws don't need 48 sets of animation. Each body exaggerates them differently: the lighter the body, the bigger the effect.

21.7 Laws on screen

Each law has a world signature (how its realm looks) and a movement signature (how characters move). Both are always on while the law is woven, so an observant player can read an island without opening a menu.

| Law
| How the realm looks
| How characters move

| Bounce
| Walls wobble like jelly when touched.
| Squash-and-stretch rebounds off walls with a springy boing.

| Half gravity
| Pebbles hover a hand above the ground; dust falls slowly.
| Floaty strides and long, slow hops; cloaks and loot sacks drift upward.

| Tilt
| Trees, lanterns and banners lean one way; water runs downhill.
| Arms windmill running downhill; bodies lean hard going uphill.

| Current
| Grass waves and leaves stream in one direction.
| Bodies lean into the wind; hair and cloaks stream; steps lengthen downwind.

| Heavy ground
| Grass lies flat; footprints sink deep.
| Stomping steps with bent knees; carriers hunch under loot; flyers walk.

| Leapfrog
| Springy turf covered in hop marks.
| A step-step-hop rhythm; each landing puffs dust.

| Frictionless
| Glossy ground with reflections.
| Skating glides with arms out for balance; spin-stops against walls.

| Inward pull
| Faint spiral streaks lead toward the Heart.
| A slight drift toward the Heart; feet drag when heading outward.

| Brittle stone
| Hairline cracks everywhere; crumbs fall from walls.
| Careful tiptoeing; wobbles on cracked tiles.

| Crystal growth

| Characters pause to tap humming crystal walls.

| Glass

| Characters crouch behind glass walls, in vain.

| Density
| Grass and props look slightly oversized.

| Transmute

| Carriers juggle sacks that flicker between gold and green.

| Day and night

| Delay

| Characters flinch a beat late; effects land after a pause.

| Haste

| Sped-up strides with motion lines and little skips.

| Held breath

| When the crew stops, everything freezes mid-motion; the crew breathes in deeply.

| Tidemark

Law card: the school shape and colour at the top, the law's name, a looping four-second preview of its movement signature, a one-line rule, notes as symbols, rank and tier pips.

Law banner: slides in at the top left for two seconds when a crew enters a realm: shape, name and one line.

Harmony on screen: a thread in the note's colour links the two pylons; a dissonance is a jagged, flickering line; a chord forms a slowly turning triangle of light between three pylons.

21.8 Interface

| Topic
| Rule

| Type
| Body text is at least 16 pixels, and a line carries at least 13 characters, below which reading speed falls sharply [175]. Contrast meets 4.5:1, checked against a perceptual model as well, because the standard formula overstates contrast on dark backgrounds. A rounded, friendly sans (Baloo 2) for labels and numbers, with tabular numerals; Marcellus for titles and law names. Nothing smaller than 13 points.

| Colours
| Ink #221C3A on paper panels at 85% opacity over the world; gold for main actions, red #E2536B for alarms, green #3FBF7F for success.

| Home layout
| Resources with storage bars at the top left; Keeper level and Renown at the top right; the stability band in the top centre; build, weave and terrain tools at the bottom left; a large skiff-shaped Heist button at the bottom right; alerts down the left edge.

| Heist layout
| Clock and alarm meter at the top centre; loot at the top right as two numbers, what the bags can still take and what is left to load (12.19), beside the Vault's progress; the law banner at the top left; crew portraits at the bottom left; Sage, cast and Get out at the bottom right.

| Ability uses
| Each crew portrait shows its ability's uses left as two pips with the number beside them, so the count never rests on colour alone; a spent pip is hollow. During the 10-second lockout a ring sweeps round the portrait's edge. An ability waiting because four are already ready (12.6) shows a pause mark until another use is taken. The Sage's portrait shows its cooldown as a dial with the seconds left in the middle (11.7).

| Icons
| Rounded shapes with a 2-point outline, flat colour and the school shape where relevant; drawn on a 24-point grid.

| Motion
| 150 to 250 milliseconds, easing out; buttons squash slightly when pressed; rewards fly into their counter. Screen shake never above 4 pixels. Reduced motion removes swoops and most particles.

| Haptics
| A light tap on buttons, a medium pulse on abilities and pylon breaks, a success pattern on escape, a warning pattern on alarm.

| Sound
| Treated as a fun variable, not decoration: sound and music raise enjoyment, flow and immersion, with a small to moderate effect on how positive players feel [71] [72]. Music, effects and alerts have separate volumes, and music can be turned off without losing information, since music a player dislikes lowers immersion [71]. Each school has an instrument: flute for Motion, glass marimba for Matter, celesta and ticking for Time, plucked electric strings for Energy, kalimba and birdsong for Life, choir and bells for Spirit. The music adds a layer for each school in the current realm, and percussion when an alarm sounds. Every school has a sound signature — six in all — so a player can hear which kind of rule is running with the screen half-watched. Per-law sounds were claimed in earlier versions and never authored: the asset budget below has six, and six is the design, and every reveal is teased with a two-second cue before it lands (19.16).

The content rows here are derived from the same inventory 26.1 is checked against (31.5), not typed beside it, so a building, ward or law added to the game moves its row in this budget. The Library was the building this table once missed.

21.10 The two tests every realm and screen must pass

The grayscale thumbnail test. Shrink a screenshot to thumbnail size and remove the colour: the realm should still be identifiable and the important elements still readable. Value structure first, colour second.

Five-second recognisability. A one-second clip of any realm should be named correctly by most players. Each realm owns a distinct value range, hue territory and soundscape (21.4).

Hero features against ambient ones. Of the 39 terrain features, only the handful that change a route are drawn to be individually read; the rest are texture. Search time rises roughly 28 to 45 milliseconds per item when things must be told apart [176], so most of the board must not need telling apart.

21.11 Made, not licensed

Nothing in this game is paid for. The world is generated in code — meshes, shaders, palettes, the music layers — which is both the art direction (21.1) and the licensing position: there is no third party to credit, no attribution chain to maintain and nothing that can be withdrawn.

| Where something must come from outside
| Rule

| Fonts
| Open Font Licence only, which explicitly permits embedding in an application and does not affect the game's own licence [197]

| Sound, if any is not generated
| Public-domain dedication only. No attribution licences, no share-alike, no non-commercial clauses

| Code
| Permissive licences only — the engine itself is one, and charges no royalty

| Everything else
| Made for this game

The traps this avoids
"Royalty-free" means no fee per use, not free and not without obligations. Attribution licences create a chain that must be kept accurate forever. Share-alike can reach into what you build with it. Non-commercial licences cannot be used in a game that earns anything. Many typefaces are licensed for documents and forbid embedding in software. A licence ledger records the source, the licence and the link for every outside file, and it is the only defence if any of this is ever questioned. The ledger's first entries are the two typefaces — Baloo 2 for interface text and Marcellus for titles — both under the open font licence, which permits embedding in a commercial application without affecting the game's own licence, provided the licence text ships with it. It does, in the legal page of the settings.

21.12 Showing what a guard can see

The detection model in 12.17 is only fair if it is visible. The rules that make it readable are borrowed from the stealth games that solved this on small screens [222].

One shape at a time. The selected guard's vision is drawn; the others show only their facing. Five overlapping cones on a phone is noise.

Sharp edges, no gradients. A tile is inside or outside. Soft fades hide exactly the information the player needs.

The fill travels. When a guard begins to notice something, the fill moves visibly from its eyes toward the crew — so the player sees how much time is left rather than reading a number.

Every guard shares the same shapes, scaled by archetype, so the rule is learned once rather than five times.

Tall grass, shade and cover are drawn as ground, not as effects, and are legible in a still screenshot.

22Monetisation

Money buys looks and a season pass. It never buys power, speed or chance.

22.1 What is and isn't sold

Sold

Starglass, in packs from about €1 to €50

The season pass premium track (cosmetic), about €6 a season

Cosmetic sets: realm skins, sky themes, bridge styles, outfits, Sage robes, emotes, law visuals

Occasional supporter packs of cosmetics

Never sold

Resources, pages or laws

Characters, traits or Sages

Skipping rest, experiments or weaving

Shields or heist retries

Random boxes of any kind

Every price is shown in real money before purchase, with no randomness, which keeps Ruleborn clear of loot-box rules in Europe and of App Store disclosure requirements. Players under 18 have monthly spending caps (20.2).

22.2 The business case, as assumptions to test

Monthly revenue = monthly players × share who buy × average monthly spend per buyer, minus the store's commission (at the 2026 rates in 22.5, which differ by store and change, so they are re-verified before any pricing decision). US withholding is a separate line (22.5)

Illustrative scenarios, not forecasts. Revenue divides evenly between the App Store and Google Play. Every input, the split included, is replaced by its soft-launch measurement and the table re-run on it.

| Scenario
| Monthly players
| Share who buy
| Spend per buyer
| Monthly revenue after commission

| Low
| 20,000
| 2%
| €8
| about €2,700

| Base
| 100,000
| 3%
| €10
| about €25,500

| High
| 500,000
| 4%
| €12
| about €182,000

| Low and base pay 15% on both stores. The high scenario's Apple half, about €1.44 million a year, crosses Apple's prior-year proceeds cliff (22.5), so from its second year it pays 30% there; its Google half pays Google's marginal 15% on the first US$1 million a year and 25% above it. At about €0.93 to the dollar that nets about €182,000 a month; in its first year, before the cliff applies, it would net about €200,000. The discontinuity is real, and 22.5 states the dead zone it creates

Costs to estimate before the vertical slice: servers and backend, contract art (characters and interface), audio, community moderation, a one-off legal and privacy review, soft-launch marketing, and the Apple Developer fee.

Launch gates
Go from soft launch to global launch only if, after two rounds of tuning: at least 8% of players are still playing after 30 days, the average "worth your time" rating is at least 4 out of 5, and at least 2% of monthly players buy something.

Risk
Cosmetic-only games earn less per player than pay-to-win strategy games, so Ruleborn needs more players who stay longer. The model works when the game spreads by word of mouth, and when cosmetics are desirable because other players see them in every heist.

22.3 The scale a mega-hit would need

$2 billion a year ≈ $5.5 million a day = 18 million daily players × $0.30, or 37 million × $0.15 (illustrative; cosmetic-only games are likely nearer $0.15 until soft launch measures it)
With about a third of monthly players playing each day, the gate in 25.1: 55 to 110 million monthly players

22.4 Gifting

Players can gift cosmetics to friends, with a monthly limit and parental approval rules for minors. Gifting makes spending social rather than competitive, and social reasons are among the strongest reasons players give for buying (19.9).

22.5 What the stores take, and what they handle

15% on the low and base scenarios; 30% on the high scenario's Apple half. These are 2026 rates, and they change — Google replaced its whole structure on 30 June 2026 — so every figure is re-verified before a pricing decision. Apple's Small Business Program (as of 2026) charges 15% instead of 30% to developers with up to US$1 million in proceeds in the prior calendar year; new developers are eligible, and enrolment is manual [187]. It is a cliff: proceeds over the line in one calendar year put every App Store sale of the next at 30%, and the 15% rate returns only the year after proceeds fall back under it — up to about two calendar years later. Google Play (from 30 June 2026, in the US, the UK and the EEA only; later elsewhere) charges a 10% service fee on the first US$1 million of annual earnings and 20% above it, plus a separate 5% billing fee: 15% and 25% in all, marginal, and reset each January. The model divides revenue evenly between the two stores until soft launch's store reports replace the split; a different split changes the high row mechanically.

The dead zone is a business fact. Because Apple's threshold looks at the prior year, revenue that pushes proceeds just over US$1 million moves the whole of the next year's App Store sales from 15% to 30%, so spend just above the crossing nets less than spend just below it. At an even split and about €0.93 to the dollar, the band runs from roughly €180,000 to €220,000 of monthly spend; if every sale were on iOS it would run from roughly €90,000 to €110,000. The exact band moves with the split, the exchange rate and refunds, so the model computes it from the month's own figures.

The stores are the merchant of record for standard in-app purchases. They calculate, collect and remit sales tax and EU VAT on those sales, so there is no need to register for VAT across Europe to sell the game. External payment links are not planned, so no fourth scenario is modelled: using them on an EU storefront would make the developer responsible for VAT, and below about US$15 a transaction a card processor costs more than Google's 5% billing fee.

United States withholding is modelled at 10% of US-source payouts: a Portuguese resident files Form W-8BEN, and the US–Portugal treaty caps tax on royalties at 10% (Article 13) instead of the default 30%. Whether store payouts are royalties or business profits is a classification that a Portuguese certified accountant with cross-border practice confirms before the first payout; if the answer is business profits, the model's withholding line becomes 0% — a mechanical change.

The entire unavoidable bill is $99 a year to Apple and $25 once to Google [196], and about €900 once for the EU trade mark 27.3 records: one EUTM in classes 9 and 41 [188]. No domain is required, since both stores accept a free hosted page for the privacy policy and support link. There is no licence fee, no subscription and no middleware (24.5).

What this means for the model: infrastructure is a rounding error against store commission at every scale in 22.2. The business risk is reach, not running costs — this game cannot be killed by its own bills.

23Screens to design

The list for Claude Design, in order of importance.

23.1 The screens as they look now

Six screens rendered from the Godot project, portrait, at phone resolution. They are mock-ups, not the built game: the world is real 3D with the shading, islands, buildings and creatures described in 21, and the interface over it is the real layout at the real size. Everything here is drawn or modelled in code, so any screen can be re-rendered after a design change.

Islands. The universe as a portrait column. Resources and strain at the top, the closest goal and the two main actions in the thumb zone, five tabs at the foot.Realm. One island close up: the Spire, houses, paths, lanterns, law pylons with their zones. Law chips float over the pylons they belong to.Landing. Open edges. Green markers are silent landings, red ones are heard. The defender cannot choose where you arrive (5.4).Heist. Eight controls, all within thumb reach: four crew, Sage, cast and Get out. The law banner names the rule that is running.Result. The moment that decided it, with a three-second replay, then one suggestion, then the worth-your-time tap (12.13).Workshop. The Workshop with its tool rail, the creator ladder, and publishing (18.6, 18.7).

Known gaps in these renders: no ambient occlusion, depth of field or soft global illumination, because the renderer used here has no graphics hardware behind it; trees and creatures are still primitives rather than modelled assets; and nothing is animated. All three close on a machine with a GPU.
