23.3 Orientation

Portrait is the default on phones; landscape is available everywhere and remembered per device.

| Reason
| Evidence

| Portrait is the only orientation a thumb can play
| About half of people hold a phone one-handed, and roughly three quarters of all interactions are thumb-driven [103]

| It matches a 5 to 9 minute session in a queue or on a train
| Our own session design (14.1) and the thumb-zone limits in 21.8

| The growing strategy games are portrait
| Last War, Whiteout Survival and Kingshot are portrait and won by widening the funnel — simpler interface, easier onboarding, less punishment — the same moves in this document [104] [105]

| Marketing is vertical
| A 9:16 video covers 75% of the screen on YouTube and all of it fullscreen, and adding vertical assets lifted conversions by more than 35% on Shorts [106]; playable ads convert far better than video [107], and a playable is a slice of the real game, so a portrait game needs no rework

Rules that make supporting both safe

Same information in both. Landscape never reveals more of an island than portrait, or rotating becomes an advantage. The board is designed to a portrait-safe area; landscape shows the same tiles, larger, with the interface moved to the sides.

Phones default to portrait; tablets and the later PC version default to landscape. The choice is remembered per device.

Never rotate mid-heist. Rotation is deliberate, suggested once after the third heist.

Cost: about 15 to 20% more interface work, concentrated in the heist HUD. The 3D world only needs a different camera frame.

Landscape is genuinely better for seeing a route at once and for two-thumb play, and it is native on tablets, which is why it ships as a real option rather than a compatibility mode. Which one players actually choose is question 9 in the fun backlog (19.17).

23.4 Making the concept legible in five seconds

Novelty is a commercial liability unless it is anchored. Across 51,411 songs, every kind of novelty was negatively associated with popularity, at roughly 5 to 6% per unit of uniqueness [159]. Ruleborn is a genuinely novel concept that fits no clean genre tag, which is a discoverability problem before it is a design one.

| Rule
| What it means

| Anchor first, differentiate second
| Signal the familiar — creatures you raise, islands you build, a heist you run — in the first five seconds. The laws are the novelty and they come after the hook

| Show, never explain
| The first screenshot is an island with a visible rule and a crew mid-heist, not a menu. No vocabulary in the first two sentences of the description

| One dominant framing
| The game is pitched as one thing, not four. The collection, the Workshop and the clans are discovered later, not pitched

| Tested, not assumed
| Five-second comprehension tests on the store page, and store-listing experiments on real traffic (26.4)

24Technical logic

| Area
| Approach

| Engine and languages
| Godot 4.5 for the client shell, interface and rendering, in GDScript. The heist simulation itself is a separate Rust library with no engine types in it, compiled into the app on the phone and to WebAssembly on the server, so both run the same code (24.7). Portrait-first, landscape optional in heists. One project for iPhone and Android.

| Heist simulation
| Fixed 30 steps a second, fixed-point numbers and a seeded random generator, so the same inputs always give the same result. The same code runs on the phone, on the server, in rehearsals and in balance testing.

| Heist validation
| The phone sends its taps with their timing; the server replays the heist against the defender's snapshot and settles loot, Renown and pages.

| Balance testing
| Automated crews with scripted tactics and a search-based planner run thousands of heists against generated universes before each release. Targets: no law in more than 25% of top defences, success rates inside the bands in 14.5, and page income within 10% of 14.4.

| Echo universes
| Snapshots of real universes kept for 14 days and offered when real targets are scarce; loot from echoes is created by the server.

| Server
| Authoritative for every resource change, heists, matchmaking, constellations and purchases. A game backend such as Nakama or a small custom service.

| Content as data
| Laws, resonances, ruleborn, traits, buildings, eras and anomalies live in data tables on the server, so content and balance ship without an app update.

| Age and safety
| Age bands from the platform (Apple's Declared Age Range and Google Play's age signals) set the protections in 20.2. Chat passes through a filter service with human review for reports.

| Analytics
| Events for sessions, heists (outcome, laws crossed, crew), weaving, births and purchases, plus the "worth your time" rating and the monthly survey in 25.

| Offline play
| Building, weaving and assigning work offline, confirmed on reconnection. Heists need a connection.

| No AI at runtime
| Guards and crews use hand-written rules, not AI services.

| Economy model
| A Python model of every cost, production rate and income source simulates engaged, regular and casual players day by day. It generated the tables in 10.2 and 14, and is re-run whenever a number changes.

| Harmony resolver
| Each law's notes are data. Harmonies, dissonances and chords are worked out per realm whenever laws are woven, and stored in that realm's rule sheet.

| Island Workshop
| Published islands are stored as data (terrain, laws, buildings, guards), validated by the same simulation, scanned automatically, and served through curated and rated lists.

| Clips
| The deterministic replay is rendered on the phone into a 15-second vertical video; no video is stored on the server unless shared.

| Instant play
| A small version of the game that can open a challenge link without a full install, where the platform's size limits allow.

| Cross-progression
| One account across iPhone, iPad, Android and later PC, with platform sign-in.

| Ruin difficulty
| Each ruin is built at the difficulty the player picked, from a pool weighted toward laws and terrain that player has not met recently (12.12, 15.5). No model changes a chosen difficulty.

| Co-op heists (1.1)
| The deterministic simulation lets two or three phones share inputs with the server as referee.

| Replay validation
| The server re-simulates submitted heists from their seed and input log and rejects any result that does not reproduce. Validation is sampled, not universal: every high-value haul, every league-affecting result, everything disputed, and a random share of the rest. Re-simulating all of them would exceed the per-request compute budget and the cost floor for no security gain, since a cheat that is only caught sometimes is still caught [200]. The same golden replays double as the regression tests that fail the build when the simulation changes [163].

| Bot detection
| Behavioural anomaly detection on crew choice and input timing, added when botting is observed rather than pre-emptively. Published behavioural detectors reach 96 to 98% accuracy on real game data [164].

| Input budget
| Under 25 ms from touch to picture for dragging, which is where performance starts to degrade; taps tolerate about 69 ms [165]. 60 frames a second is a floor, because 30 measurably harms performance while differences above 60 are not significant [166].

| Reference device
| A mid-range 4 GB Android at 60 Hz, not a flagship. Android is 81 to 95% of every target market, and the low-end price tier is the largest segment [167]. Install size is minimised because size gates installation in exactly those markets.

| Dependency map
| Notes are the highest-centrality objects in the system: each touches many laws, every resonance and every chord. Changing a note propagates everywhere; changing a law does not [168]. The note count is therefore frozen at eight, and laws are the expansion axis. Each season's new laws are noted against the band in 8.8 before they are authored — no note more than two carriers below the most-carried one, and each note that completes five chords at or above each note that completes four — and tools/note_rebalance.py fails the content build otherwise.

24.1 The backend, and what it costs

The asynchronous, deterministic design turns out to be the cheapest architecture a multiplayer game can have, and that was luck rather than judgement. A heist is a seed and an input log of a few kilobytes, the device renders the replay itself, and the server only re-simulates to check. There are no real-time servers, no held connections, no video and no uploaded files. The costs that dominate live games are absent rather than reduced.

| Layer
| Choice
| Why

| Static delivery
| Immutable, content-addressed island bundles on zero-egress object storage, cached at the edge for a year
| Most of what a player downloads never changes, so the network serves it and the origin is barely touched. Bandwidth is free on this storage, which is the single largest saving in the design [190]

| Dynamic tier
| Edge functions against a managed serverless SQL database. No virtual machine, no operating system, nothing to patch
| Publishing, validation, league tables and clan state are write-light. The paid plan is $5 a month and includes 25 billion row reads and 50 million row writes — several times what a million players generate [199]

| Object storage
| Replays and snapshots at about $0.015 per gigabyte a month, first 10 GB free [177] [178]
| A million islands at ten kilobytes each is ten gigabytes. Storage is not a meaningful cost here [190]

| Push
| Firebase Cloud Messaging
| Free and unlimited, with no per-message charge at any scale

| Analytics, flags, errors
| First-party events in our own database, a static configuration file on the edge, and a free-tier error reporting service
| No subscriptions. A remote-config service is a file we can host ourselves

| Moderation
| Deterministic word lists, rate limits, reports and mutes — no machine learning anywhere
| Preset-phrase chat means free text cannot be composed, so there is nothing for a classifier to classify (20.10)

Monthly running cost in euros, from published free-tier limits and prices. Engineering estimates, not quotes.

| Monthly players
| 1,000
| 10,000
| 100,000
| 1,000,000

| Egress
| 1 to 2 GB
| ~15 GB
| ~150 GB
| 0.75 to 1.5 TB

| Infrastructure
| €0
| €0
| about $5
| $5 to $15

| Unavoidable cash
| $99 a year to Apple, $25 once to Google. That is the whole recurring bill (24.5)

The dominant cost of this game is the Apple fee, not its servers. At a million monthly players the infrastructure is about $5 to $15 a month, because the paid allowances are larger than anything this design generates.

What actually costs money: database writes first, then origin compute. Telemetry is batched rather than written per event, and snapshots expire after 30 to 90 days.

Where the free tier breaks: the daily request and row-write allowances, in the low tens of thousands of players. The answer is the $5 paid plan, whose included volumes then carry the game to a million [191] — not a machine of our own.

Budget: about 100 to 200 kilobytes and five writes per active player per day. That is the design constraint, not an estimate made afterwards (24.4).

24.2 Determinism is load-bearing, and must be proved first

The first thing to build
Anti-cheat, replay validation, free analytics and the whole cost model all rest on the simulation producing bit-identical results everywhere. Floating-point arithmetic differs across processors, so the simulation uses fixed-point or integer maths throughout, and the first technical task — before art, before content — is proving a heist replays identically on an ARM phone and an x86 server. If it does not, every one of those advantages disappears at once.

24.3 Analytics we get for nothing

Heatmaps without logging positions. Because a replay can be re-simulated, route, detection and failure heatmaps are generated server-side from a sample of stored logs. The client never sends coordinates, which removes the largest telemetry cost in most games [179].

Replay analysis is already paid for. The replays kept for validation are the same ones used to watch what a churned or failed player actually did.

First-party only. No advertising identifiers, no third-party trackers, no cross-app tracking. This keeps the privacy declarations simple and honest, and avoids the tracking prompt entirely (20.5).

Short retention. Raw replays and logs are kept 30 to 90 days, long enough for validation and analysis, then aggregated and discarded.

24.4 Static first: why this costs nothing

Almost nothing in Ruleborn needs a server that thinks. An island is structured data, a heist is a seed and an input log, and an opponent is a snapshot rather than a person. So the game is delivered the way a website is delivered.

| Technique
| What it does

| Immutable, content-addressed bundles
| Many islands are packed into one file named by its hash and cached for a year. The edge serves it; the origin is almost never touched.

| Binary and dictionary compression
| Structured island data packed as binary and compressed with a trained dictionary, since every island shares the same shape. Five to ten times smaller than plain text [191].

| Seeds, never replays
| A heist is a few kilobytes whatever happens in it. Nothing is ever recorded as video.

| Generated, not transmitted
| The world's art and sound are made in code on the device (21.1), so those bytes never cross the network at all.

| The platform holds the save
| Progress syncs through iCloud and Google Play Games saved games, which are free to us and use the player's own storage.

| Egress-free storage
| Bundles are served from object storage that charges nothing for bandwidth, which is also what keeps the arrangement inside the provider's terms [192].

The byte budget. A session pulls about ten opponent islands at roughly ten kilobytes each, occasionally publishes its own, and sends a few heist logs: on the order of 150 kilobytes per active player per day. At a million monthly players, with a quarter of them active daily, that is about a terabyte a month — which on egress-free storage costs nothing. A million published islands is about ten gigabytes.

The dynamic tier, which is very small

Four things genuinely need code running somewhere: publishing an island, validating a submitted heist by re-simulation, the league tables, and constellation state. All are write-light. A managed serverless SQL database handles this at a million monthly players inside the $5 plan's included allowances, with automatic point-in-time recovery and nothing for us to run [193]. Validation is done in batches rather than instantly, because an asynchronous game does not need an answer in the same second.

24.5 The irreducible cost

| Cost
| Amount
| Recurs?

| Apple developer programme
| $99 a year
| Yes — the only unavoidable recurring cost of the whole project

| Google Play registration
| $25
| Once

| Privacy policy and terms
| Nothing
| Written by us, hosted free

| Domain name
| Optional
| Both stores accept a free hosted page for policy and support

| Infrastructure at a million monthly players
| About $5 to $15 a month
| Yes, and it is the smaller half of the bill

The honest statement of this project's economics: the servers are not the cost. The Apple fee is. What this configuration gives up is not reliability but control: we depend on one platform, and validation and league tables update on a cadence rather than instantly. The dependency is mitigated by keeping the data model in plain SQL and the game logic in the client, so the whole backend could be rehosted in a weekend. The cadence is a deliberate choice, not a limitation.

Three things we will not buy

Any machine-learning service in the runtime. No moderation classifier, no recommendation model, no generated content at run time. Everything is deterministic code, which is also what makes replays reproducible (24.2).

Any subscription tool where a static file on the edge will do: remote config is a JSON file, feature flags are fields in it, analytics are our own rows, and error reporting is self-hosted or the platform's own free reports.

Any paid licence. See 21.11.

24.6 Why serverless is also the safer choice

Running no machines is not only cheaper here; it removes most of the attack surface a one-person project cannot defend.

| Risk
| How it is removed

| Unpatched operating systems
| There is no operating system. Code runs as functions on a managed platform

| Open ports and stolen keys
| No remote access to anything, because there is nothing to log into

| Lost data
| Point-in-time restore to any minute in the last 30 days, automatically [199]. No backup script to forget

| Denial of service
| Absorbed at the edge as part of the platform

| Certificates and transport
| Issued and renewed by the platform; every connection encrypted by default

| A forged result
| Every heist is re-simulated from its seed and input log before it counts. A client can lie about what it did, but not about what happened (24.2)

| Someone else's data
| No advertising identifiers, no third-party trackers, no uploaded files and no free text — there is very little worth stealing (20.10, 24.3)

The one real exposure is vendor dependency. It is accepted deliberately, and bounded: plain SQL, no vendor-specific game logic, and an export of every table kept elsewhere. Nothing about the design is difficult to move.

24.7 The simulation core, and where it runs

One rule shapes the whole codebase: the heist simulation is written once, in a library with no engine dependencies, and both the phone and the server run that same compiled logic. Writing it twice — once in the client's language and once in the server's — would mean maintaining two implementations that must agree forever, and they would eventually not.

| Decision
| Detail

| Language
| Rust, compiled into the app as a native extension on iPhone and Android, and to WebAssembly for the server function [201]

| Numbers
| Fixed point throughout: 64-bit with 32 fractional bits for positions and speeds, 32-bit with 16 for angles and small values. No floating point appears anywhere in the simulation, because the same expression can produce different results on different processors [202]

| Trigonometry and roots
| Integer lookup tables with fixed-point interpolation, and integer square roots. Angles are stored as a fraction of a turn, which removes rounding around π entirely. Law effects are written in integers wherever they can be; where one needs a transcendental function, that function has exactly one frozen implementation, versioned with the rule sheet (7.3) and used on every platform, because implementations trade correctness for speed differently and that difference is what breaks bit-identical results

| Randomness
| A 64-bit heist seed expanded through SplitMix64 into xoshiro256++, with a separate stream per subsystem. Both are pure integer operations, identical on every machine [203]

| Ordering
| Stable integer identifiers, sorted iteration, a fixed tick rate, and events resolved in a defined order. Hash-map iteration order is never relied on. This is the ordered-execution contract, and this row is its one home: every law inherits it, the sixteen conditional or periodic ones included (7.10). A hash map is not iterated even on one machine, because its seed is randomised per process, so two runs on the same phone can visit it in different orders

| Floats
| Allowed only where the world is drawn, never where it is decided

Two things this settles

The store rules. The Rust library is linked into the signed application when it is built, so nothing is downloaded or executed at run time — which is what Apple's rules forbid [204]. A runtime WebAssembly loader on the device would break that; there is none.

The fallback. Rust extensions on mobile are officially still experimental [201]. If the first spike cannot get one building and loading on a real iPhone and a real Android inside about ten working sessions, the simulation moves to C# instead, which is a single-language path with its own mobile caveats. The fixed-point module and the golden replays are written to be portable, so that decision costs the spike and nothing else (24.2).

24.8 What the database holds

Small mutable rows in the database, large immutable things in storage, addressed by the hash of their contents.

| Table
| Holds

| accounts
| Identifier, platform identity, league, cosmetics owned, season pass expiry

| islands
| Owner, the hash of the current bundle, when it changed

| snapshots
| Bundle hash as the key, realm type, difficulty band, when published. Never edited

| heists
| Attacker, target bundle hash, seed, hash of the input log, resulting state hash, whether validated

| clans, clan_members, leagues, workshop_items
| Membership, standings, and published islands by author

Publishing writes an immutable bundle to storage under its own hash. Nothing is ever updated in place, which is what makes year-long caching safe.

Target selection is a file, not a query. Candidate lists per difficulty band are generated periodically as static objects, so choosing a target costs a cached download rather than a database search.

No passwords. Platform identity is exchanged for a short-lived signed token.

Submissions are single-use and idempotent: each carries a server-issued nonce bound to attacker and target, and is keyed by the hash of its input log so a retry cannot pay twice.

24.9 Tests the agent cannot satisfy by cheating

This codebase will be written largely by a coding agent, and the documented failure of agent-built code is not that it breaks the tests but that it writes tests it can pass. One study of 144 autonomous cycles found a suite that stayed entirely green while the code decayed, with most modules ending up imported by nothing [205]. The defence is tests that assert behaviour rather than existence, and that a human authors first.

| Test
| What it asserts

| Golden replays
| A committed seed and input log still produce the same final state hash, exactly

| Cross-architecture
| The phone build, the server build and the test build agree, checksum by checksum. A per-step state-hash harness runs in continuous integration with the simulation cross-compiled for ARM and x86: both hash the whole state at every step of every golden replay, and the first step at which they differ fails the build and is reported

| Property tests
| Rules that must always hold: resonance is symmetric, no chord divides by zero, no law produces an out-of-range value, no ordering depends on a hash

| Fuzzing
| Random islands, crews and inputs, checked for divergence and for saturation of the fixed-point range

| Content schema
| A malformed law fails the build rather than a player's device

The simulation's golden fixtures and the determinism checks are written by hand before the code is, and the agent is never both their author and their only judge.

24.10 The shape of one heist, in numbers

| Parameter
| Value
| Why

| Tick rate
| 30 a second, fixed
| Lockstep strategy games run as low as 10 and fighting games at 60. A stealth heist needs finer resolution than the first and no more than the second. A heist is 4,500 to 7,200 ticks; if a cheap phone cannot hold it, drop to 20 before sacrificing anything else

| Numbers
| 64-bit fixed point, 32 fractional bits
| Decimals are not identical across processors. The simulation refuses to compile if one appears

| What is recorded
| Commands, never state
| A move or an ability with its tick, crew member and target. State is always recoverable by re-simulating

| Size of a heist
| Under 2 kilobytes
| About 120 bytes of header, 300 to 700 bytes of commands, and a checksum a second. This is why storage and bandwidth cost almost nothing (24.1)

| Checksums
| Every tick in memory, one a second kept
| The first mismatched second narrows a divergence to thirty ticks, then a tick-by-tick comparison finds it exactly

| Order
| Entities by number, events by tick then source, phases always in the same sequence
| Inputs, then laws, then perception, then movement, then noise, then states, then the checksum. Iterating a map in whatever order it happens to be in is one of the three classic causes of divergence

24.11 When a law changes, history stays valid

Pin, do not patch. Every island and every replay records the exact set of laws it was made under, so a balance change never invalidates the past.

| Event
| What happens

| A law is re-tuned
| Old replays stay valid forever against the rules they were played under. Existing islands are re-costed at next login, and any that now exceed their stability budget enter a grace state: still defendable, not offered to new raiders, until the owner rebalances

| A law is removed
| Its name is never reused, and its definition stays archived so old replays still run. Islands using it move to grace state and prompt a replacement

| Any past heist
| Reproducible forever from four things: the seed, the input log, the island and the rules version

24.12 What happens when the network is not there

This game will be played underground, on trains, in lifts. Offline is the normal path, not an edge case, and the rules below are architecture rather than polish.

| Stage
| What happens

| Lease
| The server hands out a target snapshot, a seed, a single-use token and an expiry a day away. Nothing is created yet

| Play
| The heist runs on the device with no connection at all. The haul is shown immediately, marked as confirming

| Queue
| If offline, the result waits with its input log and a key made from the player, the token and the island

| Validate
| The server re-simulates, compares, and checks the token, the expiry and the caps

| Grant
| Only then is the haul minted, into an append-only ledger

| When it goes wrong
| What the player gets

| The connection drops mid-submission
| Nothing. The same key is retried and the server recognises it, so a haul can never be paid twice [229]

| The result does not reproduce
| No reward for that heist, nothing taken away from before, and a note for review. One mismatch is never a ban

| The lease expired
| No reward, with one grace submission allowed

| A cap was reached
| Whatever is left under the cap, and a clear message saying so

| A purchase completes but the confirmation does not
| The item shows as pending, is re-checked on next launch, and is never taken back once confirmed [230]

Rewards are shown at once but minted only after validation. Showing a number and then reducing it is the worst thing this game could do to trust, so nothing is ever confirmed before it is real.

The server clock is the only clock. Cooldowns, daily caps, season boundaries and lease expiry are all stamped at submission, so changing the time on a phone achieves nothing.

Two devices cannot double-count, because currency is a ledger of minting events rather than a number that gets overwritten. Only preferences use last-write-wins.

Editing an island during a raid is impossible to notice, since a raid runs against a frozen snapshot and an edit simply creates a new one (24.11).

24.13 Two edges worth naming

One lease per target per player at a time. A second device asking for the same target is handed the lease the first already holds, not a new one, so two heists against one snapshot cannot both be pending.

The entry fee is debited at validation, not at lease. A heist played offline and never submitted costs nothing and reserves nothing. The fee and the reward settle in the same transaction, which is also the only way the ledger can stay a single append-only record (24.12).

25Targets

Internal goals for soft launch and after, to be adjusted once real data exists. Nobody outside plays before soft launch, the beta included (26.2), so none of these is read earlier.

| Measure
| Target

| Players who finish the first session
| 85%

| Players still playing after 1, 7, 30 and 60 days
| 40%, 18%, 8%, 5%

| Average "Was this worth your time?" rating
| 4 out of 5 or higher

| Players who say the game feels like a chore (monthly survey)
| 10% or fewer

| Session length
| 5 to 9 minutes

| Sessions a day for active players
| 3 to 6

| Heist success in the first 30 days, then after
| 70 to 80%, then 55 to 65%

| Decisions per heist
| 15 to 25

| Players who find a resonance by day 3
| 50%

| Players in a constellation by week 2, from version 1.1 (26.1)
| 45%

| Monthly players who buy something
| 2% or more

25.1 Gates for a mega-hit forecast

The targets above describe a successful game. A forecast of one of the most successful games ever is only justified if Ruleborn clears the gates below, which sit around the top 1% of games in benchmark data [35]. Missing them doesn't mean failure; it means the mega-hit forecast isn't supported yet.

| Stage
| Gate

| Soft launch, first cohorts
| At least 7 in 10 players who finish a first session come back for a second unprompted; average "worth your time" of 4.3 out of 5 or higher

| Soft launch, retention
| Players still playing after 1, 7 and 30 days: 50%, 25% and 10%; a third of monthly players play on a given day

| Social
| Within a week, 40% of players have played with or against a friend

| Creation
| Within 30 days, 1.5% of players have published a Workshop island and 10% have remixed one. Online communities typically see about 1% creating, 9% editing and 90% watching [73] [74], so the old 5% target was above what any platform achieves.

| Spread
| Every 10 players bring at least 3 new ones through clips, links and invites; at least half of installs are organic

| End of soft launch
| The retention gates hold in every soft-launch market; at least 3% of monthly players buy something

The first two rows once sat at the vertical slice and a closed beta. Both need outside players, and nothing before soft launch recruits any (25.4), so they are read in soft launch. The phases before it exit on the harness instead (26.2). Enjoyment and week-one retention are therefore ungated until soft launch, an accepted consequence stated with its falsification condition in 19.27.

25.2 The first 60 seconds

Most churn happens on the first day, and players who complete a meaningful action in their first session are far more likely to still be playing a month later [54]. The first minute therefore has its own rules and its own funnel.

| Rule
| Detail

| Playing within 30 seconds
| No account creation, no permission prompts, no settings and no logos that can't be skipped before the first heist.

| One meaningful action
| Every first session ends with the player having robbed a vault and woven a law into their own island: something theirs, that they made.

| Permissions later
| Notifications are requested after the third session, and only with a reason on screen.

| Guidance across the first three sessions
| Not two steps and done. Tutorials paid off only in the complex game in a 45,000-player experiment, adding 29% more play time [9], and this is a complex game. The first minute is unguided play; sessions two and three each teach one idea in context, then guidance fades as the player shows competence [79].

| Funnel step
| Target

| Opened the app and reached the first heist
| 95%

| Finished the first heist
| 90%

| Wove their first law
| 85%

| Finished the first session
| 85%

| Came back for a second session the same day
| 60%

| Median time to the first heist
| Under 45 seconds

Every step is measured separately: "85% finished the tutorial" hides which step lost the other 15%.

25.3 Operational gates and early-warning metrics

| Gate
| Threshold
| Why

| Crash-free sessions
| 99.9% or better; ANR under 0.47%
| Below about 99.82% apps tend to fall under three stars, and half of one-star reviews mention crashes [157]. These are also the platform's own bad-behaviour thresholds [158]

| Defence participation
| Reported, not a gate: 15 to 20% of new players publish a defence of their own in week one, and 40% change their defence weekly
| Every player has a generated defence, so the assumption is removed rather than measured (13.3); a shortfall moves only the defence reward (13.1)

| Concept comprehension
| At soft launch, asked in the game of new players: half can say what the game is from five seconds of its store page; 70% can define chord, resonance, ruleborn and universe after the tutorial. A word that fails is cut or re-staged; “universe” is swapped for “your sky” in its one string table (15.6)
| Novelty carries a measured commercial penalty [159]; vocabulary density is the sharpest comprehension risk (15.6)

| Churn early warning
| Recency of absence, plus the funnel in 25.2
| Absence time is the single strongest churn feature across published models, which reach 66 to 95% accuracy [160]

| Experiment power
| About 900 installs per cell to read day-1 retention to ±3 points; one readable cohort beats several unreadable ones
| [161]

| Usability rounds
| Three rounds of 5 to 8 soft-launch players who opt in, not one round of five. Before soft launch, the developer only (25.4)
| Five-user sets have ranged from finding 99% of problems to 55%; ten raises the worst case to 80% [162]

25.4 How we will actually find out

No recruited panels, no paid playtesters, no thirty people in a room. Evidence comes from three places, in this order.

| Source
| What it answers
| Cost

| The simulation harness
| Decision density, skill separation, hold rates, pacing, economy health — everything structural (19.27, 13.4)
| Nothing, and it runs on every build

| The developer playing
| Whether it is worth opening. Weak evidence, recorded as weak, available daily
| Nothing

| Soft launch
| Everything a person must answer: enjoyment, meaning, whether anyone builds, whether it is worth their time
| The instruments in 14.10, answered by behaviour first and short surveys second

Behaviour beats opinion, and behaviour arrives free. Whether someone plays a second heist is worth more than what they say about the first, and the game logs it either way.

Surveys stay short and rare — the eleven-item experience inventory as the default, everything else rotated to small samples, never more than once a quarter per player.

The gap is named rather than hidden: nothing before soft launch tells us the game is fun. The simulation tells us it is not broken, which is a different and smaller claim. Enjoyment and week-one retention are ungated before soft launch; that is the accepted consequence of recruiting nobody, and its falsification condition and fallback are in 19.27.

26Scope, roadmap and production

26.1 Launch content

| Content
| Launch
| After launch

| Realms

| +6 each season, the other 24 of the 48 first

| Paradoxes
| 3
| +1 each season

| Signature resonances and chords
| 12 and 12: the signature resonances whose two laws both ship, and every chord (7.10, 8.4)
| Grow with laws

| Ruleborn
| 18
| +3 each season

| Traits
| 31
| +5 each season

| Sages
| 4
| +2 in the first year

| Visitors, paradox-born, anomalies
| 3, 3, none
| Rotating; 3 anomalies with constellations in version 1.1

| Eras
| 8
| More each season

| Building types
| 17
| —

| Law-shaped forms
| 54
| +3 with each new kind; +18 with each note added as seasonal content

| Terrain features
| 39
| +3 each season

| Ward levels
| 2
| Levels 3 to 5

| Languages
| English and Brazilian Portuguese
| One a season, by store-listing demand (26.4)

| Modes
| Heists, friendly heists, ruins, trials, rehearsals, the Island Workshop
| Constellations with their Grand Law, anomalies and league (version 1.1), co-op heists, epochs, watching live, Law Clash (18.10)

How the launch table was cut

Laws are cut by engine cost, not by rank or taste. The launch 24 are the cost-aware set: all sixteen laws that are only a parameter change, plus the cheapest of the rest on the engine-cost ladder in 7.10, four laws a school. That set costs 14 engine points, against 36 for a 24 chosen by rank (7.10). The 24 that wait are listed in 7 in cut order, expensive first, and the order is fixed here so taste cannot re-order it.

The spine survives the cut. The launch 24 must still show a two-way combination, a three-way chord and at least one paradox, because those carry what makes the game different. A cut that loses any of them is not taken.

The Island Workshop is not cuttable. Content is consumed faster than a team this size can author it, and the Workshop is the only answer to that treadmill (26.5), so it ships whatever else moves.

The cut falls on breadth: three forms a kind (11.10), 12 signature resonances rather than 44, wards at two levels rather than five, two languages, and constellations in version 1.1.

26.2 Phases

Durations are estimates for a small team working with Claude Code. Each phase ends only when its exit test passes.

| Phase
| Length
| Contents
| Exit test

| 1. Pre-production
| 6 to 8 weeks
| Lock this document; design every screen in Claude Design; art style tests; heist rules tested in the simulation harness
| Screens approved; the art-style test shows eight wards and six or seven kinds of defensive marker reading on target devices, checked by the developer and under colour-blindness simulation — if eight do not read, the ward cap drops by one at a time until they do and 13.2's bands move by its rule; the harness reports 15 to 25 decisions a run and skill separating outcomes (19.27)

| 2. Vertical slice
| 10 to 12 weeks
| A Heart and two realms; 8 common laws; 6 ruleborn; 2 Sages; ruins only; rehearsals
| The harness gates in 19.27: 15 to 25 meaningful decisions a run; a near-capture in about a third of runs; the optimal crew at least 35 points ahead of the careless one; hold rates and pacing in band; and commands explaining a majority of outcome variance. No outside player is involved

| 3. Alpha
| 12 weeks
| 18 laws; heists against players; the server; balance bots
| No dominant law in bot runs, and the same harness gates as phase 2, on the full alpha content

| 4. Beta on TestFlight
| 12 weeks
| All launch content; age protections; moderation; cosmetics; season track. Played only by the developer: TestFlight is the delivery channel, not a way to recruit testers (25.4)
| Stable; privacy and legal review passed

| 5. Soft launch
| 8 to 12 weeks
| Three markets, each chosen by the question it answers (27.3): a cheap high-volume market for technical stability, one that behaves like the target audience for retention, and a tier-one English-language market for monetisation
| The launch gates in 22.2, and the gates that need players: 25.1, the fun gate in 19.5 and comprehension in 25.3

| 6. Global launch
| —
| iPhone and Android together, with cross-progression, in English and Brazilian Portuguese. Each season the locale whose localised store listing brought the most installs is translated next, one at a time (26.4). Then version 1.1 (constellations with their Grand Law, anomalies and league; co-op heists; epochs; watching live; two new Sages) and 1.2 (Law Clash duels under the rules in 18.10; a PC version)
| —

No phase before soft launch needs a player other than the developer. The harnesses decide phases 1 to 3, and the beta exits on stability and review. Enjoyment and week-one retention are therefore ungated until phase 5: an accepted consequence, with its falsification condition and fallback in 19.27.

26.3 Team

Francisco: product owner and game designer, and owner of translation: both launch languages, English and Brazilian Portuguese, and a native-speaker read-aloud of every coined name in both (26.9). Nobody else on the team owns it, and unassigned work defaults to the developer.

Claude Code: engineering for the game, the server, the tools and the balance bots.

Contractors: a character artist, an interface artist and an audio designer.

From soft launch, when there are players: a part-time community moderator.

Once: a legal and privacy review before soft launch, and an EU trade-mark attorney for the clearance opinion and the filing (26.6).

From the first invoice: an accountant, for tax, creator payouts and cross-border VAT (26.6).

26.4 Launch experiments

| What
| How it's decided

| Store page
| Icon, first three screenshots and the video are A/B tested on real store traffic, one element at a time, for at least a week each, starting in soft launch [55]. The first screenshot must show an island with a visible rule, not a menu.

| Languages
| Store pages are translated into more languages than the game, and the extra ones measure demand before the game itself is translated. English and Brazilian Portuguese at launch. Store listings are localised into Spanish, French, German, Italian, Japanese, Korean and Chinese from soft launch, and each season the locale whose listing brought the most installs is translated next, one at a time.

| First session
| Two versions of the opening minute are tested in soft launch against the funnel in 25.2.

| Sharing
| Clip length, the challenge-link wording and the invite gift are tested against how many new players each brings.

| What is never tested
| Anything that changes a player's odds or price without telling them (19.12).

26.5 How this team estimates, and what it refuses to do

| Rule
| Evidence

| Estimate from comparable finished games where they exist; here they do not
| The planning fallacy is one of the most replicated findings in behavioural science; reference-class forecasting is the established corrective [169]. For a Godot-and-Rust game built by one developer with an agent, the class is empty. So the bottom-up figure in 26.8 is a hypothesis, not an estimate, and the specific trap is uniqueness bias: “our project is different”. The controls are iteration budgeted at 1× the build as a floor, that uplift committed before any early result, and the ten-run rescale (26.8)

| No AI productivity multiplier in the plan
| In a randomised trial, 16 experienced developers were 19% slower on 246 real tasks with AI assistance, while forecasting a 24% speedup and still believing afterwards they had been 20% faster [170]. The transferable finding is the calibration failure. AI is used for boilerplate and prototyping; review time for generated code is budgeted explicitly, and nobody merges code they cannot explain

| No sustained crunch
| Eight weeks of sixty-hour weeks produces the same output as eight weeks of forty, with more defects [171]

| No hiring out of a slip
| Adding people to a late project makes it later; scope is the lever, not headcount

| Scope discipline is the main risk control
| Small projects succeed around 90% of the time against under 10% for large ones, on data whose absolute figures are contested but whose direction is not [172]. It is applied to 26.1: the launch table is cut by engine cost

| Launch lean and grow by update
| Solo and small-team launches are the right reference class, and every one launched lean and grew by free updates. Slay the Spire's identity was three characters, not its 283 cards (PC Gamer's review at 1.0); Vampire Survivors entered early access with seven characters and one stage (the developer's own listing). Supercell is the wrong class — a large studio — and even its launches were lean: Clash Royale shipped 42 cards in English only, and Clash of Clans eight Town Hall levels. The table in 26.1 is not to be re-inflated against it

| Cadence comes from measured capacity
| Content is consumed faster than a small team can author it. The Workshop is the answer, and no public roadmap is promised that would require crunch to meet

26.6 The things that are not game design

A solo developer's real risk is not the build. It is the recurring cash, the legal work and the small number of tasks that must not be done alone.

| Task
| What it involves
| Rough cost

| Name clearance, then one EU mark
| Clearance first, all of it free: search the European and United States registers, both app stores, domains and handles before any public use; choose a distinctive coined name; claim the name in both stores early, since each reserves it first-come at no cost; and keep dated evidence of first use [198]. Then one EU trade mark in class 9 and class 41, filed by an EU trade-mark attorney: the Union grants no unregistered protection and is first to file, so a later filer could block the name (27.3). Fees are €850 for the first class and €50 for the second [188]
| About €900 in fees, plus the attorney

| The risk we are accepting
| In the United States nothing is registered. Using a name in commerce creates unregistered rights in the market where it is actually used — enough to complain, harder to enforce. If someone registers it later they gain wider priority, though a genuine earlier user usually keeps their own market. Both stores run name disputes without requiring a registration, but respond faster to those who have one [198]
| Accepted knowingly

| Company and tax
| Sole trader to begin with, under the simplified regime; VAT exemption applies below the threshold. The simplified-regime figures are the model's tax line; an accountant confirms them before the first invoice, and from then on handles tax, creator payouts and cross-border VAT [189]
| An accountant's monthly fee

| The determinism spike
| Prove a heist replays bit-identically on a low-end Android phone and on the server, and measure frame pacing, heat, battery, install size and text legibility in both launch languages on that same phone (24.2)
| The price of a cheap handset

| Competitor teardown
| Twenty hours inside three to five adjacent games, recording loop timing, onboarding, monetisation, live-ops cadence, creator model and the recurring complaints in their reviews
| Time

| Art and audio
| Generated in code wherever possible; otherwise public domain or permissive only, with a licence ledger (21.11)
| Nothing

What not to do alone

Tax filing and company decisions.

Nothing that requires a lawyer's opinion — which is why the name is coined and the assets are generated. The one exception, the EU trade mark, goes to an EU trade-mark attorney.

Anything involving images uploaded by players — which is why there are none (20.10).

Free-text chat at all, which is why there is none.

Writing the privacy policy and terms from nothing.

Running several experiments at once, which is the fastest way to fool yourself.

26.7 The order of work

No step waits for other people. The two that used to — the paper heist and the build-willingness test — are replaced by the harness in 19.27 and by the design change in 13.3. That puts the harness on the critical path: phases 2 and 3 exit on it (26.2), so it is built before the vertical slice. The browser prototype stays published throughout, instrumenting only play that arrives of its own accord, with consent; nobody is recruited to it (19.27).

| When
| What
| What it decides

| First, in parallel
| Determinism on desktop against the server build (24.2)
| Whether the simulation is honest. A failure is decimals in the simulation and dooms every language equally

| Reconcile the specifications that exist twice (30.18)
| Days of desk work, and the cheapest work in the plan. Nothing can be locked while two answers exist

| Then
| The spike on real phones (24.2, 24.7)
| Rust, C++ or C#. Blocks every line of core code

| Lock the simulation contract (24.10)
| Blocks the detection model and versioning

| Specification lock
| Detection numbers (12.17), movement and fights (12.18, 12.20)
| Blocks all balance work

| The law data table (7, 8.7)
| Blocks authoring or generating content

| Saves, bundles and versions (24.11)
| Blocks persistence and server validation

| Then, together
| The simulation harness (19.27), including the predictability measure
| Everything structural, on every build from then on, and the exits of phases 2 and 3 (26.2). On the critical path: the vertical slice cannot exit without it

| Gameplay and engine code (26.8)
| —

26.8 The build, measured in working sessions

Effort here is counted in Claude Code runs: one continuous session where the agent works until it needs a decision, hits a natural boundary or runs out of context. Hours are meaningless for this project; sessions are not.

What one run buys, and the assumptions behind it

About one vertical slice: three to six files, roughly 250 to 600 lines of code with its tests, compiling and passing. Around two to four hours of focused human work. This is deliberately smaller than what the agent could attempt, because reliable completion tops out well below maximum capability — the length of task a frontier model finishes reliably is around half an hour of human-equivalent work, even when it can attempt far longer [206]. Assume roughly two in three tasks land first time; the rest need a fix-up session. Estimates assume a maintained instructions file, a test suite the agent runs itself, and one vertical slice per session.

| Phase
| Runs
| Notes

| 0. Determinism and mobile spike
| 6 to 14
| The gate for everything. Risky: real devices, experimental tooling, and the fallback decision (24.7)

| 1. Simulation core
| 28 to 48
| Tick loop, crews, stealth, the law engine, resonances and chords, golden harness. Human-written spec required

| 2. Client shell
| 12 to 20
| Project structure, localisation, portrait interface, save and settings

| 3. Heist prototype
| 10 to 18
| First playable: render a replay, capture input, show a result

| 4. Island builder
| 14 to 24
| Terrain, laws, bundle serialisation, testing your own defence

| 5. Content systems
| 10 to 18
| 24 laws, 54 forms, 39 terrain features as validated data. Ideal agent work

| 6. Backend
| 24 to 40
| Schema, identity, publishing, the validator, target lists, rate limits

| 7. Workshop
| 10 to 18
| Publish, browse, moderate, remix

| 8. Progression and economy
| 14 to 22
| Currencies, cosmetics, purchases and receipts, the season track

| 9. Season and social
| 12 to 20
| Leagues, crews, season rollover. Constellations are built for version 1.1 and costed with it

| 10 to 13. Localisation, accessibility, compliance, telemetry
| 20 to 38
| English and Brazilian Portuguese, the accessibility set, store declarations, event logging

| 14. Launch hardening
| 12 to 24
| Load testing, a real-device matrix, a balance pass, a fix buffer

| Fix-up sessions
| 57 to 101
| One task in three needs a fix-up session (above): a third of the phase runs, 172 to 304

| Build total
| 229 to 405
| Point estimate about 320. A hypothesis, not an estimate (26.5)

| 15. Iteration: finding the fun
| 229 to 405
| 1× the build, stated as a floor rather than an estimate. It carries the experiments in 19.17 (14 to 28 runs) and a dead-code and duplication audit every 20 to 30 runs

| Plan total
| 458 to 810
| Plan against about 640

| Safe to hand over
| Not safe to hand over

| Content data and its schemas; interface screens; localisation; the Workshop; target-list generation; telemetry; property and fuzz tests
| The determinism spike; the fixed-point module; the stealth model; the validator; purchases and receipts; anything needing a real device; store submission

Finding the fun is budgeted. Building the stealth model and the law system is not the same as iterating until they are enjoyable, and for a genre blend with no reference implementation the iteration is normally the larger part. No measured multiple exists, so it is carried at 1× the build as a floor: Cerny's method implies about 0.4× and Schell's halfway rule about 1×, and neither is a measurement. The nine experiments in 19.17 are costed inside it.

No AI speed-up in the schedule. In the one randomised trial, developers forecast a 24% speed-up, believed afterwards they had gained 20%, and were measured 19% slower [170]. A felt speed-up is not a planned one.

Rescale after ten runs. These are derived estimates, not measurements. After the first ten agent runs, count the runs actually spent per feature and rescale the whole estimate — never below the iteration floor until phase 2's exit test has passed (26.2), which is the first evidence that the stealth model and the law system converge.

Audit the code as it grows. A dead-code and duplication audit runs every 20 to 30 runs, inside the iteration budget. In the largest study of changed code, copy-pasted lines overtook moved lines in 2024 as AI assistance spread, and cloned code carries 15 to 50% more defects.

Scope one slice per session. If a task looks bigger than a run, split it before starting rather than halfway through.

26.9 Names, and what we checked

Every invented name in this document was checked against what already exists, as though no trade mark would be filed: the United States position (26.6). The one EU mark filed since does not relax the check. Five names were changed, including the game's own. The rest are either coined or ordinary words used in their ordinary sense, which is why dozens of games share them without difficulty.

| Was
| Now
| Why

| Echo Knight
| Echoplate
| A well-known fighter subclass in a published tabletop setting, and ours shared its mechanic — a copy of yourself that returns. The resemblance was the problem, not the words

| Moss Giant
| Mossbear
| A long-standing creature in another online game. Defensible, but cheap to avoid

| Masquerade
| Borrowed Face
| A famous games mark in its own right. The replacement says what the law does more clearly anyway

| Creator Fund
| Island Fund
| A widely used programme name on a large platform

The game's own name

Lawbound became Ruleborn

No game called Lawbound was found, but "-bound" is well populated in the classes that matter — Stormbound and Bladebound are both registered for game software — and outside the EU, where no mark is filed, unregistered rights cover only the market actually used. A crowded field gives a later registrant room to press, and the stores answer faster to whoever holds a registration.

Ruleborn is coined, sits in a far emptier field, and names the creatures rather than the constraint — which is the better story anyway: the game is named after what was born from the scattered laws, not after being tied by them. The clearance still happens before any public use: both registers, both stores, domains and handles.

What everything else rests on

Ordinary words, used ordinarily: Starlight, Essence, Aether, Renown, Keeper, Codex, Vault, Spire, Nursery, Observatory, Constellation, Workshop, and all seven realm names. Generic or descriptive, and used as such.

Coined names for everything with character: the eight notes, the six schools, and the creature list — Featherkin, Skyskipper, Quarryback, Tidewalker, Hourkeeper, Stillwatch, Emberling, Sparkwright, Bloomling, Rootrunner, Lantern Wisp, Soulmender, Echoplate, Mossbear.

Other games named in this document — and there are many — are referred to by name for comparison, which is ordinary and lawful. No logo, artwork or text of theirs appears anywhere in it.

Every new name is checked before use, not after: the registers, both stores, a plain web search, and read aloud by a native speaker of each launch language, English and Brazilian Portuguese (15.6). The read-aloud costs nothing, so it is done for every coined name.

If Ruleborn does not clear. Two fallbacks are held in reserve, chosen now rather than under time pressure: Lawkeep and Isleborn. Both are coined, both sit outside the crowded "-bound" field, and both pass the same four checks. Neither is the name already in use — which is what the earlier list in 27.2 wrongly said.

27Risks and decisions

27.1 Risks

| Risk
| Mitigation

| The first minute loses players before the game starts
| Rules and a measured funnel for the first 60 seconds (25.2), tested in soft launch.

| Research findings don't reproduce here
| Evidence ranked by strength, theory-based claims marked, and effects assumed to arrive smaller (19.14).

| Too many rules for new players
| The complexity budget (7.8), notes that can be reasoned about (8), the learning map (15) and generous early heists.

| Some combinations break the game
| Balance bots before every release; stability costs and dissonance; laws that affect both sides; tuning without app updates (24).

| Heists feel like watching
| Decision density and controls (12.6, 12.7); the predictability proxy gates phases 2 and 3 (19.27, 26.2); if it fails, or soft launch shows players watching while it passes, the crew stops acting between orders (19.27).

| Nothing before soft launch measures enjoyment
| Accepted as the cost of recruiting nobody, and named as the regret solo post-mortems report most. The harness gates structure, the browser prototype takes inbound play, and the fallback for watching is specified now (19.27).

| Open landing makes defence feel hopeless
| Terrain along every edge (6), landing noise (5.4), loss caps and the sealed share (9.4), rehearsals (13).

| Fights reward only bigger numbers
| Stealth, ambushes, wind-ups and the level cap (12.8).

| The economy numbers come from a model
| Re-run the model with alpha data and retune through server data, without app updates (24).

| Casual players take almost three years to finish
| The curve is cut, and a catch-up discount keyed to the median player ships with it: openly labelled, on levels below the current median Keeper level (27.3, 27.5).

| Too few players to raid
| Echo universes and ruins (12.3); soft launch in a few countries first.

| Interest in deep strategy is declining
| Short sessions, the complexity budget, and depth that can wait until the player wants it.

| Teen safety
| Protections by age band, moderation and privacy by default (20).

| Earnings without pay-to-win
| Retention first, visible cosmetics, a season pass and the launch gates (22.2).

| Art scope
| Law signatures as procedural changes to shared animations (21.6); a world built in code; paid art for characters, the icon and marketing.

| Name and trademark
| Free clearance of "Ruleborn" in both stores and both registers before any public use, one EU trade mark, and the fallbacks Lawkeep and Isleborn (26.6, 26.9).

| Too much to build for this team
| The launch cut by engine cost (26.1), iteration budgeted at 1× the build as a floor, and the ten-run rescale (26.8).

27.2 Decisions taken

| Question
| Decision

| Pitch and story
| "Your islands. Your rules. Their treasure." Laws are pages of the scattered Book of Laws; pages replace fragments

| Where laws apply
| Crown laws for the whole universe (1 slot from Spire level 2, 2 from level 5) and realm laws for one island (the Heart has 2 slots, outer realms 1 to 3)

| Landing
| Open: any edge tile of any outer realm, by skiff; the Heart can't be landed on

| Stability
| Built by the Spire and Anchor Stones; seven bands from Quiet to Breaking

| Laws
| 48 in six schools: three common, two uncommon, two rare and one deep per school. 24 ship at launch, chosen by engine cost; the rest arrive by season (26.1)

| Combinations
| Eight notes giving harmonies and dissonances, 44 signature resonances and 12 chords; 12 of the resonances ship at launch, those whose two laws both ship (7.10, 26.1)

| Terrain
| 39 features in seven families, limited by terrain points

| Camera
| A rotatable diorama view at home; a follow camera in heists

| Pacing target
| An engaged player reaches Keeper level 40 in about six months and finishes everything in about a year

| Platforms
| iPhone and Android together at global launch, cross-progression, English and Brazilian Portuguese; PC in version 1.2

| Under-13s
| No kid-safe mode: Ruleborn stays 13+, now and later

| Workshop creators
| Reversed in version 1.4: a Island Fund of 10% of cosmetic and pass revenue, paid in arrears with a cap and a floor, 18+ and opt-in, on top of the in-game ladder (18.9)

| China
| Not pursued: no local publisher and no licence application

| Co-op design
| Interdependent roles, one shared goal, and results never attributed to one player

| Reveals
| Tease then resolve, in seconds, never purchasable

| How fun is judged
| Harness gates before soft launch (19.27, 26.2); validated instruments and the fun gate from soft launch on (19.5); never a single score

| Concept additions
| Law-shaped forms, friendly heists, sharing and the Island Workshop at launch; co-op heists in version 1.1

| Matchmaking against players
| Transparent Renown bands; outcomes are never secretly tuned for engagement, and the refusal is written down (19.12)

| Difficulty
| Chosen by the player and labelled; novelty and suspense do the work instead of hidden tuning

| Accessibility
| A full specification at launch, checked against its own list and under colour-blindness simulation before soft launch, with disabled players' reports read from soft launch on (20.6, 20.8)

| Audience and rating
| Core ages 16 to 34; rated 13+; protections for 13 to 17

| Control in a heist
| Live taps, with at most eight controls on screen

| Theft
| Raiders take a capped share of the exposed amount

| Building timers
| None; upgrades are instant

| Names
| The player is the Keeper; heroes are Sages; traders are Visitors

| Daily rewards
| No login rewards; self-chosen goals; a weekly streak with repairs

| Eras and Law Clash
| Eras at launch; Law Clash in version 1.2, with its rules written now (18.10)

| Cost ceiling
| Near zero to a million players: static, egress-free delivery plus serverless functions and a managed database — about $5 to $15 a month at a million. $99 a year to Apple remains the only unavoidable recurring cost (24.1, 24.5)

| Machine learning
| None, anywhere — not in the runtime, not in moderation, not in analytics (24.5)

| Trade mark
| One EU trade mark in classes 9 and 41, because the Union grants no unregistered protection; none in the United States, where dated first use holds. Free clearance, a coined name and store names claimed early come first (26.6)

| Licensing
| Nothing is paid for or licensed. Generated in code, or public domain and permissive only (21.11)

| Why raids exist
| Pages are the reason. A heist is worth two to three sessions of the Library's trickle, and pages lean toward the school raided — primary, not exclusive, and never gated by the clock (7.6)

| Law rarity
| Four ranks, plus paradoxes. Rank is a strength ladder paid for in pages; stability cost is the balance dial. The scout report shows every rank alike, so visibility pays for nothing (7.9)

| Defence
| Four layers — geometry, detection and response, wards, laws — each inside a power budget, so no layer decides outcomes on its own. Laws are no longer "the main defence" (13)

| Fair defence
| 55 to 70% of raids succeed, 60 to 75% at realm level 1; 60% of an authored defence's strength comes from design rather than level; the shape of outcomes matters more than the average (13.2)

| Four names changed
| Echo Knight became Echoplate, Moss Giant became Mossbear, Masquerade became Borrowed Face, and the Creator Fund became the Island Fund (26.9)

| The game's name
| Ruleborn is kept, but clearance is a gate before any public use: the registers, both stores, domains and handles, including "-bound" marks in the software and entertainment classes. Fallbacks, if clearance fails, are Lawkeep and Isleborn — the name in use is never its own fallback (26.9)

| Fonts
| Two open-licensed typefaces, embedded lawfully, with the licence text shipped in settings (21.11)

| What a raid pays
| Minted rather than transferred, but keyed to the target — its Vault level in B(V) and its exposed wealth in richness, graded by outcome, decaying through the day, and capped daily and weekly. The base growth rate is the master inflation dial (9.6)

| Defence budgets
| Slot limits per layer per realm level, priced against stability. Limits, not cost, are what stop one layer dominating (13.4)

| Measuring a layer
| Switch it off, re-run at least a thousand raids, measure the haul denied — across every combination of layers, with the standard error beside each share. The targets in 13.2 become a test that passes or fails each build (13.4)

| Offline
| Heists play with no connection and submit later. Rewards shown at once as confirming, minted only after validation, never taken back (24.12)

| Clocks
| The server clock is the only clock (24.12)

| Blocking
| Hides a person, never removes their island from the pool (17.8)

| One answer per system
| Where the document specified something twice, the older half is deleted rather than left standing: detection (12.17), payout (9.6), budgets (13.4), roster (11.11), rarity (7.9), production (5.8)

| Validation without players
| No paper tests, no recruited panels. A simulation harness answers everything structural and gates phases 2 and 3, the predictability proxy included; the developer is the first player; the browser prototype takes only inbound, consenting play; and enjoyment and week-one retention wait, ungated, for soft launch (19.27, 25.4)

| Nobody has to build
| Every player has a generated defence from the first minute; curation is the default creative act and authoring is the deep end. The riskiest assumption is removed rather than measured (13.3)

| Four ranks, re-derived
| 18 common, 12 uncommon, 12 rare, 6 deep at 10/20/35/60 pages — 1,200 for the full Codex. In each school the deep law with the lower stability cost became rare (7.9, 14.4)

| Producers
| One of each kind per realm; partial staffing pays pro-rata; a crew on a heist does not work (5.8)

| Guardian Posts
| One ruleborn each — the conversion that reconciles every defence table (13.4)

| The crew never decides
| No auto-attack, no auto-flee, no re-routing. A creature cannot look stupid for a decision it never made (12.18)

| Detection
| Deterministic and predictable: three vision zones, a suspicion meter that fills in half a second up close and empties slowly, six guard states, and noise that travels along paths. No dice anywhere (12.17)

| If nobody builds
| Nothing waits on it: every player already has a generated defence, and curation is the default (13.3). A shortfall in players authoring their own defence moves only the defence reward (13.1)

| Before any code
| Two gates, neither needing anyone else: determinism across devices, and the simulation harness (19.27, 26.7)

| Rules versioning
| Pin, do not patch. Islands and replays record the rules they were made under; a re-tuned law puts an over-budget island into grace rather than breaking it (24.11)

| Simulation code
| Written once in Rust with no engine types, compiled into the app and to WebAssembly for the server. Fixed point throughout, no floating point in the simulation (24.7)

| Validation policy
| Sampled, not universal: every high-value, league-affecting or disputed heist, plus a random share (24)

| Store compliance
| The simulation is linked into the signed binary at build time. No runtime code loading of any kind (24.7)

| Effort unit
| Claude Code runs, not hours. About 320 to build and a floor of about 320 to iterate, so the plan is about 640; rescaled after ten runs, never below the floor (26.8)

| Backend
| Fully serverless: no virtual machine, no operating system, nothing to patch or log into. Zero-egress storage, edge functions, a managed database (24.1, 24.6)

| Determinism
| Fixed-point maths, proved bit-identical across devices before anything else is built (24.2)

| Workshop content
| Structured data only — no player-uploaded images, ever (20.10)

| Analytics
| First-party only, regenerated from replays; no advertising identifiers, no third-party trackers (24.3)

| Matchmaking target
| The median heist is winnable, not an even match; ratings stay internal (12.15)

| Defence participation
| A 60-second guided build before the first player heist; seeded islands; defenders paid best (13.1)

| Chat by age
| Everyone under 18 starts on preset chat; 16 and 17 may opt into filtered free chat (20.2, 20.9)

| Currency
| Every cosmetic priced in real money; Starglass only as a visible 1:1 wrapper (19.12)

| Touch targets
| 9 mm or more for primary controls, never in a corner (21.8)

| Co-op
| Asynchronous and turn-based; no real-time version (12.11)

| Estimation
| The reference class is empty, so the bottom-up estimate is a hypothesis with iteration at 1× the build as a floor; no AI multiplier; no sustained crunch (26.5, 26.8)

| Notes
| Frozen at eight; laws are the expansion axis (24)

| Concept pitch
| Anchored differentiation: familiar in five seconds, novelty after the hook (23.4)

| Screen orientation
| Portrait by default on phones, landscape available everywhere and remembered per device, with the same information in both (23.3)

| Theft
| One-way by default: raiders gain, defenders lose nothing. An opt-in Open Vault league carries real stakes (27.5)

| Onboarding
| Guided across the first three sessions, then faded, rather than two steps and done (27.5)

| Casual pacing
| Settled: a visible, openly labelled catch-up discount on levels the median player passed long ago (27.5)

| Launch scope
| 24 laws chosen by engine cost, expensive cut first; 12 signature resonances; wards at two levels; English and Brazilian Portuguese; constellations in version 1.1. The Island Workshop is not cuttable (26.1)

27.3 Decisions the research settled

Every item that stood here is decided

All eight items that stood here were decided by the research programme against rules fixed in advance, and one more row settles a contradiction that was not on the list (32). They are recorded as settled rather than returned as choices. Nothing in this section is open.

| Was open
| Now

| Casual pacing. At one session a day, finishing everything took about 1,019 days — a figure the partial re-run then moved about a third later, to day 1,343, when the production rules changed underneath it (14.2).
| The curve is cut, and a catch-up discount ships as well. The discount is keyed to the median player's progress rather than to calendar time or to the player's own rate, openly labelled, applied to levels below the current median Keeper level. Calendar keying punishes a late start and rewards absence; individual-rate keying rewards slow play. No published figure gives a size, so it ships at 50% off, the cost-halving equal of FFXIV's +100% Armoury Bonus, until soft-launch cohort data move it by rule: if new players' 30-day retention in the lowest band falls more than a third below veterans' historical figure, it deepens by 10 points a season until it does not; if veterans' session frequency falls after it ships, it narrows by 10 points a season until it recovers. FFXIV's Armoury Bonus is the cleanest shipped model but keys to the player's own ceiling, which fits alting rather than a cross-player gap.

| Which countries for the soft launch.
| Chosen by which question each market answers, never by resemblance to the global market. One cheap high-volume market for technical stability, one market that behaves like the target audience for retention, and a tier-one English-language market for monetisation. The United States, Japan, South Korea and China are held back for global launch, because install costs there are high and spending budget on them teaches little.

| Whether the Island Fund survives its own test.
| It ships with its existing test and a new default of continuing. The test itself is already specified and unchanged: output and PXI-meaning recorded for the top 200 creators before the fund launches, re-measured on the same people six months later (18.9 owns it). What the research adds is the part that was missing — no comparable programme publishes its continue-or-stop criteria, so the review gets a stated date and a stated default of continuing, because a review with no default becomes a silent cancellation.

| Final names, and the trademark check.
| Clearance runs first, and the position on registration changed. The free half — the EU and US registers, both stores, domains, handles, a plain search — costs nothing and gates public use. The standing position was to register nothing unless the evidence demanded it; for the EU the evidence demands it, because the Union grants no unregistered trade mark protection and is first to file, so a later filer can block the name. One EUTM in class 9 and class 41 is about €900 in fees. In the US, first-to-use means the unregistered position holds with dated evidence of first use. An EU trade-mark attorney is named as a dependency.

| How many languages at launch — two sections disagreed, one saying English and Portuguese and one saying twelve.
| English and Brazilian Portuguese, with localised store listings as the demand test for anything further. Twelve at launch has no precedent even in the wrong reference class: Clash Royale shipped in English only and added fourteen languages later. The translation owner is named in the team table: the developer (26.3).

| Whether performative and emotional challenge (19.4) are worth strengthening.
| Left alone, and closed. CORGIS (Denisova et al., 2020) validates four distinct kinds of challenge and ties cognitive challenge to strategy games; nothing shows that adding emotional challenge raises enjoyment for a strategy audience, and nothing names a change that would. The soft-launch gate keeps reading cognitive and decision-making challenge above the midpoint and performative below it (19.4, 19.5).

| “Universe” or a softer word such as “your sky”.
| “Universe” stays, and joins the comprehension items. The concreteness effect is real for memory (Paivio; Brysbaert et al., 2014), but no study shows a grand label harming comprehension of a core object, tests a metaphor against it, or tests the Portuguese pair. The word lives in one string table; if fewer than 70% of soft-launch players can define it after the tutorial, it is swapped for “your sky” in that table (15.6, 25.3).

| Law Clash rules, ahead of version 1.2.
| Specified now (18.10). An asynchronous duel under identical conditions — same island, same seed, deterministic re-simulation, simultaneous submission — scored by the haul formula, rated with Glicko-2 apart from Renown, with nothing lost by the loser. One duel, not a series: the evidence does not require one.

| Whether constellations should later get a competitive mode.
| No: the seasonal league is the whole of constellation competition (17.5). Competition drives engagement and out-group hostility alike (Kwak et al., CHI 2015), toxicity harms retention (Shores et al., CSCW 2014; Beres et al., CHI 2021), and nothing shows that opt-in competition over a shared environment avoids it. No anomaly race ships.

| Feedback
| Addressed in

| The pitch was too technical

| The Great Code was too technical

| Time to reach every level of everything

| Defenders choosing arrival points felt wrong
| 5.4

| Revisit stability; three states were too few
| 5.5

| 15

| Connect resources, buildings, laws and characters; currencies, costs and production

| Look and feel in depth
