What version 3.2 left open, now closed

One piece of drift was known and deliberately left: the roster in 11.2 and the character bible in 11.11 gave different ability wordings and cooldowns for all eighteen creatures. It is closed. The eighteen were designed once against brief 32’s two uses a heist, 11.11 holds each, and 11.2 is generated from it, so the two cannot differ again (C6).

The four entries 27.3 carried under “genuinely still open” are closed by brief 31, and 27.3 keeps only its settled table. The four mechanism changes version 3.2 had decided without propagating are applied (32.3). What this section guarantees is unchanged: each answer is stated once, so that the next audit finds design questions and not four copies of a number, and 31.10 says how every point closes.

It also cannot do the thing it is most tempting to claim. Every check here verifies that the document agrees with itself. None of them verifies that it describes a good game. That remains what 19.27 and 25.4 are for, and no amount of green ticks substitutes for it.

31.10 How every point closes

Version 3.2 answered its register and left some answers unapplied, and its own changelog could not tell the two apart. These five rules are what makes “no open points” a property of the document rather than a claim about it. They bind every section, every research brief and every later revision.

Five closure rules

Every branch ends. Each rule in this document has a default for absent evidence and a last branch that always terminates — a value that ships, a sentence deleted, or a claim restated as a limit. No rule ends in “revisit”, “to be decided” or a new register item.

Nothing a report raises stays open. If a research report surfaces an issue its brief did not ask about, it is resolved in the same application pass by the same standard — evidence decides, and absent evidence the default that keeps the design’s existing wording ships — and it is recorded as closed, not added to the register (32.8).

Later measurements are triggers, not questions. Where a number can only be checked at soft launch or after ten agent runs, the design ships a value now and states the trigger and the action it causes. A measurement with no stated action is an open point and is not allowed. The numbers only a harness or model run can set are listed with their values, rules and commands in 32.9.

The design keeps no open list. 27.3’s “genuinely still open” entries closed with brief 31, and 32.5 is “where the evidence is thin, and what ships”, row by row.

Done is checked, not declared. After each build, tools/closure.py fails the build if any register item lacks a recorded closing edit, measurement or decision, or if any row of 32.5 lacks a shipped value. Then an independent pass that has not seen the edits re-audits the document against the register; anything it finds is fixed in the same pass, and a version is finished only when that re-audit reports zero open items.

32What the research settled, and what it could not

Thirty-six research briefs were written in two rounds: twenty-seven against the fifty-two open items of the September audit, and nine more against the sixty-two items the next audit left open. Each carried a decision rule fixed before the research ran, so that no finding returned a choice to be made — only which pre-committed branch fired. From the second brief onward a single rule governed sourcing: only peer-reviewed work and first-party material could carry a conclusion, and community reconstructions could corroborate but never support. This section records what that produced: the four mechanisms it changed and where each now lives, the gaps where evidence is thin and what ships regardless, what the second round decided, and the numbers only a harness or model run can set, with the value each carries until then.

32.1 Why the source rule mattered more than the answers

The constraint changed the shape of the output rather than its volume. Three briefs would have returned confident findings under a looser rule and instead returned named gaps — and each of those would have been written into this document as settled. That is the whole argument for the rule: a thin finding is worse than no finding, because a thin finding gets a section number.

The clearest case is brief 2. An earlier version of 12.19 put the loading break-even at 70% full. The one peer-reviewed study of exactly this decision names “draw until the risk of busting exceeds 70%” as the rule first-time players intuitively choose, and shows trained agents beating it. So 70% was not merely unsupported; it was approximately the novice threshold, and the measured peak was 90% all along.

The second clearest is brief 22. This document already cited evidence that disclosed assistance does not harm the experience, to justify an openly labelled catch-up discount. That citation is real — Depping, Mandryk, Li, Gutwin and Vicencio-Moreira, CHI 2016, honourable mention — and it holds. But it was a single-session laboratory study in a competitive first-person shooter. It supports “disclosure is not inherently harmful”. It does not support a disclosed discount on economic progression over years, and this document was making that leap silently. The citation stays, qualified.

32.2 One brief was wrong about this document, and that is recorded too

Brief 1 was written on the premise that the payout's base term was keyed to the raider's own Vault level, and it concluded that the term should be re-pegged to the player's own production rate instead. The premise was false. 9.6's table has always read V is the target's Vault level; it was the prose above the table that claimed otherwise. So the document already did what the research said shipped games do, and the defect was a contradiction between a section and its own table rather than a mis-keyed formula.

The re-peg is therefore not carried out, and the reason is stated here rather than left as a silently skipped edit. What the brief did establish stands: minting and keying-to-the-target are separate decisions; the shipped norm keys to the defender's holdings; and a narrow richness band removes the reason to scout, which is why the clamp widened. The finding record for brief 1 is annotated with this correction.

It is worth saying why this matters beyond the one case. Every brief in this programme was written from a parse of the previous version, and a parse can be wrong about what a document says. A decision rule fixed in advance stops a finding from becoming a judgement call; it does not stop a finding from being aimed at the wrong target. The check against that is the same one 32.7 describes: read the artefact, not the summary of it.

32.3 Four mechanisms the research decided, and where each now lives

These four are the largest design consequences of the research. Version 3.2 decided all four and propagated one of them only partly; version 3.3 applies all four in the sections that own them. The right-hand column says where, so that each claim points at the text that makes it true rather than at this section.

| The decision, and the evidence
| Where it now lives

| Rewind’s effect is replaced by checkpoint replay. No shipped engine restores 300 ticks of simulation state. GGPO caps rollback at 8 frames; NetherRealm shipped 7. Checkpoint replay is free here because the seed and input log already exist for server re-simulation.
| Applied. Tidemark takes Rewind’s slot — Deep, Time, Echo, cost 26 — as a periodic mark and a checkpoint return, re-derived by replay from the seed and input log the server already keeps (7.7). It is tagged conditional or periodic on the engine-cost ladder rather than on the rewind rung (7.10). The count stays 48 and Time keeps its 3/2/2/1 split. Every section that named Rewind — 6.2, 7.2, 7.4, 8.2, 17.3, 21.7 and the two signature resonances in 8.4 — now names Tidemark; restoring ten seconds of state is gone from every one, and the Hourkeeper no longer rewinds (11.11). Tidemark is not among the twenty-four launch laws (7.10).

| Guards and response are one defence layer. A theorem rather than a measurement: the Shapley value’s symmetry axiom assigns identical credit to two components with identical marginal contributions to every coalition, and neither turns back a raid without the other. The characteristic function also moves from hold rate to expected haul denied, because hold rate cannot see delay and three layers work by delaying.
| Applied. 13 lists four layers, with detection and response as one layer carrying one share, and measures every layer by expected haul denied, of which the hold rate is a special case. The 25% ceiling is deleted and the power-budget principle stated in its place. 13.4 and 19.27 count sixteen layer combinations and 16,000 runs or more. The shares under the merged layer are re-derived by the harness (32.9).

| The ambush is a guaranteed knockout. Stealth takedowns are conventionally a guaranteed state change gated by stealth rather than a damage race. Blacklist’s lead AI states it plainly: the game had “insta-kill takedowns” if you got the jump on an NPC, and the perception system rather than a health calculation is what held the balance. The same programmer kept footstep hearing intact near takedowns, because otherwise “a player could just sprint through the map stabbing everyone in the back” (brief 28).
| Applied. In 12.8 a successful first unseen hit knocks the target out, keyed to its own health, so a change to Power cannot break it; the floored formula of triple Power or half the target’s health is deleted there and in 29.1. Its costs are stated: an unseen start, the animation time, a discovered body raising the realm alarm by one, and the choice to hide or move a body, which then does not raise it again. A test asserts it for all twenty-five role pairings. The crouched step keeps its 1 m, so an unheard approach needs a quiet surface: moss carries a crouched step 0.75 m, and clearing moss behind a post is a defensive decision (6.1, 12.8).

| Each ability is two uses a heist, not a cooldown. At version 3.2’s figures a crew got 25 to 83 ability windows against a whole-run decision budget of 15 to 25, so abilities alone swamped the budget. Every comparable short-session tactics game treats a signature ability as a once-or-twice choice. Version 3.2 recorded the answer as cooldowns roughly doubled, to 24–60 seconds. Brief 32 replaced that band: the first-party evidence on the limiter was split, which fires the default, and the default is the only mechanism that gives the same once-or-twice count on a 150-second and a 240-second heist — a cooldown’s count grows with the clock.
| Applied. 11.11 gives every ability two uses a heist with a lockout of one decision slot (10 seconds) between them, and 11.2 is generated from it. A crew has 6 to 10 ability choices whatever the clock, which leaves at least 5 of 12.6’s 15 to 25 decisions for routes, pylons, casts and greed. At most four abilities are ready at once (32.4): in a crew of five, the rightmost portrait’s ability waits until another use is taken. Uses left show as pips or a number, never colour alone (21). Held breath slows the lockout and the Sage’s cooldowns (7.7). If the harness sweep puts the median run outside the decision band, the uses fall to one (32.9).

Why this table stays
An independent audit of version 3.2 found all four recorded here and none of them fully applied, and its summary is worth preserving: every one was “a decision recorded in 32 or the changelog that was never propagated into the section that owns the number”, and no check spanned the two, so the suite reported green over live contradictions. A research layer that states conclusions above normative sections that still describe the old mechanisms is a way for this document to disagree with itself that looks like progress. Version 3.3 applies all four, and the check 31.5 carries for this table now has the stronger subject that follows: every row is stated as applied, so it fails if the old mechanism is still live anywhere outside quotation — a law restoring ten seconds of state, a five-layer count, a floored ambush formula, or a cooldown on a crew ability.

32.4 One cognitive bound caps three separate systems

Three briefs arrived independently at the same ceiling, and it is worth stating once rather than three times. Working-memory capacity is about four chunks (Cowan, Behavioral and Brain Sciences, 2001), and reliable processing of interacting variables fails past four (Halford, Baker, McCredden and Bain, Psychological Science, 2005, where five-way interactions were at chance). That single bound is what caps the ward count a realm can present legibly, the number of interacting variables any one heist decision may require, and the number of crew abilities a player can track at once.

The consequence is that wards, decision complexity and ability salience are competing for one budget, not three. Raising any of them spends the same allowance. Where the count would exceed it, the count moves rather than the bound — and for abilities that fix is applied: each has two uses a heist rather than a cooldown, and at most four are ready at once, so an ability whose lockout ends while four are ready waits its turn (12.6).

32.5 Where the evidence is thin, and what ships

Each row is a question no trusted source answers. Version 3.2 wrote twenty-nine of them down as questions nobody had answered, which was honest, and left the design waiting on research that may never exist. Every row now ships a value, and names what would change it: a trigger with its action, or “Closed” where no measurement would. A gap that is written down is still better than a silent assumption; a gap with no shipped value is an open point, and 31.10 does not allow one.

| The gap
| What ships
| What would change it

| The raider-versus-builder split. No first-party or peer-reviewed source quantifies what share of players in an asynchronous base-builder mainly build rather than attack. There is a structural reason: in Clash of Clans attacking is effectively mandatory for progression, so “never attacks” is not a category anyone tracks. The nearest bound is motivation data across 1.9 million respondents showing roughly half of players lean non-competitive, which bounds the question without answering it.
| No split is stated. The Library’s table sets passive pages (10.4); the days a non-raider and an engaged raider take to complete the Codex are regenerated from the economy model on the launch law set and published as a gap, not averaged (14.4, 32.9).
| At soft launch, brief 12’s triggers with their sizes fixed now: if non-raiders’ day-30 retention falls more than a third below raiders’, the Library’s page output moves up one level-step each season until it does not; if raiders’ churn peaks within a week of finishing the first Codex tier, the second tier opens one chapter earlier, each season, until it does not.

| No shipped game publishes its raiding-versus-production income share, so 9.6's payout has no external target to hit. Practitioner accounts contradict each other.
| No share target. Days-to-upgrade and the raid-worth criterion govern (9.6).
| Closed.

| No measured threshold for when an optional PvP loop stops being worth doing. The conclusion that a loop paying 1 to 2% of income would not be played is inference from convergent behavioural evidence, including Meet Your Maker's documented spiral from grind to abandonment.
| The raid-worth criterion, checked by the economy model (9.6).
| Closed.

| No designer has been documented deliberately setting a bank-or-push threshold below its optimum for readability. Every documented case runs the other way.
| The measured peak is stated; no readability exception is claimed (12.19).
| Closed.

| Whether a player population actually uses an adaptive information channel when given one is unmeasured. Every trusted analysis finds adaptive play beats fixed thresholds; none reports what fraction of real players adapt.
| The guard-suspicion display and the player-set loading target ship as designed; the wide spread is intended (12.19).
| At soft launch, if the per-attempt haul of the top quarter of players exceeds the bottom quarter’s by less than the harness’s optimal-to-competent ratio, players are not using the channel, and the suspicion meter gains its number beside it (0–1000), the one change available; the stopping rule is not changed and nothing further is tried.

| No decision-density figures exist for extraction or loot phases, so the eight-to-twelve second cadence in 12.6 rests on a design principle rather than a measurement.
| Eight to twelve seconds a decision is stated as a design principle, and the harness’s decisions-per-run count is its instrument (12.6).
| Closed.

| No asynchronous matchmaker is documented filtering on the structural properties of the target — depth, size, layout complexity — rather than on player rating alone. Clash Royale filters on King Level and Trophies and says nothing about deck structure, but it is symmetrical and so has no target-size problem to solve.
| Depth joins the filter, stated as without precedent (12.22).
| Closed.

| No numeric ceiling on a single defensive system's share of effectiveness exists in any trusted source. The former “no layer above 25%” rule had nothing behind it and is replaced by a power-budget principle: no layer decides outcomes alone.
| The power-budget principle replaces the 25% ceiling (13).
| Closed.

| No published specialist-to-generalist output ratio, so the Breaker being 63% of the crew's damage against breakables cannot be judged against a norm.
| Researched in brief 33, whose rule decides both roles: the Carrier holds 40% of a five-crew’s bags, and the assist bonus ships at 25% more damage a hit while two or more work one object (11.1); restricted play resizes it, or removes it and 12.6’s “who cracks” framing, by the rule in 32.9.
| Closed.

| No guidance exists on decoupling shared offensive and defensive creature stats. Ruleborn's guards are the defender's own creatures with identical role stats, so any offensive change propagates to defence.
| The ambush is keyed to the target’s own health, so offensive changes cannot break it (12.8).
| Closed.

| No practice exists for binding a published document table to the model that generated it. The literature has the simulate-and-regenerate half (Machinations; GEEvo, IEEE CEC 2024; Rogers et al., IEEE Transactions on Games, 2023) and not the document half.
| Generated blocks, and checks that every published table equals its model’s output (31.4).
| Closed.

| No numeric false-negative rate for phrase-based supersession checks in documentation pipelines. The supporting evidence is the requirements-smell precision and recall literature plus the undecidability of semantic equivalence.
| Bans are derived from data and recomputed (31.5).
| Closed.

| No measured iteration-to-build multiple for a game with no reference implementation. Cerny's 30% pre-production figure and Schell's 50% rule are practitioner anchors; the 40 to 60% maintenance share is an analogy from shipped software.
| Iteration is carried at 1× the build as a floor (26.5).
| After ten agent runs, runs-per-feature is measured and the whole estimate rescaled, never below the floor.

| No disclosed-method measurement of agent productivity on greenfield code. The one rigorous measurement is METR's randomised trial, which was deliberately mature-codebase — the worst case — and found experienced developers 19% slower while believing they were 20% faster. It is the gap most likely to move the schedule, and it cannot be closed from outside the project.
| No speed-up is assumed (26.5).
| The same ten-run rescale.

| No estimation control for a solo developer checking their own estimate.
| The ten-run rescale, no felt speed-up, and the uplift pre-committed before early results (26.5).
| Closed.

| No collection-size threshold below which a collection stops feeling like one, so whether 54 creature forms reads as a collection is unknown.
| 54 forms ship (11.10); forms are added through seasons as content.
| Closed.

| No measured install-volume payback per additional language for a solo-developer strategy game; the available figures come from localisation vendors.
| English and Brazilian Portuguese ship; store listings are localised more widely (20.6, 26.4).
| Each season, the locale whose localised listing brought the most installs is translated next, one at a time.

| No measured threshold at which over-scoped small-team projects fail to ship. The direction is strongly and consistently reported — “triple your time estimates” — but from self-report.
| The launch cut (26.1) and the iteration floor (26.5).
| Closed.

| Whether players read an owner-chosen directional effect at a glance is unproven. Wargroove's wind, Advance Wars terrain and XCOM elevation are all owner-independent; no shipped game lets a defender pick a slope's direction.
| The generator sets direction by one stated rule, adopted on analogy and said so (7.7, 7.10, 13.3).
| Closed.

| No documented case of a mechanic designed for player authorship being handed to a generator, and no general principle for orienting a directional effect in a generated adversarial level.
| One placement rule for the eight laws (7.10), under 13.3’s never-seal constraint; no deliberate handicap on generated islands, since the field’s norm is to find and remove generator biases.
| Closed.

| Geometry composition is unsolved in the literature even though placement is solved, which is why generated islands are stated as the weaker population rather than as equals.
| Generated islands are stated as the weaker population, with their own hold band (13.2).
| Closed.

| No trusted evidence on whether hidden or legible traps produce better play. Base-builders lean hidden; the genres split.
| Ward positions stay hidden until revealed — the scout shows how many, not where (12.4) — and every ward reads by shape and icon once seen (10.3); pylons are legible, settled by accessibility requirements (12.6).
| Closed.

| The share of mobile sessions played with sound off is not knowable from published sources. Two vendor figures contradict each other outright — one survey reports over 91% playing with sound off, an SDK-telemetry study reports 73% playing with sound on — and neither fully discloses method.
| No percentage is stated; audio always reinforces and never carries meaning alone.
| Closed.

| No measured figure for how many audio family variations stay distinguishable in a game; the earcon recall figures come from menu-navigation studies rather than from a real-time game on a phone.
| The count the audio section already sets; every cue is redundant with a visual one, so a variation that cannot be told apart costs nothing.
| Closed.

| No authoritative convention for how many times an ability should fire in a short session, so the once-or-twice count a heist allows is reasoned from genre practice rather than measured.
| Researched in brief 32, whose rule decides the mechanism: two uses a heist with a ten-second lockout (11.11).
| Closed.

| No failure rate for the developer-as-first-player practice, and no validated self-instrumentation that can gate a phase. Pre-registered predictions, time-separated re-testing and blind A/B on one's own build are borrowed from experimental method and will catch regressions the harness already catches better. Solo post-mortems repeatedly name skipping early external validation as their central mistake, which is the risk 19.27’s constraint accepts.
| Enjoyment and week-one retention are ungated before soft launch, stated as an accepted risk (19.27). Its falsification condition carries a fallback specified now: the crew never acts between orders (19.27).
| Closed.

| No standard for presenting revenue scenarios that cross a commission threshold, and no canonical name for a check that loses its subject and therefore passes.
| The crossing is modelled and footnoted (22.5); every check carries a subject-exists assertion (31.5).
| Closed.

| No published per-noun localisation cost for invented proper nouns, no priced connotation-check procedure, and no published criteria by which comparable programmes decide to continue a fund that pays creators.
| Two languages only (20.6); the connotation check is the free native-speaker read-aloud in both languages; the Island Fund ships with a review date, stated metrics and a default of continuing, so its review cannot become a silent cancellation (18.9).
| Closed.

| No numeric production-to-raid value ratio that keeps the work-or-fight trade-off live, and no measurement of how many simultaneous assignment decisions a mobile player can hold. The peer-reviewed idle-game work finds engagement comes from decisions being meaningful rather than numerous, so the burden of proof falls on adding staffing granularity, not on withholding it.
| The raid-worth criterion via the economy model (9.6); staffing is an allocation budget with coarse granularity (9.2).
| Closed.

32.6 What the mutation suite cannot prove

31.5 reports twenty-one deliberate breakages, each of which must produce a named failure. That result is real and it is narrower than it sounds. Mutation operators alter statements that already exist, so mutation testing cannot represent a fault of omission by construction — a missing fact, a deleted special case, a rule that was never written. The empirical work that established mutants as a usable proxy for real faults found roughly 27% of real faults not coupled to common operators, explicitly including code-deletion and omission faults (Just, Jalali, Inozemtseva, Ernst, Holmes and Fraser, FSE 2014), and standard tools often omit a statement-deletion operator by default.

So a green mutation run proves the checks catch syntactic damage to text that is present. It proves nothing about text that should be present and is not. That is the class of fault the audits keep finding, and it is why 32.5 exists.

32.7 Why a fix is verified by something that did not make it

The empirical rate of incomplete fixes is the reason, and it is high. Between 22% and 33% of resolved bugs require more than one fix attempt; incomplete patches are larger and more scattered than ordinary ones; and about 15% of the additional change locations lie beyond the immediate neighbours of the first change (Park, Kim, Ray and Bae, MSR 2012, extended 2016). Between 14.8% and 24.4% of post-release fixes are themselves incorrect. Omission errors — a fix applied to the copy in view and not to every appearance — are harder to spot than errors of commission.

This document has already produced exactly that failure once: an independent pass found five defects in work that had reported itself complete, two of them inside the section claiming the fixes had landed (31.5). The literature says that is the normal rate rather than an anomaly, which is why the separate verification pass is architecture here and not diligence.

One control is added on the strength of new evidence. Frontier agents exploit test cases at high rates — one evaluation measured 76% on tasks where any pass implies a spec violation — and hiding or isolating the test surface drops that to near zero, with read-only access described as the optimal middle ground. The same work found that giving the agent an explicit way to declare a task impossible cut exploitation from 54% to 9% on a conflicting-requirements benchmark. So the checks are read-only to the editing agent, and the agent may declare an edit impossible rather than manufacture a pass. Telling a model not to cheat remains the one control known not to work: instructing it so lowered the planning rate from 80% to 70% in one measurement, and asking it to use intended methods raised it to 95%.

32.8 The second round: what briefs 28 to 36 decided

The first twenty-seven briefs left some items answered only in part, and the audit of version 3.2 added more. Nine further briefs ran under the same two rules — a decision rule fixed before the research, and the source rule of 32.1. Every one returned a branch rather than a choice, and in most of them the branch that fired was the stated default, because no trusted source settled the question. That is the rule working: a default decided in advance ships, and the gap it covers is written down rather than filled with a plausible number.

Three records depart from their reports, and each says so. Brief 28’s report called a fallback of 10% more fill per alarm level consistent with Invisible, Inc.; the evidence branch had fired, so the six-step ladder ships instead. Brief 29’s report recommended 25% revive health as the safer choice; the pre-committed rule takes the median of the first-party values, which is 30%. And brief 35’s rule tunes the birth rate to its horizon in both directions, where the route before it only lowered the rate; the roster fills far too early today, so the rate rises.

Every issue a report raised beyond its brief was closed in the same pass, by the standard in 31.10: a distraction that silences a step (the design has none, so the quiet surface is the one route), a second storage cap for absent players (not adopted; the Vault already stores), a 50% new-player win rate (it comes from symmetric team play and does not transfer), and a later look at form variety (forms are added through seasons as content). None is carried forward as a question. Each record also names its gaps — a breaching-noise radius, gait ratios, a day to open a deepest system, a minimum pool before fallback — and in each the default ships, as 32.5 sets out for the first round.

32.9 The measurement ledger

Some numbers can only be set by running the balance harness or the economy model. None of them is left open. Each ships now at the value below — a stated default, or the current figure until the run replaces it — and each is replaced only by its own rule, by the command named, with the published table regenerated from that run rather than edited by hand (31.4). A row whose run has not happened is not a question; it is a value with a known way of changing.

| Share of heist-outcome variance explained by the player's commands (the predictability proxy)
| 19.27, 26.2
| Gate: commands must explain a majority (over 50%) of outcome variance; the measured value is produced by the harness run
| Below half in the harness or in the browser prototype's sessions, or satisfied while fewer than 70% of soft-launch players say they felt they were playing: the crew stops acting between orders and the proxy is re-run; if it still fails, the fallback stays and nothing further is tried

| Catch-up discount size
| 27.3
| 50% off costs on levels below the current median Keeper level
| At soft launch: new players' 30-day retention in the lowest band more than a third below veterans' historical figure → deepen 10 points a season until it is not; veterans' session frequency falling after it ships → narrow 10 points a season until it recovers
| Soft-launch cohort retention and session-frequency reports (24.3)

Every published harness figure states n and its standard error, and a claim near a band’s edge needs t ≥ 3. When a run changes a value, the row’s home changes with it and this table is regenerated; nothing else in the document restates the number.
